## [Can an AI command line replace the GUI as the primary user experience for computers assuming the technology improves and irrespective of today s state](https://www.quora.com/Can-an-AI-command-line-replace-the-GUI-as-the-primary-user-experience-for-computers-assuming-the-technology-improves-and-irrespective-of-today-s-state/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=511422617&comment_type=2)

*2026-05-27*

A lot of what makes an expert is lots more *internal* memory of knowledge and skills — so their “System 1” — in Kahneman’s term — can be used in tandem with “System 2”. Non-experts have only the slow cognitive “System 2” to use because “Systems 1” is somewhat like an ML “AI” and requires quite a bit of training before it can be used.

In UI design, the main issues with beginners are that most things are still *external* (thus you have to supply what will later be *internal*), and that humans can only deal with a limited number of things at once before being saturated (let’s say fewer than 7±2, and say fewer than 4 ideas at a time). One of the skills that needs to be scaffolded is “control of focus”, etc.

In addition, people will forget some expertise when not doing for a while, so they will need more help. This implies that it would be good for a system to remember the latencies between visits and act accordingly.

A poor help system thinks it knows what the end-user needs (this is rarely the case). It should at least provide a list of alternatives of what it thinks the user needs to know (this is probably best done graphically (it would be very tedious to have Siri verbally conveying these questions, etc.).

All in all, today’s UIs are generally appalling — especially given the resources available— and this reveals a deadly combination of lack of knowledge and lack of investment interest.

---

## [Can an AI command line replace the GUI as the primary user experience for computers assuming the technology improves and irrespective of today s state](https://www.quora.com/Can-an-AI-command-line-replace-the-GUI-as-the-primary-user-experience-for-computers-assuming-the-technology-improves-and-irrespective-of-today-s-state/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=511380861&comment_type=2)

*2026-05-26*

It might help to concentrate on your “fellow travelers” that you named: “business, history and technology” — If I had the urge to rant, I would certainly pick on the general world view of “business” rather than “science”.

A further perspective that might help is to recognize the implications that our brain/mind is only receiving from the “outside” fluctuations of photons, air pressure, etc.

This means that everything we think is “out there” is a construction of our own mind: we actually live in a waking dream: we are both illusional and delusional.

Among other things, this means that *we* are actually creating *both* sides of every conversation (and it not only is hard to get straight our POV, it is really difficult to get what the other person is trying say clear enough to even have a “good” argument). In the end we are always dealing with our beliefs.

Science understands this (but many scientists haven’t really faced up to this — and most people aren’t even aware of the idea and are shocked when this is pointed out).

In short: we are all “crazy” and we all need help. I don’t think that “thinking smaller” is going to help, but “thinking wider” will (and it’s adding science to regular human thought that will help the most).

In a famous lecture about “The Two Cultures” C.P.Snow, who was both a PhD scientist and a novelist, pointed out that most scientists were much better versed in “letters” than the “Literati” in universities were versed in science. (This debate was about two cultures as found in universities, so the ideas would need to be expanded to the larger public to be a more worthy example.)

But we could look at this from a less lofty view, and note that most of the voting public is not versed in what they need to know to even argue to make progress rather than to win the argument.

One might say: it’s long past time for our species to teach itself how to grow up — but now we really must.

## [Can an AI command line replace the GUI as the primary user experience for computers assuming the technology improves and irrespective of today s state](https://www.quora.com/Can-an-AI-command-line-replace-the-GUI-as-the-primary-user-experience-for-computers-assuming-the-technology-improves-and-irrespective-of-today-s-state/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=511375168&comment_type=2)

*2026-05-26*

This is a terrific comment, and I like both the parts I agree with and those I don’t!

I think we have very different basic definitions of “science” (I take it as “finding things out”, and separate it from “technology”, “business”, “product”, etc.). Given the realities, that’s a bit of a cop-out, but still my POV.

I don’t think “science created the world of plastic pollution, of endless highways and of the television”, etc. And it really isn’t as reductive as most people seem to think (it’s really about understanding systems of most kinds, and the systems nature of things isn’t really reductive, but a way to be able to understand “parts” without getting misled by them.

Many scientists agree with Bacon’s early points about “human brains, cultures, institutions, languages” etc being “too noisy” and that we naturally don’t think clearly — and — as he said in “Novum Organum” “we need to invent new methods that will help us get around what’s instrinsically mentally weak and dangerous about us”.

However, an interesting point that can be extracted from your comment might be “if we’d only stayed cavepeople, then we wouldn’t have caused so much damage”. Probably true. But I don’t like this as a solution, and that — if we had survived at all — most those who did survive suffered greatly during their short lives.

I find it terrible that we haven’t found how to scale our societies intellectually to handle the difference between the invention of a rock to respond to a perceived insult, and to use a Tomahawk missile with a W80 warhead. Or to simply invent a knife and not be able to have the wisdom to not use it as a weapon. (Pretty much any technology that has leverage/power can be also used as a weapon …) This condemns your cavepeople to a miserable future if you don’t allow them to invent tools.

Re: science as “thinking better”, Bacon didn’t limit “the new science” as he called it, to finding out more about the physical world. He meant “we need to denoise our thinking” quite generally, and this hasn’t been done nearly enough (nor have most societies as a whole thought it a good idea to generally improve the real thinking schools of most members of the society).

I think you will need to look elsewhere than science to find the culprits (and you won’t need to look far).

Best wishes

---

## [Can an AI command line replace the GUI as the primary user experience for computers assuming the technology improves and irrespective of today s state](https://www.quora.com/Can-an-AI-command-line-replace-the-GUI-as-the-primary-user-experience-for-computers-assuming-the-technology-improves-and-irrespective-of-today-s-state/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=511336092&comment_type=2)

*2026-05-26*

Hi David,

I strongly suggest that you also put this comment to use as an answer to this question (since most of it is as much an answer as a comment).

A key phrase you used about 2/3rd down is “if used properly”. We should note that if we apply this to the ARPA/Parc technologies invented in the 60s and 70s that — for most people — this never happened, and the most important results have been negative. Where the actual intent was followed — and we must remember that Engelbart’s original proposal in 1962 emphasized that it would be *absolutely necessary to have education/training be non-optional (and be dangerous if it wasn’t)* — the technologies are not just part, but a critical part, of modern medicine, engineering, science, some of the arts, etc.

My main point in a talk I gave at the Royal Society last Oct was basically McLuhan’s: that human brain/minds will generally respond and acclimate to anything constant enough in their environments as though they’d wandered into a new culture. If those environments are shaped (by purpose or accident) to be less rich in thought and perspective, it will be inevitable that the acclimation to it will be less rich also — this is because “normal” has been “renormalized downward” (and invisibly). And vice versa.

This is why humans didn’t need the bad use of the Internet and “AI” to produce the lowered norms that resulted in e.g. the climate problem blindness and Donald Trump: TV was sufficient. Television for many reasons, including those pointed out by Neil Postman, and the downward spiral of Reality TV (people behaving badly as entertainment) produced a US which is a close model of “The Apprentice” but with more punishment than “You’re fired!”.

There’s a subset of YouTube which is absolute gold for learning some very important things — including many kinds of music. And it’s difficult to draw the line. I certainly wouldn’t want to lose the great stuff. But this is like living in a nice house next to a vast garbage dump (or the Pacific Gyre), we wind up starting to get used to the land fill, even when we are consiously fighting to deal with it not hurting us.

We’ve seen that there are enough people who are interested enough in just making money that they will not only turn ideas into a garbage dump, but will actively try to get their potential customers acclimated to it. This is really low class and anti-civilization, but that is where we are. Stupid and greedly people are taking advantage of the slowness of civil laws to cause great damage that will take a long time to clean up. (It is much easier to destroy than to rebuild.)

The big problem (I claimed) is that what actually needs to be done to handle the scaling of “applying the industrial revolution to human desires” isn’t being done, and it’s a diasaster (basically unlimited free legal drugs) that has already happened, and will be yet larger as more is allowed to happen.

I think your argument that “if properly used” is moot — and a distraction — until it is.

Best cheers

Alan

---

## [How do I lay out my software architecture before I code if the requirements change every day](https://www.quora.com/How-do-I-lay-out-my-software-architecture-before-I-code-if-the-requirements-change-every-day/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=505370714&comment_type=2)

*2026-03-18*

All those things happened, but most of them happened very early and were not a sequence of inventions. For example, arguably the first interactive higher level language was done in 1954 on Whirlwind, by Laning and Swerler. It was called George and was an interpreter. Laning later was part of the Apollo Guidance System computer effort at Draper Labs, and part of the OS was an interpreter (even though really slow, it saved space and provided features the HW couldn’t).

The way I look at the actual history is that a lot of the early computerists — and early on — realized (one way or another) that the point of a programmable computer was to create behaviors the HW didn’t supply directly, and that included computer like behaviors of any desired kind. There wasn’t really a sequence of inventions per se but lots of different things happening at different places more or less at the same time.

One advent of a stage of ideas was caused more by the Algols than by particular architectures. Again, the point of HLLs was that most things that wanted to be done with computers were not straightforward in any typical machine code back then. And there were real difficulties in producing efficient code. For example, FORTRAN wasn’t that HL but still required an enormous — at that time typically IBM brute force — effort to achieve its objectives. This opened up the motivation for “more smarts, less work”, and various kinds of intermediaries were tried — most especially, those that used some form of “reverse polish” scheme that could easily be translated into and interpreted.

By coincidence, last night I was re-reading a true classic — Randell and Russell’s “Algol 60 Implementation” from the early 60s — and these guys also had a feel for context and history and so provided in the first part of the book a invaluable summary of many of the implementation efforts for both of the Algols.

Their approach was exemplary — basically for English Electric and the British Atomic Energy department — they dealt with two of the main at odds requirements: (a) jobs needed optimized code, but (b) this used up too much time of all kinds for the extensive debugging that was needed. Their solution was to do two semantically identical Algol compilers. The first was “one-pass” and interpretive for debugging and short jobs. The second was “multipass” and did quite a bit of work to optimize machine code. Both had as prior art the Algol compiler done by Dijkstra (which was a classic in “thinking things through” and had been done very quickly compared to all others).

Another way to look at this is not fall into the “Evolution trap” which is to falsely think that “evolution means optimisation” and therefore what we have today must be the best methods and thinking, etc. Actually, evolution will only “find some fits”, and with regard to computing, what you actually get will more the result of how smart and knowledgeable computing people of each era were. My view of the real pioneers is that they were pound for pound much smarter and more capable and more inventive, etc., than most computer people today. So if you look into the details of the past, you will often be amazed by just how well these folks could and did “think things through”.

---

## [Did Alan Kay know Bill Atkinson Was there any cross fertilization between SmallTalk and HyperCard Talk](https://www.quora.com/Did-Alan-Kay-know-Bill-Atkinson-Was-there-any-cross-fertilization-between-SmallTalk-and-HyperCard-Talk/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=505104162&comment_type=2)

*2026-03-15*

Hi Mark — this is a very good example of “an excellent question that requires context to be supplied in order to ask” — for which Quora doesn’t supply a form. It should probably be separate from this one, but I’ll try to do something as short as possible that is still a reasonable answer.

I thought HyperCard was a really important contribution to what we thought in ARPA and Parc was a critical part of the real value of “personal computing”, namely workable end-user programming. We got some of the elements of this in the early Smalltalk’s use of live GUIs and interactive programming. We wanted this to work with children, not just pragmatically, but epistemologically. We were also paying attention to what we thought would happen via Moore’s Law (this was the basic premise of the Parc “time machine” strategy).

Years later at Apple, the simpler goals and the sensibilities of the HyperCard project produced real breakthroughs along some of the dimensions of this large set of goals — partly by not attending to some of the things that needed to be attended to — like scaling, “what should programs really be like”, etc. This is why I said I would have ruined this project if I’d stuck my nose in — they didn’t produce an overall answer, but they did produce something that was very successful for its time (more successful for end-users than Smalltalk), and that provided much food for thought about next generations.

HyperCard was a huge influence on Etoys — not so much in architecture — but it forced thinking about *simplicity* in new directions that could still incorporated modularity and scaling — for example, it led to a very different conception of object, composition, system, etc., from that of Smalltalk (and this conception worked very well comparitively). It’s also worth noting that Smalltalk had the flexibility and power to manifest Etoys, where HyperCard would have been intractable …

The will of the wisp that drove our original research at Parc (and still does today) was the believe that adults — to be reasonable participants in a democracy today — need to be grounded as children in “science/systems”. We were never trying to teach programming (Seymour wasn’t either, his will of the wisp was math), but we were trying to invent programming environments that everyone could use. Smalltalk invented a few facets, and — as I’ve said — Hypercard invented others that were hard for us to see, but were critical …

And there are more that are yet to be found and understood.

---

## [Did Alan Kay know Bill Atkinson Was there any cross fertilization between SmallTalk and HyperCard Talk](https://www.quora.com/Did-Alan-Kay-know-Bill-Atkinson-Was-there-any-cross-fertilization-between-SmallTalk-and-HyperCard-Talk/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=505056398&comment_type=2)

## [What role did the unique features of the Alto computer play in the development of programming languages like Smalltalk and Lisp](https://www.quora.com/What-role-did-the-unique-features-of-the-Alto-computer-play-in-the-development-of-programming-languages-like-Smalltalk-and-Lisp/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=505019476&comment_type=2)

*2026-03-14*

Not to get too deep in the weeds here, but a few of these misunderstandings can be cleaned up by

(a) noticing the vintage of the Alto I gave YP that Ken Sherriff (a truly amazing guy) restored in your reference [1] (it is almost 10 years later in the Alto run and had many extensions), and

(b) reading more carefully the notes and comments at the end of his article.

The original purpose of the Alto was to make at least 100 *maximal* “bang for the buck” super personalcomputers (or “personal supercomputers”) with *minimal *HW which could act as a time machine so that SW of 10–15 years in the future could be worked on in the early 70s. The “reasonable ratio” between RAM (the first VLSI RAM Intel 1103s) and discrete more expensive “faster RAM” was about a factor of 5, which suggested that a “loadable microcode” scheme would allow an “emulation architecture” to be a practical tradeoff. Another result was that many functions — such as the display logic and I/O controllers — could be mostly in microcode and not require actual HW, etc.

The first Alto took Chuck Thacker and company about 3 months and a few weeks to design, build, and debug. (I will not go further into this magical process). But I should mention that our strategy had included simulating ahead of time many of the technologies and schemes used on the Alto. These included the bit-map display, half-tone painting, high quality fonts, animation, real-time music synthesis, various kinds of virtual machine emulators, etc. We liked NOVA 800s for these simulations, and one of the early decisions was that one of the first architectures it would be good to be able to emulate simply was that of the NOVA in order that useful parts of the simulations could be carried over.

Also, in the summer of ’72, a few of us — including Butler Lampson, Peter Deutsch, and me — put in quite a bit of time thinking about and working out “the meta” of the kinds of languages each of us was envisioning. The virtual machines would have to be different, but could there be a “family resemblance” that Chuck could take advantage of when the personal supercomputer project started?

The basic scheme for the Alto was to have 512 32-bit microinstructions in a ROM, which would include most of the hardware functions and a simple NOVA emulation, *plus* a 1024 32-bit microinstruction fast dynamically reloadable RAM for various emulations and experiments where fast new HW would normally have to be developed.

Microcode schemes are usually very tricky and as clever as possible, and are not for the faint of heart. Another story which I won’t relate here is that the Alto was both very successful and our larger plan (to do a new research computer every few years) was stymied by Xerox. So the Alto wound up being used pretty much everything — including acting as routers for our networks, servers for printers and file storage, etc. — eventually about 2000 were built, and the basic machine was “extended” beyond what should have been actually done, via many modes and kludges. A simple example is that the dispatch address in a microcode instruction was only 10 bits, and that assumed that there would only be 1K instructions. When this got extended “bank modes” had to be used etc.

The bottom line for your comment is that (a) there was only one VHLL using microcode for many years — Smalltalk — and it used everything it could — but (b) “everything” for quite a few years was just 1K RAM loadable microinstructions for some of the kernel, (c) Smalltalk’s inner kernel was actually smaller than most if not all of the other language kernels when they came along, and (d) the “time machine” development platform was a moving target, in its later years, the Alto was a workhorse, but most later development was done on the Dorado, the NoteTaker, the “Dande-” machines, etc. All using virtual machines, but not built with Alto microcode.

Best cheers

---

## [What role did the unique features of the Alto computer play in the development of programming languages like Smalltalk and Lisp](https://www.quora.com/What-role-did-the-unique-features-of-the-Alto-computer-play-in-the-development-of-programming-languages-like-Smalltalk-and-Lisp/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=504977182&comment_type=2)

*2026-03-13*

The 1973 Alto had 128K bytes of 1103 RAM (half of which was used for the 808x606 bit-map display). The kernel engine had 1024 instructions of 32-bit wide loadable microcode, indexed by 16 zero-overhead tasking program counters. There were one or two Model 30 disk pack file storage drives.

There were no restrictions on what you could do with this, and many different things were done. You could run multiple VHLLs if you wanted and programmed in different ways — you did not have to reboot the machine to change microcode, etc.

What actually happened most of the time was dependent on the limited resources. The general screen painting primitive (BitBlt) invented by Dan Ingalls was marvelously compact, but it still took about 300 microcode instructions for its kernel. The kernel of the Smalltalk virtual machine was also about 300 instructions.

INTERLISP didn’t fit and was originally run on the MAXC mainframe (also microcoded). Eventually, Peter Deutsch made a Lisp that could do a few useful things that ran on the Alto, and later on the much larger faster Dorado.

In practical terms, the kernel of most of the VHLLs we did at Parc pretty much consumed the tiny microcode memory, but that was the only barrier to running multiple micro-kernals in parallel.

Overall, this achitectural approach was extremely successful.

---

## [How much did Daniel Bobrow and Alan Kay interact at PARC in the days that led to the invention of modern Object Oriented Programming Is Bobrow and Winograds An Overview of KRL 1976 the first publication using OO in](https://www.quora.com/How-much-did-Daniel-Bobrow-and-Alan-Kay-interact-at-PARC-in-the-days-that-led-to-the-invention-of-modern-Object-Oriented-Programming-Is-Bobrow-and-Winograds-An-Overview-of-KRL-1976-the-first-publication-using-OO-in/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=504036911&comment_type=2)

*2026-03-03*

An interesting side note is that — back in the 60s and 70s — pretty much no one was trying to establish “priority” of ideas — and the community’s use of “memos” and “notes” were how things were quickly communicated publically.

The parent institutions were a bit random on how well these survived. For example, MIT was extremely good at not just giving rise to “memos” and “TRs”, but in preserving them. Utah and e.g. Stanford also had many memos and TRs, but there was no system to preserve them (I’m assuming that most did get preserved but one can’t go to a site as. convenient as MIT’s CSAIL’s to be able to search the thoughts of both the LCS and MIT-AI).

Because the official journals were so slow at reviewing and publishing, much more publishing was done for the various -JCCs etc.

However, the community did have a good memory of who did what, when, so that eventual recognition was pretty accurate (and it was a sign of those particular times that virtually no one was in it either for recognition, or for money (there wasn’t much to be had in those days), but for the thrill of actually being able to do the work and art with like-minded colleagues.

## [How much did Daniel Bobrow and Alan Kay interact at PARC in the days that led to the invention of modern Object Oriented Programming Is Bobrow and Winograds An Overview of KRL 1976 the first publication using OO in](https://www.quora.com/How-much-did-Daniel-Bobrow-and-Alan-Kay-interact-at-PARC-in-the-days-that-led-to-the-invention-of-modern-Object-Oriented-Programming-Is-Bobrow-and-Winograds-An-Overview-of-KRL-1976-the-first-publication-using-OO-in/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=503932676&comment_type=2)

*2026-03-02*

Why wouldn’t you decide that “common-usage” (and modern definition of the term) originated with C++? Bjorne specifically said that he was not trying to do something like Smalltalk, but was instead going to do to C what Simula did to Algol? And Java was eventually touted as a better way to do something like C++?

These are the two most used languages called “OO”, and it could be argued that C++ didn’t need either of the earlier late bound “OO” languages (Smalltalk and Lisp-based) to have happened before it (although I think that enough really interesting and thought provoking stuff had been done in Sketchpad and Smalltalk to make the general approach attractive).

Also, because of the very late-bound and extensible nature of Smalltalk-72, it was possible to not include inheritance as a feature, and yet experiment with many forms of it beyond that which was like Simula. For example, there was what came be to called “slot inheritance” — the orginal idea and use I credit to Pat Winston in 1970 (you avoided mentioning him — though I did in my ACM HOPL paper) — with which many experiments were done early in Smalltalk (many by Larry Tesler) and this would have been a very good principle to carry forward in Smalltalk-76 (but we were more focused in other areas of language design and had to make decisions about how to deploy our small forces.

Personally, if the key issue is “inheritance” I would select Simula 67 as the origin of your version of “OO” — my version of “OO” (and the origin of the term) was rather different and depended on being able to send messages and not to rely on a sematics that was ultimately functional.

## [How much did Daniel Bobrow and Alan Kay interact at PARC in the days that led to the invention of modern Object Oriented Programming Is Bobrow and Winograds An Overview of KRL 1976 the first publication using OO in](https://www.quora.com/How-much-did-Daniel-Bobrow-and-Alan-Kay-interact-at-PARC-in-the-days-that-led-to-the-invention-of-modern-Object-Oriented-Programming-Is-Bobrow-and-Winograds-An-Overview-of-KRL-1976-the-first-publication-using-OO-in/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=503869010&comment_type=2)

*2026-03-01*

Here’s an example of why I think your approach to “history” is not strong or rigorous enough: you say in your answer: *“OOP as understood today was born in 1976 via the interaction between Bobrow’s and Kay’s teams at PARC.”*

Even if I agreed with your definition of “*OOP as understood today*” (I don’t) it quite misses the actual historical interactions. The whole sentence is not accurate in several important ways — it is basically false.

Part of the reason is that it confuses when papers were written and published with cause and effect, and also misses the interactions in the ARPA community about this particular subject over the previous 10 years.

As I mentioned in my answer above, I met and talked with many of my fellow ARPA reseachers from the time I started grad school in 1966 (ARPA even had a serious travel budget for grad students!). I especially liked to chat with AI researchers because one large part of the research back then was how to represent concepts and manipulate them.

Another confusion is possibly that back then many of the important “papers” were “Research Notes”, “Technical Reports”, Theses, etc. not journal publications. (This is partly because there was too much lag between ideas and publishing them, and partly because ARPA didn’t care about journals (there was no “publish or perish” back then in its research community).

I think — for Quora purposes — it’s enough just to disagree strongly (a) with your guesses on timing, (b) what you are basing your guesses on, and (c) with your guesses on how the ideas came about via people and interactions.

As for origins, here’s a nice quote from Goethe “We should all share in the excitement of Discovery without vain attempts to claim Priority”.

## [How much did Daniel Bobrow and Alan Kay interact at PARC in the days that led to the invention of modern Object Oriented Programming Is Bobrow and Winograds An Overview of KRL 1976 the first publication using OO in](https://www.quora.com/How-much-did-Daniel-Bobrow-and-Alan-Kay-interact-at-PARC-in-the-days-that-led-to-the-invention-of-modern-Object-Oriented-Programming-Is-Bobrow-and-Winograds-An-Overview-of-KRL-1976-the-first-publication-using-OO-in/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=503824122&comment_type=2)

*2026-03-01*

“The best way to predict the future is to invent it” is something I said in a meeting at Xerox Parc in 1971. (If you think about this, it is not a new idea, just a new phrasing of an old idea — and came out spontaneously). It turned out to be memorable, and eventually found its way into printed media.

Similarly, with “object-oriented programming”. It came out spontaneously when someone asked me what I was thinking about a couple of months into grad school in late 1966.

The idea of making scalable and safe-systems by using encapsulated modules had been around for a long time in various fields, and by “modules that could be instantiated” for almost as long (think Biology). The word “object” was already in use (but generally meaning a data-structure with multiple fields), so I did something I think is a bad idea: to use an old term in a new meaning. I should have thought harder and said something like “process oriented computing”.

But I think the idea of requiring something to be in print before it becomes official is also not good. For example, the bios in Wikipedia not only don’t allow direct testimonies by the subjects, but require a double hearsay (which process — single or double — is not allowed allowed in court).

Does this whole line of “history” make any sense?

## [How long have you been designing a computer system](https://www.quora.com/How-long-have-you-been-designing-a-computer-system/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=503334256&comment_type=2)

*2026-02-24*

Despite the name, “Autocoder” was “just” a macro-assembler (with special features to allow assembly time optimization of the macro expansion). This was very much like having a subroutine library that could tweak itself up to a point.

As with most such tools, it was a mixed bag, and most macros were used by the person who wrote them. Sharing macros — especially when the “tailoring to conditions” features were used — vastly increased the difficulties of debugging in machine code with core dumps — most debugging was done at one’s desk, you were lucky to get one or two minute a day with a machine and its operator (it was bad enough to debug one’s own creations!).

However, this is a good analogy to think about the consequences of trying to generate with an “AI”. You will get a lot of code you didn’t think through, and even when it seems to work you will not know about possible bad edge-cases and lurking bugs.

There hasn’t been a Moore’s Law for SW to match up to the simple but vast scalings of HW, and “AI”s don’t constitute an advance IMO.

## [Mr Alan Kay You ve said Smalltalk was about messages not objects and about creating a medium for thought Do you think modern software has lost that goal and is it still possible today to build systems that truly](https://www.quora.com/Mr-Alan-Kay-You-ve-said-Smalltalk-was-about-messages-not-objects-and-about-creating-a-medium-for-thought-Do-you-think-modern-software-has-lost-that-goal-and-is-it-still-possible-today-to-build-systems-that-truly/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=501841052&comment_type=2)

*2026-02-09*

Suppose the “music book” is taken literally — for example, it is in MIDI form, or by a program that directly transcribes the notes into MIDI?

For example, we know enough about the practices of the 19th century to be sure that what we would then hear would be very far from what Chopin was thinking about or played. And this is also rather far from today’s human pianists playing earlier music as well.

Written music is almost always — at best — hints from the composer as to what was in mind when the piece was composed. These were to be combined with “present understanding” of styles, and the pianists’ own styles in performance. Some composers didn’t like how others interpreted their pieces, and might put many more directions into the written score. But “nuance” is the perfect word for what gets missed in the explicit “message”.

This is one way to see what “The Music is not in the music” might be driving at. The written score is trying to preserve *something* but it is the hints rather than the Music itself.

## [Are Smalltalk and Pharo out dated](https://www.quora.com/Are-Smalltalk-and-Pharo-out-dated/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=501576010&comment_type=2)

*2026-02-06*

This is a very thoughtful comment, and I don’t have a good direct answer. But I would ask the question: why not invent a new language from scratch that really makes a difference with today’s situations and issues — especially: scaling, safety, levels of abstraction, pervasive networking, turning both “intentions” and “requirements” into programs, undoability at all levels (including larger system levels, etc.), and many more.

Starting off with the idea of *improving* the Smalltalk approach could be a red herring, and a real barrier to the kinds of thinking that need to be done. I.e. the problem finding should be stated along the lines of “how can we qualitatively improve programming over all” rather than “how can we improve Smalltalk-like languages (qualitatively or not).

## [](https://www.quora.comhttps://codecamp.quora.com/What-people-misunderstand-about-OOP-1?comment_id=499429800&comment_type=2)

*2026-01-16*

We are still referring to two rather different meanings for “OOP” — and I’ll admit that mine — while earlier — is not the one most people use.

The way I thought of it in 1966 was as a universal way to *define* computational elements using only one kind of building block *if* the software engineering could be done to make it work “all the way down”.

It was not a programming paradigm, but a structural, design, and definitional method — it allows every kind of entity in any kind of programming paradigm to be easily defined using a single organizing principle.

You could use *real OOP* (my phrase for what I was thinking about 60 years ago) to easily create your favorite forms and tools for your favorite way to program. You say “I envision projects from start to finish and start designing the blocks and program flow in my head.” Making your programming “blocks” using “real OOP” simply puts a meta-level underneath everything — AND — provides a way to add or change the underlying semantics of these as your learning curve moves upwards.

*Real OOP* is really a meta-language. One can use its systems nature directly (for things that are more system-like than algorithm-like) or indirectly (or vice versa — for example to recapitulate older ways of thinking about programming).

Most programming languages don’t have a meta-level and most programmers don’t “think meta” — this is where much confusion has arisen.

I hope this better clarifies this question and answer.

---

## [Why do Fender guitars have different headstocks than other guitars](https://www.quora.com/Why-do-Fender-guitars-have-different-headstocks-than-other-guitars/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=497135390&comment_type=2)

*2025-12-25*

Besides the natural conservatism — actually quite a bit of it is genetic — of humans, there is the question of risk vs reward in experimentation, especially with musical instruments which often take hundreds of hours to make.

For example, with pipe organs — which take thousands of hours — and which had very few changes over hundreds of years (and most of them regional) — I found the immense jump very impressive taken by Aristite Cavialle-Coll on his very first organ, done in his 20s — and it was a very large instrument.

Nevertheless he introduced many ideas and techniques for pretty much the very first time — basically trying to get at a “symphonic” presence — and all of them worked well enough to introduce a whole new set of sounds (Cavaille-Coll actually went around France finding organists who were willing to learn to play in the new style! Two of them were Widor and Franck.).

To the main point: Cavaille-Coll’s sound created a genre—it was so striking that the 600th organ sounded very much like the early ones. He had hit a sweet spot right off the bat, and stuck with it. Genius compositions were very influenced by the sounds of these organs, and they helped solidify the genre.

Torres was an experimenter, and introduced the fan bracing, etc., but I think coupling his guitars with the young virtuoso and composer Tarrega created a genre that gradually solidified into what we think of as “classical guitars” today.

To hear a really great earlier instrument, you could try listening to one from the town of Miracourt ca 1830 — it’s a really different approach, and the extra bass strings make a wonderful addition (why didn’t then keep them?).

Here’s Raphaela Smits in a stunning performance on an original instrument.

[https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jcy7E4uHYK8](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Jcy7E4uHYK8)

## [](https://www.quora.comhttps://codecamp.quora.com/What-people-misunderstand-about-OOP-1?comment_id=495325959&comment_type=2)

*2025-12-07*

To clarify a bit … First, the term “object” was already around and already meant several different things, so I added to the confusion when I answered a question to what I was working on in 1966 with “Object oriented programming”.

Two of the existing meanings were blocks of memory with multiple fields, and LISP’s more interesting “atoms” (also occasionally called “objects”) which were basically property lists of “data” and “functions”).

Second, I should have answered “process oriented programming” because my simple minded insight back then was that you could universally define any computer entity at any level by computers on a network (one computer for each idea/process). This was a “math insight” and I realized that a lot of software engineering would need to be invented to make the insight practical (however, some of this also existed, for LISP, for Simula, for operating systems).

The additional attraction — besides the realization that you could simulate any idea you needed — was that (a) “instantiation” was crucial: to get more instances of a useful idea, and (b) to be able to parameterize them somehow. This was very much prompted by Sketchpad and Simula.

An interesting question — to this day — is how to safely parameterize when making instances? Internal properties was one pretty good way.

Another interesting question — again to this day — was how group “similar” collections of instances — for example, we have both Plato’s way (today we’d call this “class-based”) and Aristotle’s way (today we’d call this “grouping by similarities”.

The year after I first saw Sketchpad and Simula which catalyzed my interests, Simula 67 appeared with new interesting design features, including inheritance, which was “too powerful” but tantalizing.

To cut to the chase: I’ve always thought of this area of research as just one part of trying to design compact flexible (etc.) systems from ways to think about modules. I never thought that any version of OOP was a programming paradigm, but as a universal way to describe computer entities in ways that allow them to be late bound and loosely coupled.

## [Was the work of Adele Goldberg and others introducing children to programming with Smalltalk not seen as a duplication of Paperts work at MIT Why a new teaching language Why not just teach the children LOGO](https://www.quora.com/Was-the-work-of-Adele-Goldberg-and-others-introducing-children-to-programming-with-Smalltalk-not-seen-as-a-duplication-of-Paperts-work-at-MIT-Why-a-new-teaching-language-Why-not-just-teach-the-children-LOGO/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=494378562&comment_type=2)

*2025-11-28*

Just to clarify about the original LOGO project, it wasn’t used to compare computer languages to human languages, but to provide a way for children to understand certain aspects of human languages — for example, 12 year olds making a Pig Latin translator, or translating from English to French. In many ways, the examples chosen, and what the children were able to do, taught them much more deeply about programming than the LOGO turtle work, which came later, and from Seymour.

This is because you can “stay trivial” with Turtle Graphics, and a much better teacher is needed — as Seymour was — to get the magic to happen. The results — when attempted in regular classrooms — were often disappointing.

Yes, both Licklider and Bob Taylor were experimental psychogists who were in charge of ARPA computer research, and for Taylor, also the computer research later at Parc.

An important principle of the ARPA/Parc era (also shared by ONR, etc.) was that the “powers that be” had to separate “responsibility” from “control” (and they had to do this in the “command and control” culture of the Department of Defense). Not doing this is the fatal error of most subsequent attempts at recreating something like the original ARPA or Parc institutions.

So, besides the humanities, and especially psychology/biology furnishing Lick with more perspectives to come up with visions (e.g. “symbiotic relationships” in biology), the fact that he was not a technologist helped him come up with the principle to separate “responsibility” from “control”: he was “responsible” and “accountable” for the whole research community, but he realized that the scientists/researchers had to “control” the problem choosing and methods.

In a sentence, “Visions instead of goals, People instead of projects”.

This can be extended to the idea that “people who have expertise in making/accumulating wealth rarely have the expertise of people who have spent their lives learning about and working on technical problems” i.e. funders should fund, researchers should research.

I have written elsewhere about how and why this worked so well.

---

## [Was the work of Adele Goldberg and others introducing children to programming with Smalltalk not seen as a duplication of Paperts work at MIT Why a new teaching language Why not just teach the children LOGO](https://www.quora.com/Was-the-work-of-Adele-Goldberg-and-others-introducing-children-to-programming-with-Smalltalk-not-seen-as-a-duplication-of-Paperts-work-at-MIT-Why-a-new-teaching-language-Why-not-just-teach-the-children-LOGO/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=494377395&comment_type=2)

*2025-11-28*

Richard Hamming (the 3rd Turing Award winner) had many great pieces of advice. A really good one was: “It is much better to work on the right problem in poor ways than to work on the wrong problem in good ways” (or something to that effect).

The ARPA/Parc era (60s and 70s) was characterized by research funders who wanted and were willing to pay for “problem finding” — generally speaking, they were not out after engineering projects to produce artifacts but ideas/methods that could help us deal with our severe problems (many of which were of human origin).

## [Was the work of Adele Goldberg and others introducing children to programming with Smalltalk not seen as a duplication of Paperts work at MIT Why a new teaching language Why not just teach the children LOGO](https://www.quora.com/Was-the-work-of-Adele-Goldberg-and-others-introducing-children-to-programming-with-Smalltalk-not-seen-as-a-duplication-of-Paperts-work-at-MIT-Why-a-new-teaching-language-Why-not-just-teach-the-children-LOGO/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=494169026&comment_type=2)

*2025-11-26*

SLOGO was an early idea at Parc and I don’t know of any outside implementations. However, the LOGO communities responded to Smalltalk by coming up with a variety of “LOGOs with objects” (they realized that the turtle was actually an object and that it would be nice to instantiate it, and therefore why not extend LOGO generally with objects?).

One of the ways they did this was to keep the functional “command” syntax of LOGO and introduce a new verb “TELL” that would allow you to send a message, etc. This was (a) clear, (b) introduced more stuff to learn, and (c) did not allow the simple uniform extension mechanism of Smalltalk …

## [What did Claude Shannon do and accomplish according to Alan Kay](https://www.quora.com/What-did-Claude-Shannon-do-and-accomplish-according-to-Alan-Kay/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=493224252&comment_type=2)

*2025-11-17*

Yes, Bell Labs was a national treasure before the (necessary) “divestiture” which allowed other companies to produce telephone related products (the monopoly worked in certain areas, but held back progress in others — such as modems, etc.).

One of the benefits of the monopoly was Bell Labs. There were signs on the walls that said “Either do something very useful or very beautiful”. The “baby Bells” (the regional Bell companies) were the source of about half of the funding of Bell Labs. When these were unhooked, the central company made the mistake of trying to turn Bell Labs into a product producing organization (which eventually turned into Lucent Inc, which eventually failed).

One of the signs of progression was that many of the scientific fields we have today didn’t exist when Nobel set up the prizes, and the Nobel Committee was conservative (they couldn’t even award Einstein a Nobel for either of the Relativitiy Theories, but eventually gave him one for the much safer photoelectric effect …).

However, there are other new prizes that have been set up that are deemed “Nobel Equivalents” in various fields, such as: the Turing Award, the Draper Prize, the Kyoto Prize, etc.

## [Is it possible to design a self consistent computational universe where a small Forth like meta model provides the semantic substrate similar to how Smalltalk provided an object substrate](https://www.quora.com/Is-it-possible-to-design-a-self-consistent-computational-universe-where-a-small-Forth-like-meta-model-provides-the-semantic-substrate-similar-to-how-Smalltalk-provided-an-object-substrate/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=492609110&comment_type=2)

*2025-11-11*

Not to be contrary, but I think you have to deal with the underlying issues of both of your alternatives (this is what I meant above when I said you will have to address *“What is actually needed” perspectives regarding allocation of storage & levels, processor, safety, security, etc.*

Also, part of the meta definition should allow you to not to have to “freeze” anything. Basically, you will be on a learning curve, and many computerists get caught by too early binding of decisions that the learning has revealed to be inadequate and need to be changed. If you can’t always do that, you will wind up on a downward spiral …

Uncle Don reminds us that “The ultimate evil in programming is premature optimization” — you can hold off optimizing, but you need to emphasize an iterative/learning design process.

## [Why did Alan Kay say MS DOS was a distressing thing](https://www.quora.com/Why-did-Alan-Kay-say-MS-DOS-was-a-distressing-thing/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=492261353&comment_type=2)

*2025-11-08*

A central point here is not that one should try to provide a lot of actual OS function when one only has 16KB of RAM — but that one should provide the architecture and hooks that will be required when much more RAM is provided and needed. These can be stubs on the tiny configs, but what you don’t want to do is to take a very limited design and try to expand it: the architecture will not survive the scaling.

Also, the very first versions of the IBM PC came in two sizes: 16KB and 64KB. Not a lot of the former were bought … This was part of a larger IBM strategy, which was to post a very low price for the tiniest (and least useful configuration) — but it looked tempting to unsophisticated buyers — and then charge for “extras” which were actually needed to run the system.

A much better way to think about this would have been to realize that the reason personal computers were possible is because Moore’s Law was happening, and that means that you must include the future somehow in your designs for the present.

One way to do this is to make an operating system *architecture* (rather than a *program*) that can expand as the Moore’s Law exponentials kick in. Microsoft (and Bill) were quite unsophisticated in the late 70s and early 80s — and arguably beyond. They eventually found much more sophisticated systems designers, but a lot of the damage was already done, and produced appalling legacies — and real barriers to better designs (for example, they tried to rectify many of the initial bad ideas with .net, but the Microsoft P&L divisions fought .net and eventually neutered it … )

## [Is our capacity for problem solving limited by abstraction Particularly in Computer Science](https://www.quora.com/Is-our-capacity-for-problem-solving-limited-by-abstraction-Particularly-in-Computer-Science/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=483765877&comment_type=2)

*2025-08-27*

First, this is why I posed the question “philosophically” with an “if” at the front.

Second, quite a bit of biochemistry is now being simulated “successfully” in that it is able to predict what Nature does with high precision, and — at Caltech — exhibited something that scientists hadn’t noticed (but when they looked Nature did do what was predicted).

Third, we indeed can’t “prove” that — in general — math can totally simulate Nature. This was what Einstein meant when he said *“ ... as far as the propositions of mathematics refer to reality, they are not certain; and as far as they are certain, they do not refer to reality."**

However, if you look at Goedel’s proof, you should be able to see that modern day computer theorem provers and checkers can indeed prove this (as can many humans). The proof — and the meaning of math in general — can be considered completely syntactic. (The “prettiest” version of this proof I know of is in Minsky’s early book “Computation: Finite and Infinite Machines”, and he very cleverly uses “Goedel Numbers” as a grounded representation to make a pure Lisp in — to provide more motivation for what Goedel was actually doing, etc.)

We can “recognize a proof” about (say) various infinities (e.g. the very interesting differences between countable and uncountable infinities) but we can’t “embody” more than tiny “finities” — but that doesn’t stop us from finding ways to represent ideas about them and showing that certain relationships can or can’t exist.

And that takes us back to the original question (which was about abstractions and their limits for our own understandings).

——

 **Geometry and Experience, Lecture before the Prussian Academy of Sciences, January 27, 1921*

---

*2025-08-12*

This is a very good point Jamie. In fact, my main criticism of Smalltalk in the decade after Parc was that it wasn’t put through an adaptation phase that dealt with the reality now outside.

Inside Parc, the “image” idea was good because Smalltalk was also the OS on the Parc machines and could also take advantage of the loadable microcode they had. On the tiny memories of the Parc computers it made sense to do “Lisp sharing”, but as you point out this made every dependency look the same and made it tricky to marshall and export and exchange “useful items”.

In fact, “dependancies” in general are still a very large bugaboo for SW development of any kind, wouldn’t you agree?

## [What were the advantages and disadvantages of having complete control over hardware and software designs at Parc in the pursuit of developing personal computing](https://www.quora.com/What-were-the-advantages-and-disadvantages-of-having-complete-control-over-hardware-and-software-designs-at-Parc-in-the-pursuit-of-developing-personal-computing/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=477833516&comment_type=2)

*2025-07-10*

Hi Johnny

Our Goal (and Official Mission) was to do real long range exploratory research, and Parc was set up in Palo Alto as far away from Corporate Headquarters (which was in Connecticut) as possible, to lower the amount of meddling from execs (we also had a legal agreement with Xerox …).

However, we wanted to be “more real” than we were in the 60s, so the scheme was to try to build/support 100 users for each invention (so we had to be able to build at least 100 Altos, the Ethernet had to support 100 things plugged into it, a SW package had to be documented and bug-free enough to support 100 users, etc.

We called this “Living Lab”. This required more engineering, testing, and maintenance than just demos or prototypes, but not the level of engineering required for many 1000s of instances of products (in the end we wound up making about 2000 Altos, so this did require a bit more serious engineering).

The main computer researchers at Parc (as with ARPA) chose their research topics/problems/projects. So we couldn’t have been more happy (especially for the first 5 years).

## [What were the advantages and disadvantages of having complete control over hardware and software designs at Parc in the pursuit of developing personal computing](https://www.quora.com/What-were-the-advantages-and-disadvantages-of-having-complete-control-over-hardware-and-software-designs-at-Parc-in-the-pursuit-of-developing-personal-computing/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=477828123&comment_type=2)

*2025-07-10*

Good question.

In other answers and comment replies I’ve especially pointed to FPGAs as one modern semi-equivalent to the use of microcode in the past. “Semi-” means that the special leverages you get with FPGAs are things like massive parallelism, integration of memory and processes, special kinds of architectures for special needs, etc.

Microcode was a simple naive question of being able to run many cycles during one main memory cycle (if you could always be waiting on main memory, you had won the game (meaning that no discrete HW could beat your microcode program)).

## [How can I convince myself that real OOP is a better way to build systems What s the reason other than Alan Kay s god given vision Is there any math behind these ideas or are they just a bunch of good looking ideas](https://www.quora.com/How-can-I-convince-myself-that-real-OOP-is-a-better-way-to-build-systems-What-s-the-reason-other-than-Alan-Kay-s-god-given-vision-Is-there-any-math-behind-these-ideas-or-are-they-just-a-bunch-of-good-looking-ideas/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=475015132&comment_type=2)

*2025-06-17*

I should clarify more here. Above, I mentioned Dave Reed’s 1978 thesis (about how to go about making an “OS” for an Internet sized system). I had an urge to get a project going to actually implement it to understand any hidden problems and practical means and difficulties. For various reasons, this didn’t happen until the start of the 2000s.

The heart of the project was not me, but a very powerful trio of Dave Reed himself, David A Smith, and Andreas Raab. They should get all of the early credit — and for more recent credit: David A Smith, Vanessa Freudenberg, Aron Lunzer, Yoshiki Ohshima , etc.

The writer of your link gets confused by the 3D aspect — which is just an illustration of what can be built on top of the Croquet principles. And the last date of the link is 2011.

I’m pretty sure that LambdaMOO had no actual intersection with the Croquet work (though MOOs and MUDs were around after the Internet, and especially the Web became more available). The heart of the Croquet fun was in understanding how to do practical replicated “bit-identical” computation using “pseudo-time” over possibly very large and slow networks. The “practical” part of the sentence provides much of the “fun”.

## [Why do you think people today are less likely to explore programming on their own compared to the early days of computing](https://www.quora.com/Why-do-you-think-people-today-are-less-likely-to-explore-programming-on-their-own-compared-to-the-early-days-of-computing/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=473686879&comment_type=2)

I was thinking of more than “a few dozen”, but I’ll confess that I don’t have any official figures for any of the dates.

I can relate that there were hundreds of college students doing computing in the US in the early 60s onwards (because there were at least that many just in the ARPA research projects, most of which were in US universities , etc.).

There was a minor explosion of computers in the early 60s just from IBM 1401s along (well over 10,000), and many universities bought small minis, especially from DEC, etc. Most of the main universities have also built their own systems in the 50s onwards.

In 1969 I was talked into teaching an “advanced systems design” course at Stanford, and it had more than 100 students, etc in it. Stanford also had a “Forum” of about 75 businesses — online live video, etc. — and I wound up teaching a few courses through this (about 5 to 20 at each company).

The percentage of self-learners back then is a bit of a mystery …

*2025-06-03*

People have wide differences in what they think of as “early” — I think many people relate most things to their own lives rather than to history.

## [Why do you think people today are less likely to explore programming on their own compared to the early days of computing](https://www.quora.com/Why-do-you-think-people-today-are-less-likely-to-explore-programming-on-their-own-compared-to-the-early-days-of-computing/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=473149754&comment_type=2)

*2025-06-02*

The earliest Macs offered “desktop accessories”, of which IMO the most effective and wonderful was called “Rolodex” (by Bill Atkinson). (I loved this!) It was extremely useful, and also a proof of concept for what years later became HyperCard.

One principle we had at Parc that didn’t survive into the commercialism of the 80s was “don’t give the users anything worse when you try to give them more convenience and power” (one aspect of this was WYSIWYG and considerable efforts towards readable text on displays, etc.).

And, the GUI at Parc allowed various kinds of safe end-user programming, even for kids. Also, the first micros were often shipped with a form of BASIC and this was always there for many early micros.

When I joined Apple and, after admiring Rolodex, I pointed out that a really great additional desk accessory could be something like a safe simple end-user language. This didn’t happen until many years later until Apple started shipping HyperCard with every Mac, and this was not done like a “desk accessory” until years after this, and not for what the Mac became until “AppleTalk” was done …

I think most of us can agree that this was very much “too bad”.

## [In the early days of personal computing what one platform if successfully commercialized could have set computing on a better trajectory than the 1980s micros did Details in comments](https://www.quora.com/In-the-early-days-of-personal-computing-what-one-platform-if-successfully-commercialized-could-have-set-computing-on-a-better-trajectory-than-the-1980s-micros-did-Details-in-comments/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=470244453&comment_type=2)

*2025-05-10*

The “largest bottleneck to overall progress” is most definitely the lack of our understanding of humans, how we are affected by the ways we communicate, what we need to learn to safely deal with the “new” and with “more powerful tools and media”.

In fact, there were quite a few of us in ARPA/Parc that understood the existence of the problems — and we did do things to try to help. The big deal was that we didn’t appreciate the depth and magnitude of the problems, and didn’t come close to making a dent in how general society saw things.

Some of the sources that revealed the existence of the problems included both History *and* Anthropology/Psychology, McLuhan, Havelock, Ong, Postman, etc. Some of the problems were our own naivete in thinking — as many others have over the years — that society’s histories — and wise critics — will provide object lessons for not repeating terrible and stupid errors.

For example, just because McLuhan — and especially Postman — understood how media affect societal thinking, and were able to compare reading with television, there was no deep reason why society as a whole would heed this (both with regard to TV, and with future inventions that could be made to affect like TV). So we should have done a lot more to explain the potential bad that lurked behind the good.

If I could go back in time, this is what I’d work on (and I’d include other real dangers that people in general (and the “bizbiz” mind in general) sweep under the rug for short term gains) — for example, climate, governance, pollution, etc.).

---

## [In the early days of personal computing what one platform if successfully commercialized could have set computing on a better trajectory than the 1980s micros did Details in comments](https://www.quora.com/In-the-early-days-of-personal-computing-what-one-platform-if-successfully-commercialized-could-have-set-computing-on-a-better-trajectory-than-the-1980s-micros-did-Details-in-comments/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=470155737&comment_type=2)

*2025-05-09*

Hi Mark

This is pretty much the way most of us in the ARPA community thought from the late 50s (before ARPA) until now. Basically, it was about “intellectual amplifiers interconnected worldwide” not “personal computers on a network”.

The earliest metaphors/analogies were to “utilities” that already existed for e.g. water, power, gas, etc. and these were echoed by various forms of early time-sharing with SAGE, CTSS, etc.

The need/desire for graphics, UIs to match up with the mass of users, gradually resulted in smarter and smarter “terminals”, which soon were made from full-blown computers themselves, connected to main-frames, and eventually via networking. (Ivan Sutherland had a great joke about this movement.)

My Dynabook idea was never stand-alone, etc., and Larry Roberts and I used to make bets about how much computing would be internal by the time decent flat-screen displays appeared (he thought the displays would show up earlier and most of the computing would be external to the hand-held device).

The first community to think/want stand-alone personal computers was the 8-bit micro community in the 70s, and one could rightly argue than they really didn’t understand (they didn’t!).

“Personal computing” ultimately depended on bandwidth, latency, capacities, UIs, etc. And, even with the amazing extension of Moore’s Law into this century (far beyond what we used for extrapolating in the 70s), we still wind up with tradeoffs between local and global shared computation.

But your first comment asked what we should fix if we could go back in time, and you didn’t really answer that yourself. (I will try to answer this in another comment soon to come.)

---

## [In the early days of personal computing what one platform if successfully commercialized could have set computing on a better trajectory than the 1980s micros did Details in comments](https://www.quora.com/In-the-early-days-of-personal-computing-what-one-platform-if-successfully-commercialized-could-have-set-computing-on-a-better-trajectory-than-the-1980s-micros-did-Details-in-comments/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=469855277&comment_type=2)

*2025-05-07*

Pretty much everyone who has tried a VHL architecture for a CPU has blown it the first time around (we were lucky at Parc that we had blown it already in the 60s before Parc). The main culprit has been “trying to do too many things” in silicon combined with its sibling “too many optimizations”.

Once you calm down and try to accomplish just a few key deep needs, it is much easier to get special architectures to pay off.

At the technical level, I think an HP/Parc joint project would have worked really well (we knew and admired quite a few of the HPers back then). (And, of course, when Mead-Conway came along, it was HP that did the heavy lifting on the fast-fab part.)

An important side point here is that — as Butler used to point out — every couple of years the design approach changes qualitatively. This means that good designers would not have tried to do an Alto on a chip, but would be — again — looking at whole system design and seeing what that actually meant.

For example, with much higher levels of integration, having to emulate multiple processors with a very fast single HW CPU would start to give way to actually using multiple processors for some of the work (this was pointed out in the first Dynabook paper in 1972).

The Notetaker used 3 8086s for (Smalltalk, Graphics, I/O) but these could have wound up being different types in a productization of the Notetaker. (The idea of needing and using a GPU went all the way back to Utah in the 60s, so it was not a new idea.)

---

## [In the early days of personal computing what one platform if successfully commercialized could have set computing on a better trajectory than the 1980s micros did Details in comments](https://www.quora.com/In-the-early-days-of-personal-computing-what-one-platform-if-successfully-commercialized-could-have-set-computing-on-a-better-trajectory-than-the-1980s-micros-did-Details-in-comments/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=469647016&comment_type=2)

*2025-05-06*

Hi Helge

Let me start with the end of your comment. I had the Dynabook idea in 1968 — and of course thought about “when will there be a flat-screen display that will minimally work?” as one question among the many. I was in the middle of the Flex Machine with Ed Cheadle, so we had an idea of the actual transistor count to do the kind of SW we though of as “minimal personal computer”.

However, I did take a much closer look when I got asked to write the 1972 “A personal computer for children of all ages” paper for the ACM Nat’l Conference. By this time I was at Parc, we were doing a major mainframe computer with the new Intel 1103s, thinking about the Alto, looking at Smalltalk, etc. And, Xerox had several viable technologies for non-CRT displays …

In 1972 — in part because of MOS — it was much easier to think about various futures for silicon (and most flat-screen technology was going to need some form of “thin-film” technology to deal with finding and setting pixels). In that paper I noted that one interesting trade-off was that multiple slow (but cheap) CPUs were a natural fit to the computing needs of a personal computer (we did do this architecture later in the 70s in the Notetaker, and it worked will with MOS CPUs).

The gating factor — whether we knew what we were doing or not in SW — was price/bit of RAM. The next factor was could we do our own CPU chips and at what level of scale?

An interesting question to go along side your questions: what if any of the chip companies had been at all interested in SW? (None of any capacity were.) Intel could have been a natural partner, but Andy Grove didn’t even what to make any kind of CPUs, let alone HHL ones.

The fast MSI microcode inner machine of the Alto was the solution to the problems of Intel not being interested in SW, and programming being difficult. However, it was also expensive (and stayed expensive) as Moore’s Law brought down the cost of MOS RAM.

My best guess right now is that a different design trajectory on the same time path as the 8086 or 68K could have produced a fair amount earlier multiple CPUs that would fit most of what we knew in the early 70s.

Another way to look at this is through the ideas and understandings of the “Mead-Conway” VLSI design project done jointly with Parc and Caltech (and whose main MVP hero was Lynn Conway). That could have started earlier in the 70s (it was a collection of fortuitous accidents that it started at all, and required very special people, so maybe it could only have happened when it did).

Yet another really interesting route that could have been taken would have been a joint project between Parc and HP (the vibes were much more similar, and there were already many friendships). HP did do its own chips and fab, and was very interested in more personal systems. Parc had the SW/architecture expertise. That could have been really interesting.

Basically, back then, the best people in the world pushed available envelopes as hard as they could be pushed. A different theory of “business” would have allowed a few more envelopes to be available. Because things really need to be built in Software Engineering to vet designs — especially then — the SW learning curves and learning latencies still pretty much existed.

And, there could have been more “welcoming silicon arctitectures” in the 80s to what was learned in SW in the 70s.

---

## [Why wasnt the GUI by Xerox PARC or Xerox Star developed earlier There was Sketchpad in 1963 which was a predecessor of a GUI 20 years before](https://www.quora.com/Why-wasnt-the-GUI-by-Xerox-PARC-or-Xerox-Star-developed-earlier-There-was-Sketchpad-in-1963-which-was-a-predecessor-of-a-GUI-20-years-before/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=469573462&comment_type=2)

*2025-05-05*

There are a few misunderstandings in your comment, but let me just correct the one about the Alto. The Alto could have a chain of pointers to any size rectangular bit-maps, ranging from one rectangle that subtended the entire display of 808x606 1-bit pixels, to many smaller rectangles (and there were also “size of pixel” modes. All could be mixed and matched. This allowed different software research groups to play with tradeoffs of various kinds.

For example,

the bit-map 2.5 D animations used double-buffering of double sized pixels — this produced smooth animations with still reasonable resolution.

The forerunner of MS Word (called Bravo) used a very intricate scheme for both text and display to minimize display memory for textual graphics

The Smalltalk group mostly used full res full bit maps — so *did* keep the whole bit-map in memory — but also used the multi-modes for some of the research e.g. the SHAZAM animation development system, color display on the Alto, etc.

---

## [In the early days of personal computing what one platform if successfully commercialized could have set computing on a better trajectory than the 1980s micros did Details in comments](https://www.quora.com/In-the-early-days-of-personal-computing-what-one-platform-if-successfully-commercialized-could-have-set-computing-on-a-better-trajectory-than-the-1980s-micros-did-Details-in-comments/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=469570407&comment_type=2)

*2025-05-05*

That is one of the important central questions I was grazingly addressing.

One of the reasons more planning in countries and global economies hasn’t worked anywhere near as well as the idea should, seems to be that there are quite a few people who try to game every situation, and do things when it would be better to wait a little.

The Parc situation was an interesting example of planning — and was very much an extension of ARPA style. In this case, we realized — pretty obvious really — that it was going to take a lot of time and work to invent the software and user interface designs for personal computing — and that we’d be behind a very bad 8 ball if we did this work on contemporary HW. The solution was to “believe Moore’s Law” and use extra money to “buy into the future” — in other words, to make a research personal computer that SW of the future could be done NOW, and which Moore’s Law would make affordable about 10–15 years hence.

The government and other computer companies could have also done this, but they didn’t.

Given the general lack of trust and the urge to game things, it is possible that the (bad) way things happened, might be the only way they could have happened.

The above sentence could also apply to the (now very likely) climate disasters to come. Our species could be just too short term, short-sighted, and greedy to be able to survive.

---

## [How did Smalltalk 80 manage to work with only 32 000 objects maximum roughly](https://www.quora.com/How-did-Smalltalk-80-manage-to-work-with-only-32-000-objects-maximum-roughly/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=467420401&comment_type=2)

*2025-04-19*

One of the basic processes at Parc aimed first at notions of what was needed, and then at edge inventions and engineering.

The Alto screen was 808 by 606 because that was deemed to be the minimum pixels needed to show what a full 8.5x11 sheet of paper could hold — this display was oriented in portrait mode for this reason.

The researchers realized at the time that the human visual system is more horizontally oriented and wanted a two full pages display. This was done as soon as more capacity became available — it was not quite twice as large in terms of pixels, but did fit two full pages.

## [How did early software developers manage to create efficient programs with such limited computing resources](https://www.quora.com/How-did-early-software-developers-manage-to-create-efficient-programs-with-such-limited-computing-resources/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=466221284&comment_type=2)

*2025-04-10*

Again, the question and topic here is *early* SW, and back then, compile time was not at all trivial. Here is a quote from a letter by B.G.Oldfield to John Backus giving some statistics from the *early* FORTRAN compiler:

“Our average compiling time is approximately **15 FORTRAN statements per minute** and, using an expansion ratio of 7, this means that **105 machine language instructions per minute** can** **be compiled. Considering the addition of subroutines and input and output, **compiling time will normally require two to three times as much 704 time as an equivalent assembly.**”

FORTRAN in the late 50s was very interesting for the efficiency of the code it generated, but I can tell you that it took a very long time to compile (and in an era where computer time was much more expensive than human time). ## [How did early software developers manage to create efficient programs with such limited computing resources](https://www.quora.com/How-did-early-software-developers-manage-to-create-efficient-programs-with-such-limited-computing-resources/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=465552236&comment_type=2)

*2025-04-06*

A good question, and difficult to give a Quora size answer that does both groups justice.

A big difference was where the two groups were in the “personal computing” learning curve. The “micros” were people who wanted to “touch and do”, and to some extent to learn. What they were happy with was very different from the ARPA/Parc community (which e.g. had been on their learning curve and vision path since 1962 (and earlier)), and whose members were mostly card carrying scientists and engineers).

By the time 8-bit micros came along — ca 1975 — ARPA had already made deep impressive personal computing systems, and Parc represented a third generation of visions and goals.

Another difference was that — for many people — the “micros” were happy to do what the 8-bit CPUs and the small memories were able to do. In contrast, the ARPA/Parc community was driven by visions of what *should* been done, and *what was actually needed*. This resulted in many new inventions at all levels, and lots of fundamental building and testing.

Commercially, in the 80s, these differences resulted in many “unfortunate” designs setting up very poor defacto standards for too many areas of what personal computing should have been. Many of these problems persist to today (in part, because it is hard to get rid of bad standards once they’ve been adopted).

*2025-04-06*

Yes. The Lexitron is one of the “dedicated word-processors” being sold (for about $18K IIRC) that we tried to get Xerox to look at (especially the Lexitron 900) — and expand their imaginations to realize that a “productized version of the Alto” could also be made and sold for about the same price, could do what the Lexitron did (and much better), but could also be a general purpose personal office machine. Etc.

If Xerox had been in a different position competitively during this time, I think they might have been able to come around to this realization (but this didn’t happen). It would have been an important stepping stone.

It’s important to realize here that the Alto had already been running for 4 years starting in early 1973, so productization would have required up to date components, and some changes to the design (this set of ideas was sometimes referred to as the “ALTO III”).

## [How did early software developers manage to create efficient programs with such limited computing resources](https://www.quora.com/How-did-early-software-developers-manage-to-create-efficient-programs-with-such-limited-computing-resources/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=465108286&comment_type=2)

*2025-04-03*

From John von Neumann: “Mathematics is the relationships of relationships” (i.e. it is a plural for a reason, and mathematical thinkers are allowed to make up a new math when it will help understanding and reasoning about ideas).

This can be tremendously helpful in computing, e.g. as opposed to just jumping in and coding in whatever is available.

An important question should always be “Will what I’m doing be helped by inventing and implementing a better mathematical framework?” … (This is one of the main underlying impulses for inventing higher level languages — however, hardly anything has happened since the 80s — my conclusion is that something important got lost by the new wave of computer people.)

## [How did early software developers manage to create efficient programs with such limited computing resources](https://www.quora.com/How-did-early-software-developers-manage-to-create-efficient-programs-with-such-limited-computing-resources/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=465107460&comment_type=2)

*2025-04-03*

Certainly not in the case of the far right of your diagram, and for several reasons

(a) the Alto — as a time-machine — was far beyond the class (and architecture) of 8-bit micros, and for most early 16-bit micros — such as the 68K and 8086

(b) our group (which did Smalltalk, etc.) did not use an OS (we realized that a reasonable reflective dynamic OOP language already was more powerful/useful than a separate “OS”).

Other, closer to home research projects, did use a BCPL made OS designed by Butler Lampson, and based on an earlier design by Strachey. This was eventually replaced by the Cedar/Mesa system (which was similar to the Smalltalk system design).

The philosophies of computing were extremely different between Parc and those who were doing things with 8-bit microprocessors.
