## [How did early software developers manage to create efficient programs with such limited computing resources](https://www.quora.com/How-did-early-software-developers-manage-to-create-efficient-programs-with-such-limited-computing-resources/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=465099845&comment_type=2)

*2025-04-03*

“Math wins!” means that “really using what ‘math’ is all about” will often wind up with much more compact, understandable, powerful, etc. representations of the intrinsic relationships of a situation (it doesn’t mean that some existing math is going to help (sometimes it does), but means that “math itself” is likely to help.

The actual history of Algol-60 is not just about coming up with features and forms for the features, but one of the great results was a leap forward in how to represent the design of a programming language in general (what is now call Backus Naur Form (BNF)). This is a “meta-language” (a language for talking about/describing programming languages.

A description using BNF is much clearer, but it still “just sits there” on a page. We would like to somehow get a program that would take in an Algol level program, and — according to the description in BNF — at least parse it to see if the program is syntactically correct.

And, we’d actually like our parsing program to also generate code for some machine, so the result is a “meaningful program” than can itself be run.

If we can do this, then we should be able to describe the meta-language — as a parsing and generating program — in itself by creating a description of itself using its own conventions. (That is the “Figure 5” example I showed above.)

A fun exercise — and also fun to watch — is for a student to hand simulate Figure 5 and run the result on the simple Meta II interpretive machine given in the paper.

And, with this the student (and we) can:

make language compilers like the Algolic ones in the paper

improve our meta-language translator by running new descriptions of itself through old running versions, etc.

Understand more deeply, the *idea* of **meta**

Understand more deeply how “math wins!”

An important idea here is that (a) this can be done even more simply than Val showed us, and (b) it can also be done more elaborately and powerfully (as Val has pointed ways to think about things like this).

You say ~8K LO C++ today. I’m not doubting you. That’s still a factor of 16 larger than Nile done about 17 years ago (which — by Moore’s Law, if it still means anything — is about 11 doublings, which is about a factor of 2000).

## [American computer pioneer Alan Kay s concept the Dynabook was published in 1972 How come Steve Jobs and Apple iPad get the credit for tablet invention](https://www.quora.com/American-computer-pioneer-Alan-Kay-s-concept-the-Dynabook-was-published-in-1972-How-come-Steve-Jobs-and-Apple-iPad-get-the-credit-for-tablet-invention/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=463463082&comment_type=2)

*2025-03-24*

I don’t think I’m “arguing” here. I’m just a much more reliable source of first hand information.

Also, I’m just about to be 85 and have cancer. I have better ways to use my time than to go through the often ridiculous process of trying to get errors corrected in Wikipedia (and, now, I’m starting to think, in Quora).

First, let me suggest that you actually listen to the Xerox YouTube piece. If you do, you will see that that it is what is called an “image piece” (it is not a commercial at all — it is talking about a future time and that customers will then have “something like this” — it is quite clear about this).

Some of what it shows is real software (the “Laurel” GUI for email) and some is made up (it acts as though it responds to voice commands). Parc had nothing to do with this piece.

2023 was the 50th anniversary of the Alto and a number of us who worked on in celebrated at the Computer History Museum. One part of the prep was to try to estimate how many Altos were actually made. The best we could come up with was “between 1500 and 2000”. You give a more accurate looking figure of “2120” (which I’ve never seen before). Do you have a source? (This is more that anyone who worked on the Alto came up with.)

The reason there were “at least 1500 made” was because they became popular, first within Parc, then within Xerox, and then we started to give them to universities, and even gave a bunch to the White House Executive Building. They were great for making and maintaining friendships of various kinds.

Most of the draw here was Bravo — the predecessor of Microsoft Word — the Ethernet, and the Dover Laser Printers (both also done at Parc).

It’s worth thinking about Moore’s Law again. When the Alto started working in 1973, there was nothing like it, and the most edge of the art components were used. There were some slight re-engineerings over the years, but as time went on, the Alto started to get less cost effective as a “time machine” for traveling into the future.

In 1976, it was realized — and put to Xerox — that a vastly re-engineered Alto — an “Alto III” — could be *sold* for the same price as a dedicated word processing machine (about $18K-$20K) — but of course would be able to do much more — and it would make a pretty good product in the ‘70s. Xerox was in some turmoil around then and wasn’t interested in the idea, for various reasons.

Instead, what was pretty much the design that was manufactured for research use — called the “Alto II” — kept on being made, and for about the same *cost*, and was given to various users. There never was an Alto *product*.

*By the way, your YouTube movie above is just a picture, but can be found on YouTube.

## [American computer pioneer Alan Kay s concept the Dynabook was published in 1972 How come Steve Jobs and Apple iPad get the credit for tablet invention](https://www.quora.com/American-computer-pioneer-Alan-Kay-s-concept-the-Dynabook-was-published-in-1972-How-come-Steve-Jobs-and-Apple-iPad-get-the-credit-for-tablet-invention/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=463402457&comment_type=2)

*2025-03-23*

I read both the Wikipedia article and reference [1], which is a 1981 Byte magazine article about the Alto by Thomas A Wadlow. Both have important inaccuracies — the most important of which for our discussion is by Wadlow: he correctly says:

**“It is unlikely that a person outside of the computer-science research community will ever be able to buy an Alto. They are not intended for commercial sale, but rather as development tools for Xerox, and so will not be mass-produced.”**

A little later he says something ambiguous: **“Each Alto is housed in a beautifully formed, textured beige metal cabinet that hints at its $32,000 price tag.”**

By that time, Altos **cost** about $22K to be built and obtained **inside of Xerox**. They were **never** for sale: i.e. never sold as a product — they never had a “price tag”. (However, I don’t blame you for misunderstanding the form of his sentence (and it is Wadlow who made the error of both “price” and the actual number).)

I won’t dispute your Santa Clara house figure from 1972 (but we were in Palo Alto and the photo was taken in 1977).

I think you were caught in the middle between what you term “publically available information” — which unfortunately had many errors (this is all too often the case) — and “actual facts” from someone who was one of the computer research group leaders at Parc who actually worked on Alto HW and SW.

Also, Woz had nothing to do with any of this. It’s important to understand that the first Alto started working in April 1973, and it was 3 more years before there was even an Apple Computer company (April 1976). Steve Jobs’ visit to Parc to see the technology there happened in 1979 (I was in the room), so this was 6 whole years after the Alto had been done.

The Lisa happened about 10 years after the Alto (ca 1983) and the Mac in 1984. (This is a longish and interesting story of its own.)

---

## [American computer pioneer Alan Kay s concept the Dynabook was published in 1972 How come Steve Jobs and Apple iPad get the credit for tablet invention](https://www.quora.com/American-computer-pioneer-Alan-Kay-s-concept-the-Dynabook-was-published-in-1972-How-come-Steve-Jobs-and-Apple-iPad-get-the-credit-for-tablet-invention/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=463354154&comment_type=2)

*2025-03-23*

The Alto was not a product, nor was it nearly “as expensive as the house it was set up in” (more like 1/3rd a house at the time of the photo). The point and goal were very different.

The deep idea is that a personal computer is going to require quite a bit of new design and experimentation — especially regarding the SW and user interface — so we decided to make a “time machine” that would allow us to do this work in a simulated future: i.e. on a machine that would be expensive in the 70s but — because of Moore’s Law — would be affordable by a wide range of end-users in the future.

Since personal computers will be part of a community of users, we would have to invent a HW configuration that would allow us (the research community) to build at least 100 of them and network them together.

In other words, the unit *cost* of a research Alto could be 10 times or more as high as an equivalent machine could be *sold for* 10–15 years in the future, but not much higher. Thus, we had to figure out just what this meant in practical terms.

I’m going to vastly simplify — for this reply — our thought processes, which involved many factors, including memory, graphics, computational power, etc. We were sure that we didn’t want to ask Xerox to pay more than $20K/machine (we started our thinking with $10K/Alto and let this slide upwards a bit). So we were still talking about $2M-$4M for 100 machines. We had simulated doing high quality fonts, multiple synthesized voices of music, dynamic 2.5D animation of toned graphics, etc. so we had some idea of what kind of computing power we needed ca. 1972–3.

Moore’s Law was pronounced ca 1965, and by the early 70s had settled down to something like “a factor of 2 improvement every 18 months” (there was not an equivalent for file storage hardware).

An oversimplification would be to note that — if an Alto cost ~$20K ca. 1973, then — per a very simple use of Moore’s Law (let’s be conservative and say “doubling every 2 years”) — the equivalent would cost ~$1K ca (a factor of 20 is about 4.5 turns of M.L. in about ~10 years). More realistically, we thought aiming for about 15 years out for a $1K-$2K consumer computer that could do what we wanted the Alto to be able to do was a good target.

I’m sorry, but your account of history is pretty much inaccurate sentence by sentence.

---

## [Can everything in the universe reality be described with numbers mathematics](https://www.quora.com/Can-everything-in-the-universe-reality-be-described-with-numbers-mathematics/answer/Andrew-Cook-98?comment_id=463170215&comment_type=2)

*2025-03-22*

With all due respect, you seem to be confusing the *idea* of a *number* with a particular *representation* of a number. And the decimal expansion of 1/3 is also an infinite decimal (0.33333…) but does *not* include all combinations of numbers (or binary digits).

The **idea* *of pi is easy to express, originally: a finite ratio of two finite lengths. Early on, Archimedes came up with a useful way to estimate this ratio by establishing inner and outer bounding polygons. Later were established formulas to compute pi to any degree of accuracy.

As for Shakespeare — or the Library of Congress — very large *finite* numbers with randomly picked digits will do the trick.

## [What was it like to be at Xerox PARC when Steve Jobs visited](https://www.quora.com/What-was-it-like-to-be-at-Xerox-PARC-when-Steve-Jobs-visited/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=461778469&comment_type=2)

*2025-03-14*

I wouldn’t be able to answer your questions without gathering a lot more information about what “business” might mean here (at the very least I think it means “lots of different things”).

However, a really interesting set of (then secret) systems were done for the CIA in the early 80s by Xerox Electro-Optical Systems (XEOS in Pasadena) in Smalltalk on special Parc workstations. XEOS was actually a Xerox “spook” company whose expertise was satellite photography and interpretation, etc.

One idea was to generalize spreadsheets in several important ways, including (a) making a “cell” a general MVC entity (this allowed any kind of computation and graphics etc in each cell), and (b) to not require cells to be in simple arrays (this was already in the spirit of Smalltalk). One use of these ideas was to make a “quick response to crisis” system that was much used.

Parts of these systems were later used to made two products (the “Analyst”, and “Humble” (an expert system builder)).

——

A useful idea here is to note that the general organization of the Smalltalk user world was not in stovepiped apps, but in multiple “projects” (where each project is a separate place that keeps over time all the resources needed to accomplish a “project”). These would at first seem to be multiple persistent “desktops” but with intercommunicating entities of any kind. This means that something with many features — like AutoCad — would still allow any other useful creations to be integrated, etc.

——

Another useful place to look for answers to your questions would be at great collaborative environments, starting with Engelbart’s NLS. “Communication/collaboration means agreeing on overlaps of terms and meanings” so a good business system would have much more specific design on how to intercommunicate, share, make decisions, etc. I think this is a lot of useful work that needs to be done (it is usually underdone and badly, but I think there are possibilities for better designs …).

## [What were the biggest mistakes made by Xerox](https://www.quora.com/What-were-the-biggest-mistakes-made-by-Xerox/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=456074364&comment_type=2)

*2025-02-07*

I don’t know enough of the internal details to reply to parts of your comment.

The ARPA/Parc notion of what constituted a minimal-actual personal computer was qualitatively different than other notions (which included “homebrew” ideas, IBM’s ways of thinking about their marketplace of customers, etc.)

An interesting clue as to how IBM felt about personal computers early on is that they were willing to farm out the OS of the PC — an unthinkable idea in previous IBM culture, and one that signalled they didn’t much care, and weren’t looking much ahead.

Microsoft hired Dave Cutler from DEC when they realised they (a) needed a real operating system with protected processes, etc., and (b) didn’t know enough to design and make a real one. The design that Cutler came up with was IMO too much like VAX/VMS and not enough like what a real-time OS should be like.

Again admitting my ignorance, but it was my impression during the 80s that much of IBM’s OS design talent was focussing on consolidating their mid-range systems (/36 and /38) while thinking about the AS/400 - which replaced both the System/36 and System/38. There were some good ideas here, though IBM was quite late to most of the interesting tables by then.

## [How does Alan Kay describe the nature of his way of collaboration Would he be willing to work with teams that are below average which could diminish the quality of his work and lower his motivation](https://www.quora.com/How-does-Alan-Kay-describe-the-nature-of-his-way-of-collaboration-Would-he-be-willing-to-work-with-teams-that-are-below-average-which-could-diminish-the-quality-of-his-work-and-lower-his-motivation/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=454054022&comment_type=2)

*2025-01-26*

I agree that this is a tricky phrase. Also, I think important parts of it depend on each individual (because part of “quality of effort” has to do with how we deal with what we lack).

For example, I have always been a better “starter” than a “finisher” (one way of procrastinating would be to keep starting new projects and not finishing old ones). “Quality of effort” here meant both “hanging in there” longer *and* finding colleagues to work with who were better at finishing than starting.

“Quality of effort” also has to do with the amount of will put into patience — with the rate of progress, and with dealing with others.

Basically: make a list of what you don’t do well, and figure out ways to counter these, then start countering … Etc. Judge how well you are doing at this and cut way down worrying about results …

## [What are the limitations discovered in the early programming language that triggered the need for OOP](https://www.quora.com/What-are-the-limitations-discovered-in-the-early-programming-language-that-triggered-the-need-for-OOP/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=453696033&comment_type=2)

*2025-01-24*

Squeak Smalltalk’s main reason for existence was to be an implementation language for the next end-users’/kids’ language (whatever it was going to be). We did one called “Constructo” along the way, but the one that really worked well was called “Etoys”.

Along the way within the Squeak project, we supercharged Bitblt, changed the graphics architecture to be formed around John Maloney’s “Morphic” ideas (John was part of the Squeak project), and put in a fair amount of new architecture to make Squeak self-defining so it could be ported to new HW and OSs in hours to a few days. This allowed it to spread widely.

Because Apple owned the original source of the first Smalltalk-80 release, we were able to make Squeak free and open source (MIT licence, etc).

It was in the STEPS project ca 2006 that we went for a radical set of features for a new language.

## [How was the Xerox Alto done in only 3 months](https://www.quora.com/How-was-the-Xerox-Alto-done-in-only-3-months/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=453616882&comment_type=2)

*2025-01-23*

This is the most interesting comment I’ve gotten in quite a while … and it deals partly with two rather different computing cultures.

The simplest version of the story I can come up with, goes back a few years to the ARPA-IPTO project at UC Berkeley “Project Genie” to do an advanced extremely efficient and safe OS (adding memory management etc) for the SDS-930). The chief architect was Butler Lampson, and many later luminaries contributed important parts to both the HW and SW of this system.

This turned out very successfully — to the point that Bob Taylor of ARPA-IPTO “forced” SDS to turn it into a commercial project (so he could buy more of them for the various ARPA projects in the late 60s). BBN got one, Engelbart’s NLS project got one, etc.

The cleanliness and efficiency of the design allowed Engelbart’s “Mother Of All Demos” to be successfully done in Dec 1968. The first ARPAnet transmissions were done about a year later, and the 940 with the Genie OS started to become a platform of choice.

The machine was getting too slow so BBN decided to do to the DEC PDP-10 what Berkeley had done to the SDS-930, and this resulted in a memory mapped machine with an OS modeled after Project Genie — it was called “Tenex”. This then became the platform of choice for the ARPA and ARPAnet linked research community.

Parc viewed itself as an extension of this community — just funded by Xerox not with the dwindling funds from DoD. So we wanted a PDP-10.

And here are a few wonderful ironies. In the late 60s Xerox bought SDS more or less sight unseen, mostly on the reputation of the SDS-940 (which as I mentioned had not been designed by SDS but by the Berkeley computer folks).

This turned SDS into XDS, who were now deep competitors with DEC, especially the PDP-10.

Parc now had Butler Lampson and many high level Berkeley folks, and we were part of the ARPAnetwork and needed a PDP-10. But Xerox would not allow Parc to buy one, and offered an SDS Sigma 7 instead.

This precipitated a three phase meeting at Parc.

(1) The first phase idea was just to quit.

(2) The second was to consider writing a decent operating system for the Sigma 7 (and we did have Butler after all.) However, the group consensis was that it would realistically take about 3 years to do a good/reliable OS that could support a large number of users.

(3) But Parc people were also skilled at building computers from scratch, so the idea of making a machine that could emulate a PDP-10 and run the already existing Tenex was extensively discussed.

(a) we could probably do this in under a year (we also had Chuck Thacker, Ed Fiala, Ed McCreight, etc.).

(b) we could test the new Intel 1103 dynamic RAM chips (so would not have to mess with core memory).

(c) we could microcode the machine so it could also run other instruction sets (e.g. a custom LISP virtual machine).

(d) it could run faster than a PDP-10

(e) it could be made much *much* more reliable (and that became a big motivation).

In the end, we decided on option 3 to make the Multiple Access Xerox Computer (MAXC) a pun on the name Max Palevsky who had sold SDS to Xerox …

All worked out. MAXC was made in less than a year, and ran for years without crashing. The later Alto personal computer was basically made from a MAXC memory module plus a fast microcoded inner machine.

And, with all due respect to Ken Thompson, a much much better choice for a lab computer to do Unix on would have been the Data General Nova — especially the 800 — it had the most bang for the buck, and was much easier to program than the PDP-11 …

---

## [What programming language does Alan Kay consider to be the most advanced](https://www.quora.com/What-programming-language-does-Alan-Kay-consider-to-be-the-most-advanced/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=446248943&comment_type=2)

*2024-12-09*

I started as a professional programmer in the Air Force ca 1961, and — as with most programmers of that time — wrote most of my programs in one kind of assembly code or another for a variety of machines.

Many of the systems written in assembler during those days would live up to your criteria of “stable, reliable, and efficient”. A really interesting complex system done in low level was the OS and the mission programs for the Apollo Guidance Computer — there was never a bug that happened in this code over all the missions.

A really great fail-safe OS written in assembler in the 60s was that for the SDS-940 (originally done at UC Berkeley).

Quite a few stable systems have been done in BCPL, the ancestor of C, and as you say, in C itself (the Mars example required a careful subset and considerable extra effort and technique to be successfully stable). The Linux kernel was also very carefully written, etc.

In sum: houses of cards can be made when done very carefully. I don’t disagree.

But my point was quite different. And I think/hope I made it clear enough in the last part of my answer.

## [What are your thoughts on a future where code is represented as a structured model rather than text Do you think that AI powered coding assistants benefit from that](https://www.quora.com/What-are-your-thoughts-on-a-future-where-code-is-represented-as-a-structured-model-rather-than-text-Do-you-think-that-AI-powered-coding-assistants-benefit-from-that/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=439741318&comment_type=2)

*2024-11-01*

If I’m presented with the word

lāʻau

I only recognize some of the constituant “markings” but can’t interpret the whole into what it “means”.

Similarly, if presented with the markings

पेड़

I don’t recognize the constituants or the “meaning” of the whole.

But if presented with the word

tree

I can hold forth for a while trying to explain what it stands for to another English speaker.

In fact, all three of the markings stand for the same meaning in three different languages (the first two are Hawaiian and Hindi).

What we are interested in here is not how humans can turn markings (data) into meanings, but whether machines can (and if so, how?).

At a low-level, I think we can say that “a compiler that does its job turns codes written by humans into codes — preserving meaning and intent — that hardware can interpret and carry out”.

A more interesting question has to do with the extents that machines can now — and could in the future — understand intents that are larger in scope: to include the human worlds of goals and processes.

I think this is philosophically possible, but not currently comprehensive enough to be either safe or interesting.

## [How did Alan Kay first get started with computing](https://www.quora.com/How-did-Alan-Kay-first-get-started-with-computing/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=434770783&comment_type=2)

*2024-10-02*

Hi Ian

I was very happy to read the link you shared above about BALGOL and Bob Barton. I remember writing a few test programs in BALGOL and running them — ca ‘62/’63 — on the BALGOL compiler on the B220 at Air Training Command at Randolf Field AFB in San Antonio Texas.

Part of the lure was the most beautiful manual anyone had ever seen for a programming language in those days — with wonderful fonts, etc. and layout. I vaguely recall remembering the details behind this manual but this has now faded.

The compiler was small and fast with clever back-chaining for “fixups” of various forward references (Wirth used this same technique in Euler a few years later).

One possible overlap between the 205/220/5000 was that the 205/220 machines and the B5000S was that the “sign position” of the 205/220 was more than +/- (these were “decade machines” and used 4 bits for each position). I vaguely remember that “6” in the signposition meant “code” (we should check this) and this allowed various interesting things to be done, including protection. This could have had some bearing on the B5000 “tag bit” (although I also vague recall that the Bendix G-20 that Dave Evans had worked on at Bendix also had a tag bit …).

## [What does Alan Kay suggest here Computing is not about clocks its about biology How come the essence of computing is more about the way living organisms evolve](https://www.quora.com/What-does-Alan-Kay-suggest-here-Computing-is-not-about-clocks-its-about-biology-How-come-the-essence-of-computing-is-more-about-the-way-living-organisms-evolve/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=433960982&comment_type=2)

*2024-09-27*

The “philosophy” here is a notion — fascination — that goes at least back to the Pythagorean Greeks — about “being able to build everything from just one kind of thing”. (I think these days, one could write an interesting essay on the philosophical relationships to the fields of Quantum Field Theory to Liebizian monads).

For Smalltalk, the connection was the recognition that communicating objects could simulate any computationl entity at any scale (Liebniz had the monads not communicate, but achieve their harmony via God — Smalltalk had to also do the work of sustaining communications (but did do all of this in terms of objects).

The larger idea of everything from just one thing in computing goes back at least to Charles Sanders Pierce in the 1900s when he showed that all the logical relationships could be created just by combinations of “not-both” (NAND), so that any computer can be made from just this one kind of element. Smalltalk was an attempt to do this for VHLL programming, etc.

## [What are the Seven Wonders of computer science](https://www.quora.com/What-are-the-Seven-Wonders-of-computer-science/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=427643869&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-23*

This is a good comment question.

The original question asked for “Wonders”, and I didn’t include either RDBs or OOP because I didn’t think they were were/are wonderful enough. Another commenter wanted “Unix”, which I omitted for also not nearly being “Wonderful” enough.

And RDB even less than OOP because the center notion was of “data” rather than “ideas” or “processes” — for example, you can query an RDB for a person’s birthdate, but not their *age* because the latter is an ongoing process and doesn’t fit in the basically “unit record” structure of RDBs. In an OOPB what is defined and stored are “ideas”, so you can ask an object representing a person not just for “date of birth” but also for “age”.

I mentioned Ivan Sutherland’s Sketchpad as a Wonder, not just because of what it does, but how it does it — for example, it was one of the earliest interesting artifacts built with OOP ideas, and its internal organization transcended both RDB and OOP in scope and dynamics.

---

## [How might Alan Kay elaborate on You could think of it Val Shorres Meta II as an active interface language on objects](https://www.quora.com/How-might-Alan-Kay-elaborate-on-You-could-think-of-it-Val-Shorres-Meta-II-as-an-active-interface-language-on-objects/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=426774492&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-18*

That is a good statement of the basic idea: essentially “publish and subscribe” done at large scale.

Some of the early good ideas were in “LINDA” by Gelernter. A very workable and efficient distributed use of “P&S” is the center of Bret Victor’s “Dynamicland” and its language “Realtalk”.

A good question — for which I don’t currently have a good answer — is how to deal with the pragmatic and semantic problems of vocabulary as the scale expands (names are essentially local agreements, so we’d like to transition to descriptions as scale increases).

One observation is that a universal programming language is easier — smaller, etc. — to distribute than complex file formats (c.f. Postscript).

This could/should(?) be the case for universal bases for specs.

The architectural issues and possibilities are extremely interesting.

## [Why didnt Doug Engelbarts chording keyset become a standard interface like the mouse did It requires more training but would have a dramatic impact on user performance](https://www.quora.com/Why-didnt-Doug-Engelbarts-chording-keyset-become-a-standard-interface-like-the-mouse-did-It-requires-more-training-but-would-have-a-dramatic-impact-on-user-performance/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=426120958&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-14*

One mental exercise would be to think of “really optimum” UIs that only a few people might take the time to learn. For example, stenotypists in court can type as fast as multiple people can speak — stenotypers can type up to about 300 words per minute, which is twice as fast as normal fast speaking (about 150 wpm). Most of these schemes could be adapted to an iPhone held with two hands and a special surface.

Typical iPhone typing is about 20–40 wpm — regular keyboard typing is about 35 to 80 wpm.

And there is also dictation to be considered. A recent Stanford study indicates that speech-to-text is about 3 times faster than typing on an iPhone (note that every scheme — including dictation — requires some training).

## [What happened to the children exposed to Smalltalk at an early age in Xerox PARC back in the 1970s 1980s Did some of them grow to become great programmers scientists Do they use Smalltalk to this day Or was there](https://www.quora.com/What-happened-to-the-children-exposed-to-Smalltalk-at-an-early-age-in-Xerox-PARC-back-in-the-1970s-1980s-Did-some-of-them-grow-to-become-great-programmers-scientists-Do-they-use-Smalltalk-to-this-day-Or-was-there/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=425474978&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-11*

Hi Jamie

The Open School was such a wonderful school that my initial advice to our research team was from the Hippocratic Oath “Above all do no harm”. It was already a great experience for children before we showed up.

I thought we were able to add a few good things — a lot of which were simply funding of additional good processes brought in from the outside. One of the best was to convince Doreen Nelson to adapt her “City Building Curriculum” for 3rd graders — this eventually got done really well.

Your contributions helped our understanding of “kids and computers” a lot, and the several “Playground” systems were interesting and worthwhile experiments.

One the whole, I think we learned more from being in the whole school process for many years, than (perhaps) the school learned from us. (But, I do think that we indeed “did no harm”!)

## [Why did Xerox let Apple Steve Jobs come in and study their technology](https://www.quora.com/Why-did-Xerox-let-Apple-Steve-Jobs-come-in-and-study-their-technology/answer/Mickey-Menkin?comment_id=425302260&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-10*

Here is a partial answer (and I’ve made other related answers).

[Why was there a mismatch between Xerox management and PARC?](https://www.quora.com/Why-was-there-a-mismatch-between-Xerox-management-and-PARC)

Two important parts of the answer to the question in your comment are (a) Parc was explicitly set up as a long range research and invention lab, not a product development division, (b) the Xerox management who set up Parc changed a number of times, and was very different after Parc had made a number of significant inventions (c) Xerox got in trouble in its copier business in the 1976 on period (competion from Japan and Kodak) and was distracted away from other kinds of products, (d) Xerox had a lot of internal strife between P & L divisions. Etc.

One of the “too bad”s was that Parc’s process was to engineer and make “enough of an invention to serve 100 people”, (so: there had to be at least 100 Altos, etc. — we wound up making almost 2000 thousand). This meant that research examples were a lot more engineered than mere demos, and many of them could have been put in the market with much less productization effort than most research prototypes.

A large (e) for the above was that Parc basically invented an entire industry, that was really too large for one company. Most companies, especially Xerox, did not know what to do with that large a set of inventions (so that all could benefit).

---

## [How valuable is it to apply concepts like the Curry Howard correspondence proofs as programs and inductive proofs to the development of dynamic complex systems considering their correlation with type systems](https://www.quora.com/How-valuable-is-it-to-apply-concepts-like-the-Curry-Howard-correspondence-proofs-as-programs-and-inductive-proofs-to-the-development-of-dynamic-complex-systems-considering-their-correlation-with-type-systems/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=424797700&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-07*

Yes, I was referring to the need here of often having to write code *and* a constraint for it, when the constraint itself should be able to generate the underlying code (note that this would be a good extension of what HLLs are supposed to do in the first place).

This brings up a very old and still pertinent question: if the constraints really define what the program is intended to do, then why not try to generate the underlying code from the constraints? Then you would debug the constraints. And, finally, you might add “helpful hints” if you have optimizations in mind. But the *meaning* of the program is in the constraint/requirements system.

---

## [How valuable is it to apply concepts like the Curry Howard correspondence proofs as programs and inductive proofs to the development of dynamic complex systems considering their correlation with type systems](https://www.quora.com/How-valuable-is-it-to-apply-concepts-like-the-Curry-Howard-correspondence-proofs-as-programs-and-inductive-proofs-to-the-development-of-dynamic-complex-systems-considering-their-correlation-with-type-systems/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=424793326&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-07*

There is obviously a lot of good work here, and I’m sure a lot was learned.

Much of my reaction is “there should be a better notion of programming” (implying a much better language is needed than C#) — and — psychologically, I think the pragmatic bent of most programmers would be more enticed if the constraints were cleverly part of the mainstream programming process.

In this example, a lot of the constraints could be turned directly into code, so two forms are not needed. If there is still a bug, we’ve now got to check if our constraints are bad, our code is bad, or both. This seems against the larger goals of HLL design.

## [What is the importance of Christopher Alexander s work for system software designers](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-importance-of-Christopher-Alexander-s-work-for-system-software-designers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=424251976&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-04*

Our mental limitations require two very different approaches to operate at the same time for progress (a) be able to use and improve old ideas that seem to work, and (b) be able to qualitatively get past improvement by radically changing contexts.

I’ve given many talks which will have a section about this. The “(b)” part is quite difficult for most humans, and — in addition — can cause great difficulties for the small percentage of humans who are attracted to “the new” (this is because “most ideas are mediocre down to bad”, and this produces trade-offs between “safe and known” thinking and “avant-garde” thinking).

However, it is quite interesting that qualitative turning points can be identified in history, and very often these can be identified with a few individuals who were able to have ideas that were actually extremely important and good. Just to pick one: Newton. We can also identify a few similarly important inventions: e.g. writing, and thousands of years later: printing.

A smart society will fund both incremental activities and radical inventional ones. (Usually the latter are ignored until great stress — like a war — is placed on the society.)

In my experience, funding “visions instead of goals” has been a great heuristic to eventually *find* great goals and the people to realize them.

---

## [What is the importance of Christopher Alexander s work for system software designers](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-importance-of-Christopher-Alexander-s-work-for-system-software-designers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=424238923&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-04*

I should have mentioned these four books. IMO the best part of these books are very like the best parts of NOASOF — extremely valuable to motivate one’s own pondering.

I think of them in the same vein as I do Julian Jaynes’ “The origin of consciousness in the breakdown of the bicameral mind” — i.e. probably “not ‘true’ “, but invaluable for provoking thought.

My advice here is to take the forms of these thought provoking books and — instead of following the contents — make up new principles to see how the idea space can be covered (and it’s certainly OK to use some from the ideas of others).

My favorite modern “computery thinker” is Bret Victor — partly for his ideas per se, but also that he has been able to use what was valuable from the past, yet to recast the past in the light of where we are right now, and should be probing into the future. I don’t think that Bret has missed anything (or much) that is valuable, but what he does is still “New” (rather than “News”).

---

## [Do you think that Americans would take the jobs left vacant if all illegal immigrants were to return to their countries](https://www.quora.com/Do-you-think-that-Americans-would-take-the-jobs-left-vacant-if-all-illegal-immigrants-were-to-return-to-their-countries/answer/Scot-Morgan?comment_id=424125236&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-03*

In summers, in the 1950s, I did the front end of this as a teenager, and completely agree with the OP’s bottom line: “I would not do this again, for any amount of money”. (And the pay back then was abysmally small.)

It was in Massachusetts, was brutal work, and no migrants were involved. The tobacco was of lower quality, and was aimed at cigar wrappers. The commenter Anne Brown mentions tobacco farms in Norther Conn — the ones I worked on were similar. The oozing of cut tobacco is quite acidic, and I’ll never forget it.

My family thought it “would build character” for me to work on farms during the summers. I found pretty much all farm work to be backbreaking and dispiriting. But this was what people in that mostly agrarian area still wound up having to do.

It certainly convinced me that this life was not a path I wanted to follow.

In summers, in the 1950s, I did the front end of this as a teenager, and completely agree with your bottom line: “I would not do this again, for any amount of money”. (And the pay back then was abysmally small.)

It was in Massachusetts, was brutal work, and no migrants were involved. The tobacco was of lower quality, and was aimed at cigar wrappers. The commenter Anne Brown mentions tobacco farms in Norther Conn — the ones I worked on were similar.

My family thought it “would build character” for me to work on farms during the summers. I found pretty much all farm work to be backbreaking and dispiriting. But this was what people in that mostly agrarian area still wound up having to do.

It certainly convinced me that this life was not a path I wanted to follow.

---

## [How do I convince my math teacher that 2 is not irrational I have proof for it that I came up with but he wouldnt take a look at it](https://www.quora.com/How-do-I-convince-my-math-teacher-that-2-is-not-irrational-I-have-proof-for-it-that-I-came-up-with-but-he-wouldnt-take-a-look-at-it/answer/Aaron-Brown-165?comment_id=424114163&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-03*

I liked this answer very much. A very large point and purpose of Mathematics is that it can be explained and results obtained with generally much more consistency and accuracy than most systems of thought.

Learning and believing a claim of someone in mathematics is “not maths”, nor is insisting that one believes — the maths teacher should have done much more to help this student.

People are often put off by the most used proof, which is by contradiction, and these kinds of arguments can seem “tricky”. Bertrand Russell pointed out in an essay — which included this example — that a simpler larger proof is often easier to follow.

For example, if you look at rational numbers as ratios of integers, and the integers as having prime factors, then a good question for students to think about is: what will be the case for squares of these, and when will a square of a rational number be an integer?

## [What is Alan Kays perspective on the obsession with learning memory management and other low level aspects when it comes to programming education](https://www.quora.com/What-is-Alan-Kays-perspective-on-the-obsession-with-learning-memory-management-and-other-low-level-aspects-when-it-comes-to-programming-education/answer/Graham-Dearsley?comment_id=423792265&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-01*

I just received and read the Teacher’s Resource Book for Fletcher Level I “Mathematics For Schools”. It is 309 pages long and quite dense in a number of dimensions (it is hard to imagine any busy teacher of 1st level children having the time or inclination to actually read it through).

It makes a lot of the “classic blunders” that most attempts in this direction in the US also do. One of many is to use adult labels for everything. Another is to rush too early into teaching “numerals” and positional notation, and arithmetic algorithms. There are much better ways to approach the gaps between “what numbers and arithmetic means” and “how to do practical arithmetic in the outside world”. (For example, an abacus is a wonderful bridge between the two worlds …).

The best ways to teach “real maths” to young children I’ve seen — in the US — have been by Mary Laycock and independently by Julia Nishijima at the Open School in LA.

A newer way to look at this — which Papert gestured at, but has never to my knowledge been fully done and tried with children — is the approach advocated by the legendary Woody Flowers at MIT.

His idea (for college ages) is to take the existence of a personal computer as a given, and to develop a mathematics based on addition (partly on the cognitive grounds that addition is one of the few almost built-ins for humans). The next concept is tricky, but children can get it via examples, is equality. The next concept is reversability.

Woody worked up an outstanding new and perhaps more useful perspective on math using these and computers to help deal with additive ideas in the large (including calculus, linear systems, sampling, etc.). I found this very interesting, and likely to be more deeply understandable for more people.

---

## [What was Alan Kays experience like working at Apple](https://www.quora.com/What-was-Alan-Kays-experience-like-working-at-Apple/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=423772802&comment_type=2)

*2024-08-01*

I’m happy to write a little (but not a lot) more. As compactly as I can: the ARPA-IPTO computer research community (which included Parc) was almost entirely aimed at generally uplifting human thought, and especially to include uplifting the combined thought of groups (e.g. see Engelbart). Papert was already doing important things with children, and he inspired me greately — this turned into the Dynabook idea, and this was one of larger ideals at Parc. The “Alto” computer was originally called the “Interim Dynabook” etc.

Steve Jobs was mainly a marketing guy, but also liked the Dynabook idea — it fit in with his and Apple’s early views as being a “Bicycle for the mind” or “Wings for the mind”, etc.

The Apple BoD mistakenly fired Steve. However, John Scully was also interested in helping children (and adults) “learn to think better than most adults”. This underwrote the large “Vivarium” project I got started at MIT and LA. I was also able to convince John to be the champion of Bill Atkinson’s “WildCard” project (later named HyperCard). As far as the outside world was concerned, this could be my most significant accomplishment at Apple.

The most significant technical issue with turning HyperCard into a browser was that its internals were not suitable (it was the design and the millions already in the user community that were the main assets). There were already a number of “HyperCard-like” systems (e.g. “SuperCard”) that had a few more useful features. The old Parc hands at Apple plus those who understood the significance of HyperCard had been bugging Apple management for years to upgrade and make HyperCard multiplatform, etc. When the WWW happened, this group renewed its push for HyperCard as the basis for Web Browser. (The pitch was (a) this is a really good model (b) there are millions of known HyperCard users to initially appeal to.)

The 90s were a time of Apple decline, so nothing happened. We were courted by the CTO of Disney — Bran Ferren — and eventually took the entire group there at the end of ‘96. We had done a version of Smalltalk (Squeak) that was free of the Xerox license, and convinced Apple to let us release it under the MIT open source license, so it could now be used by everyone. We decided to do “lapware” for Disney Online (meaning “a child on a parent’s lap in front of a constructive computer app”), and this turned into Etoys. A followon version of this done at MIT turned into Scratch.

A bottom line for most of your questions is that much of what happened from the 40s to the present day has depended on the type and quality of the funders. The DoD funders were not trying to make money *and* they wanted to uplift our species — the VCs starting in the late seventies + IBM drove much of personal computing for monetary gain. The marketing people during this time did not want to sell stuff with big learning curves. Etc.

MicroSoft + IBM PC was a huge “bottom dollar” lure.

“Going against the grain with colleagues”? I was at Parc because they wanted me there to do “whatever”, so doing Smalltalk, the GUI, children, etc. was not exactly “against the grain”, it just wasn’t what any of the other researchers were initially trying to do. The most significant people I worked with at Parc were Butler Lampson, Chuck Thacker, Dan Ingalls, Adele Goldberg, etc. The most significant outside of Parc were Seymour Papert, Marvin Minsky, Nicholas Negroponte, Jerome Bruner, Bobbie Blatt, etc.

I’m happy to accept your apology, but there’s no need to. I understand your frustration. (However, I suggest you might try a different tone when trying to find out what is going on …)

---

*2024-07-30*

The design of Wigmore was originally for the Beckstein Piano company, and they wound up with a curved vault (which is not supposed to be a good idea) and a Renaissance reflecting “shell” which had not been used in rooms for hundreds of years. This turned out fabulously well, and it is one of the favorite places for both audience and players.

They needed to refurbish in the early 2000s and everyone worried about possible damage to the incredible acoustics. This led to one of the first really accurate impulse response acoustic models.

The restulting report was able to explain several of the reasons/causes of the great sound for both the audience and performers.

One of the desired changes was more rake in the stalls. The acoustic model was able to warn when too much rake would damage the sound. This led to a smaller rake than planned + what everyone thought was the original great sound.

This sound is wonderfully “golden” for every kind of chamber music, even in a full hall. It is particularly shockingly good for weak instruments (like guitars, and especially lutes).

## [Does abstraction in computer science use mathematics as a basis](https://www.quora.com/Does-abstraction-in-computer-science-use-mathematics-as-a-basis/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=423362553&comment_type=2)

*2024-07-30*

## [Does abstraction in computer science use mathematics as a basis](https://www.quora.com/Does-abstraction-in-computer-science-use-mathematics-as-a-basis/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=423362115&comment_type=2)

*2024-07-30*

This is a good thoughtful comment.

To start with the end of it, the “s” in mathematics is indeed the sign that it is a plural (this is carriend through in UK and Europe for the shorthand “maths”). So anyone can define any system for relating and reasoning in some consistent way.

Most of the time when “maths” are used and discussed, the users agree on “which kind” (and this is most often the large body of knowledge and notions of argument developed in Europe over many centuries).

Even within this body of knowledge there are different notions of “consistency” — the most used is to have some definition of “true” and to do things in a way that preserves this.

And, yes, everything is up for grabs, you are free to not only define what you mean by “relationship” and “object” and “inference” and “truth”, but you are free to define any kind of system you want to think in terms of (you can see why having a notion of “consistency” and “propagating ‘truth’ “ can help clear up some of the fog and noise all humans are born with).

On the other hand — back to Einstein — beware of definitions! And thinking you are inferring about the real world. For example, you are free to define certain classes of humans as “vermin” (this was done in the 30s), and then you can reason logically — but completely inhumanely — that this class of humans therefore should be exterminated. (This is why I think mathematics is really dangerous without science to tame it. Reasoning just with logic alone can come to disastrous conclusions.)

---

## [What is Alan Kays perspective on the obsession with learning memory management and other low level aspects when it comes to programming education](https://www.quora.com/What-is-Alan-Kays-perspective-on-the-obsession-with-learning-memory-management-and-other-low-level-aspects-when-it-comes-to-programming-education/answer/Graham-Dearsley?comment_id=423357323&comment_type=2)

*2024-07-30*

I quite agree. One of my favorite Marvin Minsky’s sayings about New Math was “The trouble with New Math is that you have to understand it every time you use it!”.

On the other hand if whatever “new math” they pushed on you failed to deal with the concept of inverses early on, it was not a good approach to any kind of real understanding of classical maths.

And most musicians would agree also. But I think all of us would advocate some sort of “play first, understand soon” kind of approach, because we humans are so pragmatic about doing things that we tend to ignore what is actually needed be be understood over the long haul.

The unusual elementary school music curriculum in the Hadley Mass public schools was really great at both parts of this for young children.

I think a modified N&S approach would be felt as “real play” if (a) done well, and (b) if the learners didn’t have huge inflexible goals about wanting to get programming jobs, via C or C++, etc. (I think these are OK to learn *after* one has enough perspective to criticize them and only use them for small pragmatic reasons — the problem is that they become a “way of life” and that makes real thinking about computing really difficult.)

---

## [Does abstraction in computer science use mathematics as a basis](https://www.quora.com/Does-abstraction-in-computer-science-use-mathematics-as-a-basis/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=423348268&comment_type=2)

*2024-07-30*

Another way of looking at this is via a nice reminder given by Einstein in 1921 to an audience of physicists: *" ... as far as the propositions of mathematics refer to reality, they are not certain; and as far as they are certain, they do not refer to reality."*

 *Geometry and Experience, Lecture before the Prussian Academy of Sciences, January 27, 1921*

This is tricky and good. He could have pointed out that since our brain/minds only contain beliefs that are by default detached from reality, we are in a pickle when we try to think about our actual situations.

Mathematics is a kind of intermediary. It is “in our heads”, where good conclusions are difficult. The idea is that there are *methods* and *definitions* that can be followed to allow more consistency than ordinary thinking. For example, you can come up with a definition for “truth” and the “propagation of ‘truth’ in a chain of arguments” that can be both useful and satisfying.

If you want to map these conclusions back out into the world, you need to be very wary (for the reason Einstein gave).

I’ve often gotten people to think about this map as a example and a metaphor of our essential dilemma.

![](https://qph.cf2.quoracdn.net/main-qimg-f7a394ff775f1b18ce17f7f97317df76)

It is a good way to think about the best that can be inside our heads. We can “do math” using this map, and get consistent deductions. If we try to do *science* with this map, we will wind up with “on the planet Earth, we have never encountered any place at all like this”.

This should help you to see that *science* is a kind of “negotiation” between what’s in our heads and “what is ‘out there’ ?”. In the terms we’ve been using, it is a “relationship” between representations and phenomena (or perhaps “experiments”).

I hope that helps.

.

---

## [Does abstraction in computer science use mathematics as a basis](https://www.quora.com/Does-abstraction-in-computer-science-use-mathematics-as-a-basis/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=423339729&comment_type=2)

*2024-07-30*

In part because I got a degree in pure math in my mispent youth, much of how I think of the term “relation” is a somewhat looser version of how maths define it (I got some good enough definitions by typing “mathematical relation” into Google). For example, there are set oriented ways of dealing with pairs of elements, there are systems ways (draw an arrow between two things and give it a name, and try for a definition, etc.). See my aphorism from John von Neumann above.

For “idea”, I again went to Google. One definition I found was: “**any conception existing in the mind as a result of mental understanding, awareness, or activity**. a thought, conception, or notion”.

I also have a hard time thinking of “ideas” without also thinking of Plato’s notion of “Ideals” (sometimes translated as “ideas”). Plato had a “class and instance” form where the “class” was called an “Idea” or “Ideal” and was beyond human ken, but our species is able to perceive the instances.

This was too categorical for Aristotle, who advocated that categories were created by humans via enumerating their elements (and thus you could make a new category from an existing one simply by forcing anything you wanted into any set).

This is uglier than Plato’s pristine notion, but sometimes useful (the pure categories are ultimately very hard to deal with, and ultimately make it hard to think).

I’ve pointed out in talks that “Most ideas are mediocre down to bad”, so one has to adopt heuristics to deal with thinking. For example, because of the above you both (a) need to have lots of ideas, and (b) need to have some way of discarding most of them in a way that allows new thinking and (c) allows some of the old seemingly bad ones to be recalled when needed.

For me this was a skill I had to learn to do much better than what nature gave me at birth.

---

## [What is Alan Kays perspective on the obsession with learning memory management and other low level aspects when it comes to programming education](https://www.quora.com/What-is-Alan-Kays-perspective-on-the-obsession-with-learning-memory-management-and-other-low-level-aspects-when-it-comes-to-programming-education/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=423336800&comment_type=2)

*2024-07-30*

I had to check this, even though I had written a few JOVIAL programs and knew Jules Schwarz back in the day (“JOVIAL” stands for “Jules’ Own Version <of the> International Algebraic Language” — “IAL” was the original name and predecessor (later called “Algol 58”) of Algol-60). Jules was “highly memorable”!

The version I knew was documented really well here [A specification of JOVIAL | Communications of the ACM](https://doi.org/10.1145/763973.763978) and doesn’t say anything about units (and I don’t remember any).

The documentation mentions a version for the Q-32 (an enormous followon to the SAGE Q-7s) and it was an incident of Licklider writing a program in JOVIAL for this computer and wanting to use a library function he knew existed at MIT in FORTRAN via the telephone line between the two different computers that got him to say “Our main problem with our intergalactic network in the future will be communicating with aliens”!

An interesting thing about JOVIAL is that it was done so well — and loved by several of the armed forces — that it stayed extremely viable, and was stardardized better than most languages — and so, hung around quite prominently for many years.

It is possible that units might have been added to JOVIAL after the brief brush with it I had. The Wikipedia article mentions nothing about units.

## [How would Alan Kay go about designing a systems design course What contents should be covered and what knowledge or skills should one gain Which exemplary projects papers should be included to facilitate](https://www.quora.com/How-would-Alan-Kay-go-about-designing-a-systems-design-course-What-contents-should-be-covered-and-what-knowledge-or-skills-should-one-gain-Which-exemplary-projects-papers-should-be-included-to-facilitate/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=423260043&comment_type=2)

*2024-07-29*

Most “system thinkers” have a number of perspectives they take on them, but a good place to start is with “what does it mean to send a message and to receive a message?”

A simple diagram of this could have a blob for the sender (S), an arrow to show where the message should go, and another blob (could be the same blob) as the receiver (R). There is an implicit time duration to move the message from S->R.

It’s important to see how many things can be mapped into that abstraction. For example: meshing gear teeth, biological structures, human and animal cultures, quarks, atoms, galaxies, and all manner of computer organizations from gates to the Internet.

The delays and distribution of knowledge produces many non-linearities, only some of which are helpful

One bottom line is that we generally want the system to be both non-linear (we want the system to not just do more than the parts but usually to be quite different than the parts (e.g. consider the parts of a spoked wheel and then making a wheel from them)).

The other bottom line is that we want to be able to define “stabilities” for the systems and have these obeyed. (This gets to the fun of having to deal with the errors and noise in the mechanisms you are using to deal with the errors and noise in your original system. This very large idea was thought about very deeply by Shannon in the 40s and 50s. Most of our modern technologies — and life itself — depend on some version of these ideas.

Good system design takes as many of these factors into account (and great ones will include what seems to be outside the system, but which is actually part of the environment of the system, into account as well).

---

## [What is Alan Kays perspective on the obsession with learning memory management and other low level aspects when it comes to programming education](https://www.quora.com/What-is-Alan-Kays-perspective-on-the-obsession-with-learning-memory-management-and-other-low-level-aspects-when-it-comes-to-programming-education/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=423206889&comment_type=2)

*2024-07-29*

A few years ago I was invited to do a talk on “Software Engineering”, and did quite a bit of prep to find out the current states of art (this is generally still quite low or nonexistent, with very few exceptions).

I found and spent a fair amount of time talking to Gerard Holzman [Gerard J. Holzmann - Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerard_J._Holzmann) who had been lured away from Bell Labs by NASA to help debug/”integritize” the code for the “Curiosity Rover”. Here’s one of the slides I made:

![](https://qph.cf2.quoracdn.net/main-qimg-ca4b2d7169f57d1c5db3396f46a0d1ef)

NASA uses an unprotected subset of C and threads rather than processes. Gerard came in after the project (because worries started). The 3.8 millions lines of threaded C he debugged and verified is still running: wikpedia says: *The rover is still operational, and as of 29 July 2024, Curiosity has been active on Mars for 4258 **[sols](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sol_(day_on_Mars))** (4375 **[total days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Days)**; 11 years, 358 days) since its landing (see **[current status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Timeline_of_Mars_Science_Laboratory#Current_status)**).*

This was terrific work! However, much of the work had to be done because of NASA not having made a language that was inherently made to create models as part of its properties, and to automatically check them.

Needless to say, Gerard has been well recognized in the field for his deep and groundbreaking work.

## [What is Alan Kays perspective on the obsession with learning memory management and other low level aspects when it comes to programming education](https://www.quora.com/What-is-Alan-Kays-perspective-on-the-obsession-with-learning-memory-management-and-other-low-level-aspects-when-it-comes-to-programming-education/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=423171937&comment_type=2)

*2024-07-29*

Check out [How NASA Lost a Spacecraft From a Metric Math Mistake | SimScale](https://www.simscale.com/blog/nasa-mars-climate-orbiter-metric/)

This should be interesting to anyone who has programmed even a little. Many languages have a notion of “type” (usually meaning that you can constrain a variable to a particular “type” of value, for example to be an integer, floating point number, a text string, etc.).

A very interesting side note is that there are subgroups who are very religious about this idea, but do not push for a much deeper concept of “types”. For example, NASA generally programs in a subset of C — which does have typed numbers for magnitudes, but not “dimensional numbers” (e.g. “feet, inches, meters, pounds, kilos, etc.”)

Both of these descriptions are mandatory for science and engineering use of numbers, but there have been very few programming languages in history that have done the work to enlarge “types” to actually be useful except in mostly trivial cases.

And NASA, which depends on science and math, not just for planning but for success in missions (and not killing people in the process), and which has the resources to create a much better and safer programming language for their missions, completely blew this.

(It’s worth poking into why … and in the meantime, it’s worth taking a look into what the code for the Mars rovers actually looks like, and what they are forced to do to debug it …)

## [What does Alan Kay think of Ted Nelsons criticism of Xerox PARC Taking away the right to program by hiding everything behind the PUI and making computers into paper simulators with single collumn text](https://www.quora.com/What-does-Alan-Kay-think-of-Ted-Nelsons-criticism-of-Xerox-PARC-Taking-away-the-right-to-program-by-hiding-everything-behind-the-PUI-and-making-computers-into-paper-simulators-with-single-collumn-text/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=417431878&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-28*

Good and interesting question. Part of the answer is that Smalltalk-78 extended “down to the bottom” and “below” (its virtual machine definition was in microcode). It was almost completely written in itself (the later Squeak Smalltalk was completely written in itself and carried its bootstrapping tools with it). All the Smalltalks were made completely from objects.

This means that the management and control of space and time and I/O, etc., were done by Smalltlalk objects. What we called the “image” was a snapshot of all the objects in a stable state (this is was what was in the disk file that got recovered).

There was also a model of a file system that could be used to deal with any external files (Smalltalk itself stored all of its state dynamically as related objects in the image).

One way to think about this is that any more complete base for a dynamic programming language will be doing what OSs used to do — and the overall services will generally be more complete and extensible. (We didn’t think of Smalltalk as a “programming language” but as a “system development system”.)

Another part of the answer is to allow errors but not crashes. The extent to which this was done successfully was remarkable — mostly by Dan Ingalls, who should have shared the Turing Award for this. Part of the integrity of the system was because the debugger was also written in Smalltalk, and this — plus that we thought all systems should be like the Ethernet: never crashing, etc. — motivated the systems design.

In practice, any error would cause the kernel to set aside the whole process that contained the error, and give the user the option to set it aside and continue, or to use the debugger to fix and restart the process. We once found over 1500 suspended processes in a system that was “working quite well”!

This way of looking things, along with Ethernet, was part of the realization that e.g. the Internet was going to have to be eternal — it would have to work even with errors (and thus all of these systems would be very like the idea of “error detection and correction” in a channel that Shannon and Hamming had got people thinking about regarding lower level messaging and representing).

---

## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=416232965&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-21*

Hi George

For your first paragraph, you really should take the time to look carefully at CYC — it is qualitatively beyond your generalizations. You are right that it “requires a tremendous amount of work to encode both information and rules” — and that is what the CYC researchers did over more than 30 years — and they went far beyond “data” and “rules”.

The problem with ML and LLMs is that they aren’t really semantic — and can’t get far enough with correlations, no matter how many.

Your last paragraph has some of the gist of my suggestion, but the idea is to turn meanings about intentions in human understandable forms into the safe same meanings in computer runnable forms. The mechanism would not really be a “rules engine” as such are thought of today.

I don’t think this has been done yet. I do think it is a very hard project — but I think it needs to be done and should be done.

## [Why did Alan Kay once say GNU Linux was a budget of bad ideas What is generally wrong with GNU Linux and Unix like OSes](https://www.quora.com/Why-did-Alan-Kay-once-say-GNU-Linux-was-a-budget-of-bad-ideas-What-is-generally-wrong-with-GNU-Linux-and-Unix-like-OSes/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=416156118&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-21*

I mentioned caching, and mentioned Gerry Popek’s LOCUS system as an example of a Unix like “migrating process” system that was tried and **DID** work well. I also mentioned Dave Reed’s 1978 thesis, which was implemented — by Dave Smith and others — as various versions of the Croquet system, which also does work well across slow large area networks such as the Internet.

It is usually possible to find examples that don’t work well for many ideas — but it’s more interesting when there do exist examples which do work well. For this particular question, LOCUS is an extremely good example for how a Unix like system can be successfully distributed and dynamically configured. (I also picked it because I didn’t work on it, and can thus legitimately praise it to the skies!).

## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=415957210&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-20*

Hi George

Good comment! It’s fun (and sobering) to realize that the “formulas” in FORmula TRANslation in 1956 were considered (and are) declarative. And Sketchpad’s end-user programming was entirely in terms of “relationships which much be made to be true”.

CYC was a very large and comprehensive attempt to achieve a very hard vision/goal: *“a general simulation framework that can simulate end effects and results for all applications or systems, regardless of the domain, and without specializing the programming model”.*

And you are quite right about the need for a lot of domain “knowledge and lore” — because it is difficult “*to establish meaningful communication and deliver meaning without a lot of domain knowledge and a lot of context*”. That was one of the main realizations and aims of CYC.

Note that the current poorly thought through LLMs these days try to mine the Internet for this needed extra knowledge and context. This is a very bad idea because there is so much crud, and what’s good is usually not in a usable form.

However, e.g. I have a monumental textbook tome about “Dynamics and Statics” which is a thousand plus pages for learners, and for which this and even more has been adapted to be part of the CAD and SIM tools for many kinds of materials and structural engineering.

People from my research community and generation have been advocating for real “knowledge based and reasoning tools” for many decades now. What we’ve seen is most of the field going in the oppostite direction.

So I think you have a good picture of the problems — it could be widened a bit. We mainly differ in my thinking that it has to be done regardless (that is part of the general attitude about difficult problems in the 60s and 70s: i.e. do them anyway!).

---

## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=415862780&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-19*

The word “model” is used in too many contexts — but I still like to use it ...

I meant “semantic model” **in such a form that it could give rise to a running simulation of the desired system** — maybe requiring a supercomputer.

UML is not at all in this class.

As I mentioned in my answer, I think that runnable debuggable human understandable transformable “requirements” are a critical next stage.

The problem with separate “code verifiers” is that they can be complex statements themselves in terms very different from the “code”.

This “two languge problem” has been one of the main difficulties with this approach for the last 60 years or so (this is usually actually at least a “three language problem” because neither the “coding language” nor the “verification language” are of much use as a “human understanding language” so non-strict English gets included as well.)

I think going for a much higher level language with real semantics of intention that can also be used by human decision makers is a much better way to go.

## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=415860166&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-19*

This got me curious … I now live in London, so I checked to see if there is “legislation over word usage”.

I found this:

[https://www.gmc-uk.org/registration-and-licensing/the-medical-register/a-guide-to-the-medical-register/unregistered-medical-practice](https://www.gmc-uk.org/registration-and-licensing/the-medical-register/a-guide-to-the-medical-register/unregistered-medical-practice)

Go to this page and go to the section:

***“Falsely using protected titles***

*It is illegal to falsely use the protected titles below:*

*doctor of medicine*

*general practitioner (GP)*

*surgeon*

*physician*

*licentiate in medicine and surgery*

*bachelor of medicine*

*apothecary, or*

*any name, title, or description implying that they are registered with us.”*

They point out that “Doctor” can also apply to a PhD in something, so it is “Doctor of Medicine” that is protected.

In the UK you can call yourself an “engineer” without any certification, but you cannot call yourself e.g. a “civil engineer”, without being certified.

I think that — therefore — calling one’s self a “software engineer” should require real certification.

(I don’t think there is currently a law here about this — but a few more Post Office and NHS disasters/scandals are likely to make it happen).

## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=415405076&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-17*

A quite old but good example is given in a paper by Ron Ferguson in Byte magazine: [https://www.google.com/url?sa=t&source=web&rct=j&opi=89978449&url=http://tinlizzie.org/~awarth/prolog-ferguson81.pdf&ved=2ahUKEwiclYXzmeKGAxXkQkEAHecHANUQFnoECBEQAQ&usg=AOvVaw1gyPTBQIm23f0OlBTG7AgC](https://www.google.com/url?sa=t&source=web&rct=j&opi=89978449&url=http://tinlizzie.org/~awarth/prolog-ferguson81.pdf&ved=2ahUKEwiclYXzmeKGAxXkQkEAHecHANUQFnoECBEQAQ&usg=AOvVaw1gyPTBQIm23f0OlBTG7AgC)

He shows how you can easily make a meta-interpreter for “robot programming” in Prolog, that scrutinizes the inference process to detect and avoid plans that would violate Asimov’s 3 laws of robotics. On a larger scale, this is the kind of “finding entailments” that CYC would do.

One would think that any software operating in the real-world would be forced to submit a *plan for an action* to a plan checker before carrying out the action. But this is very rarely done.
