## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=415396639&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-17*

My answer above uses two main analogies (a) to “established engineering” as a whole, and (b) to medicine/heath/life (which could be considered as a special kind of engineering/science).

In talks, I’ve used Venn diagrams to show how “the actual work these days” is a combination of different perspectives and expertise accompanied by a comment along the lines of “these days one really has to learn all the different perspectives, though they might be most centered in one of them”. The background of the diagram itself could be considered to be “Art” in its original sense. The intersected “sweet spot” is the point of this diagram.

![](https://qph.cf2.quoracdn.net/main-qimg-500b314c50620a2aee570171792becc6)

Historically, we started with tinkering and proceeded counter-clockwise, with science in its modern sense appearing only 400–500 years ago. It’s worth noting that most computing today still most resembles “tinkering” and barely touches the other lobes.

I feel sympathetic to your frustrations. If we replace “Software” with “Civil”, or look at established Engineering as a whole, I think we will find versions of most of your suggestions.

I think the field can do even better in a variety of dimensions. For example, “engineering” is still finding that mathematics and especially science are critical parts that can’t be approximated with the pragmatic cookbook methodologies of the past. The ability to do not just CAD, but CAD/SIM, is one the greatest gifts brought to established engineering by computing, science, and mathematics.

Similarly, the possiblities for CAD/SIM in software quite change the whole development field — but, sadly and ironically — computing is lagging in developing tools along these lines for software production (even as the other engineering fields have developed qualitatively powerful changes in how they use computers to design and test). This is a “cobbler’s children have no shoes” and “physician heal thyself” bizarre situation.

So I would mainly point out that part of the SE problem is “not something good is broken and needs to be fixed”, but we have to add “introduce more new good” as a critical process (or we will wind up missing the main points about why things are not good.

---

## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=415230306&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-16*

You rightly say: *“But it can be very difficult to make precise statements about such properties as intent” *— this is one of the reasons why I’d try to focus on how to better express *intent*, how to verify the meaning(s), how to turn *intent* into both (a) actions, and (b) pragmatic actions. Etc.

Over the years, a pretty good heuristic has been to go after the really difficult central problems to see if a solution within the essense can be turned into many solutions of particulars. This is not easy, but it has led to a number of important new ways to approach making good software.

This is also why I am fascinated by “constitutions” (especially when compared to case law). I also like the UN’s “Universal Bill of Rights” as an excellent attempt at expressing *intent* in a compact usable form. Most systems have so many degrees of freedom that something like a larger *intent* is needed for dealing with them.

---

## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=415224827&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-16*

My quick reaction to this is “kind of like CYC”, “kind of like ART”, etc., but moving to “slow thinking”, I would start by trying to find and deeply criticize “systems that seemed to point the way”.

For example, one start would be to take the visions, ideals and ideas behind CYC, and try to create something new whose initial examples had to do with actively and accurately dynamically representing the meanings and actions of significant programs.

One one hand, this would be following in the footsteps of the progression of HLL inventions that started in the 50s and seemed to run out of steam — as far as level of expression at least — in the 80s. One of the big problems in designing these is that the semantics of the several languages involved — from the human facing ones to the machine facing ones — have to cohere. At the next level, are the human facing languages (a) expressive enough (b) debuggable (i.e. are they saying and actually doing what is intended?

The latter notion of accurately capturing the actual **“intent”** is central here.

Given where we are today, I think we should make one of the main criteria, the ability to automatically simulate the intent. Even this brings up real problems (and long before the bad idea of trying to use LLMs is brought up).

---

## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=415222890&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-16*

A key question. First, I don’t think there is *no discipline* — this is why I said in the answer: “There is also the problem that only parts of “Software Engineering” resemble “Established Engineering”.

A few years ago I was given a title — “**Is Software Engineering Still an Oxymoron?”** — and asked to do a talk answering the question.

> [YouTube video](https://youtu.be/D43PlUr1x_E)

To me, there is such a thing as SE — just from the definition of the two terms separately — but its general state is not up to what today requires. An important part of this talk is that I wound up doing considerable emailing and Zoom chats with many of the best practioners today, some of whom knew much more about certain aspects of SE than I thought I did.

For example, I was fascinated by the work of — [Gerard J. Holzmann - Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gerard_J._Holzmann) — who did the SPIN model checker (etc.) that co-verified and helped write the 3+ million lines of C code for one of the Mars Rover expeditions. I had several long chats with him — some of them concerned why NASA had not made a language that integrated the model checker and how code was written.

That this should have been done, and what was required here, indicates to me that vast improvement is required in this large general area.

So I suggest that a good place to start is to gather examples where good reasons can be found for declaring the code “not nearly good enough”, and especially “requires a qualitatively better way to address this kind of problem”.

## [What do you think is the future of software engineering Could advancements in technology eventually render it obsolete or will these advancements serve as valuable tools that enhance the work of software engineers](https://www.quora.com/What-do-you-think-is-the-future-of-software-engineering-Could-advancements-in-technology-eventually-render-it-obsolete-or-will-these-advancements-serve-as-valuable-tools-that-enhance-the-work-of-software-engineers/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=415069707&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-15*

Thanks for these examples.

I think there is “safety” and **SAFETY** — for example, your example of the *“product control software (essentially a documentation control system)”* seems benign next to discussions of nuclear reactors, however, I think that being able to actually have readable and testable requirements for SW is non-optional. For example, in this day and age, I think “requirements” need to be runnable, debuggable, “reasoning”, and part of the actual application (we could think of this part of things as the “grounded meaning” of the system). The rest of the system should be about “segregated optimization” that is protected by the dynamic requirements.

Analogies to medicine and health are sometimes healthy. We have no problem with the idea that open heart operations should be done by trained certified experts. A “commonsense” notion at the other end of the scale is that a Band-Aid can be applied by anyone. However, one of the largest factors in all forms of “health engineering and maintenance” is *sanitation* and protection against sepsis.

This means that even Band-Aids have human processes associated with them — especially in this age of super-staph bugs.

A larger example — COVID-19 — is that the countries who had both the lowest deaths per million, and the most bearable lockdowns were Asian countries which already had an ethic of putting on a mask to protect others. Countries whose citizens resisted mask wearing in a number of ways, were much more dangerous, and had 10–40 times more deaths per million. (There have also been numerous papers and books published showing that — in the US alone — upwards of 200,000 additional deaths a year are caused by hospital personnel carelessness re sanitation. — so much for professional training!)

Incidentally, two of the worse countries in deaths/million were the US and the UK, both of which have mandatory courses in Biology — with the needed content — but where the larger processes of school, teaching, interest, etc., failed to touch the imaginations of the young students with regard to life and health.

I think we can find many points of analogy to engineering and science in the large. For example, what is the state of “public imagination” with regard to greenhouse gases in the atmosphere and the larger climate — and their and their children’s future?

## [How can I convince myself that real OOP is a better way to build systems What s the reason other than Alan Kay s god given vision Is there any math behind these ideas or are they just a bunch of good looking ideas](https://www.quora.com/How-can-I-convince-myself-that-real-OOP-is-a-better-way-to-build-systems-What-s-the-reason-other-than-Alan-Kay-s-god-given-vision-Is-there-any-math-behind-these-ideas-or-are-they-just-a-bunch-of-good-looking-ideas/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=412672094&comment_type=2)

*2024-06-02*

In the late 50s, John wanted to be able to reason about various kinds of programs — and wanted programs to be able to reason about various kinds of programs. This meant in part that he wanted any kind of “fact” (a variable or relationship) to include the *situation* where the *fact* could be considered to be *true*. His solution was a kind of modal logic in which each relationship had an additional parameter that held the situation. So a robot’s location would be represented as “**at(time t, red house)**” and “**at(time q, blue house**), etc. A “situation” was a collection of facts which could be considered mutually true.

This allowed histories (world lines) of progressions connected by the computations that would move a situation into the next situation.

This is a large idea about “time”, which is that “simulating time” as part of your model leads to many kinds of useful consistent reasoning.

Note that the use of “clocks” in HW does this for simple transitions, and the later use of “transactions” in data bases uses this idea in the large. The first Simula used this to deal with race conditions in theoretically concurrent simulations. Etc.

John wrote a landmark paper in 1958 (“Programs with common sense — AI Memo 17) which posited an interface agent he called the “Advice Taker”, which could do temporal reasoning, etc. Minsky got John to write a followup paper “Situations, Actions, and Causal Laws” which included ideas about how the system should be structured to do temporal modal reasoning. Both these papers eventually got published in the book “Semantic Information Processing” edited by Minsky.

## [It has been said that Windows is Bill Gates way of helping Seagate to sell ever larger hard disk drives If that is true then I claim that Smalltalk is Alan Kays way of helping Intel sell ever more computationally](https://www.quora.com/It-has-been-said-that-Windows-is-Bill-Gates-way-of-helping-Seagate-to-sell-ever-larger-hard-disk-drives-If-that-is-true-then-I-claim-that-Smalltalk-is-Alan-Kays-way-of-helping-Intel-sell-ever-more-computationally/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=410855831&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-23*

Here’s a deeper look at history. The intent of my group at Parc was to learn about how people in general — and children in particular — could be helped to both deal with personal computing (as it might be designed in the future), and to see to what extent the design of personal computing could help children learn “powerful ideas” in math, science, and society.

The larger goal of Parc was to make a “time machine” that would allow all of us to compute and design as though we living in the late 80s instead of the early 70s. This required having faith in “Moore’s Law” (which was not a law, but a possibility), and to spend money to build — in 1973 — many personal computers with powers we thought could be possible in ~15 years.

If you are concerned with “throughput” then you would have really been unhappy with the first version of Smalltalk, which — on the same machine — was about 180 times slower (but even more wildly malleable than the very malleable later Smalltalks).

On the other hand, it was just what we needed to start working with children right away, with GUIs right away, with “tiny code” right away, etc. It was a tool we made for our research purposes. In a few years Parc was ready to do a much faster microcoded personal computer, but Xerox balked.

This resulted in a “second phase” Smalltalk done by Dan Ingalls, et al., that was about 180 times faster (and with much larger effective storage), not nearly as flexible, especially for children, but allowed other experiments to be successfully done. This type of Smalltalk was continued to eventually become Smalltalk-80.

I have a feeling that you are serious when you say *“Clearly, I care about throughput; what else does a consumer get from a computing device but a stream of bits?”*

This makes it very difficult to explain what the ARPA/Parc community was trying to invent in the 60s and 70s — and how we had to go about it. It also makes it very difficult to explain the qualitative differences between how “actually new” things — such as GUIs, end-user languages, etc. — are researched and invented vs. the complex world of products and marketing.

In any case, Xerox Parc was always trying to do research (it was not set up for product development), and I think it succeded in coming up with many inventions, that were solid enought to be productized by product divisions. Our view of “throughput” was “faster than the human nervous system for what we are trying to find out”.

If we go to neutral ground for a moment, consider that the “chip world” was anti-graphic-processing-units, even after this was shown to be both needed and a good idea. For your “throughput” concerns, many areas of what personal computing needs require many kinds of specialized mechanisms HW and SW to provide what can be good about personal computing.

Another neutral ground was HyperCard, which I was happy to watch Apple really do a good job with. I was sad that the architectures — Motorola and Intel — that HyperCard had to run on, forced the implementors of HyperCard to write much too much in machine code (precisely for your concerns about “throughput”). However, this crippled many kinds of extentions that the more than 4 million script-writing end-users of HyperCard thought they needed.

What HyperCard needed was for Intel and Motorola etc to supply architectures that allowed really high level languages to be emulated as directly as possible, to allow for balance between flexibility and speed — especially for end-users. Merely making poor architectures faster doesn’t help much until they are so fast they can be treated as Turing Machines (and even then …).

So we are from two very different worlds. I think you are commenting about how late-bound highly flexible languages — like LISP or Smalltalk — don’t fit well on the Intel and Motorola CPUs that were aimed by marketing decisions at the C world and machine code.

Sic transit gloria mundi

## [How can I convince myself that real OOP is a better way to build systems What s the reason other than Alan Kay s god given vision Is there any math behind these ideas or are they just a bunch of good looking ideas](https://www.quora.com/How-can-I-convince-myself-that-real-OOP-is-a-better-way-to-build-systems-What-s-the-reason-other-than-Alan-Kay-s-god-given-vision-Is-there-any-math-behind-these-ideas-or-are-they-just-a-bunch-of-good-looking-ideas/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=410093825&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-19*

Yes. Quite a bit of the early work on RPC was done at Parc, though not by my group. We thought the communications system had to be more flexible and scalable — and not in the form of commands. How to coordinate systems of computations over WANs was a major topic of conversation.

The experiments using Smalltalk over the Ethernet mostly used “proxy objects” that were standins for objects on other machines. Other methods were also tried.

Dave Reed’s thesis at MIT in 1978 was also quite exciting: it was both object oriented and used “pseudo-time” for coordination (an idea we already liked from both Simula I and earlier from McCarthy). We never got around to building a “deep pseudotime” system at Parx, but years later hooked up with Dave Reed to try out his ideas in a system called “Croquet”.

## [It has been said that Windows is Bill Gates way of helping Seagate to sell ever larger hard disk drives If that is true then I claim that Smalltalk is Alan Kays way of helping Intel sell ever more computationally](https://www.quora.com/It-has-been-said-that-Windows-is-Bill-Gates-way-of-helping-Seagate-to-sell-ever-larger-hard-disk-drives-If-that-is-true-then-I-claim-that-Smalltalk-is-Alan-Kays-way-of-helping-Intel-sell-ever-more-computationally/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=409829572&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-17*

If you know of any actual VHLLs relative to this day and age, I’d like to hear about them.

One of the main keys to microcode in its era was that it was 5–10 times as fast as the next layer of memory (usually RAM) — this meant that you could synthesize tailored instructions for the language’s VM — this is many times more efficient than macros (which are working at the RAM level instruction pace). I.e. dedicated HW would not be faster (because the whole idea was to be waiting on main memory to cycle).

## [It has been said that Windows is Bill Gates way of helping Seagate to sell ever larger hard disk drives If that is true then I claim that Smalltalk is Alan Kays way of helping Intel sell ever more computationally](https://www.quora.com/It-has-been-said-that-Windows-is-Bill-Gates-way-of-helping-Seagate-to-sell-ever-larger-hard-disk-drives-If-that-is-true-then-I-claim-that-Smalltalk-is-Alan-Kays-way-of-helping-Intel-sell-ever-more-computationally/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=409822867&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-17*

The two main things that were learned from the B5000 experience were (a) it really makes a difference to design the HW for a very high level language, and (b) that we are still on learning curves, so we don’t know enough about VHLLs and what we need next, so: there needs to be something that can play the role of microcode to allow new principles to be made into primitives.

This worked very well with the microcode machines at Parc: they helped Smalltalk and Lisp, and also allowed for HLL experiments in other directions. Neither Intel nor Motorola could understand this.

10 or so years ago FPGAs got past a capacity threshold, and started to be both strategic and tactical to occupy that role. This works in both directions (a) because you have FPGAs you can think of VHLL ideas not practical without special architectures, and (b) VHLL ideas you have might be aided by what can be done with FPGAs.

## [What is the Chronology of Early OOP see self answer for details](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-Chronology-of-Early-OOP-see-self-answer-for-details/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=409404057&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-15*

Hi François

Presented as a long list, it winds up making the actual history sound much more complex than I recall from living through it.

Part of the actual simplicity is that the number of “computer scientists” working in this area was small, and most of them knew each other — and most of them read the main literature (for example, the CACM in those days was a primary technical publication, very different than it is today).

Simula I got off to a slower start than it deserved because the main article (in CACM 1966) was difficult to read (the translated Norwegian into English was OK, but many of the term choices were new and the ones that overlapped with usage in the US and UK, etc., did not use the already established terms. E.g. what Ivan called a “Master” and what was eventually called a “Class”, Simula I called an “Activity”. What most computerists called “Instances”, Simula I called “Processes”.

I was lucky to learn Simula I in Nov 1966 because I was a new grad student, and was assigned the task of getting “Algol” running on the university computer. “Algol” turned out to be Simula I, and we grad students had to read the assembly code to understand what it was trying to do. Our first test examples were taken from the ACM paper. This was all very lucky.

Another way to understand some of the history is to contrast the backgrounds of most of the proto-computer-scientists back then with the typical backgrounds of computerists today.

Because there were no undergrad degree programs in computing in the 60s, most of the researchers back then had majored in established “difficult” subjects such as mathematics, physical sciences, deep engineering, etc. This provided a lot of knowledge to form analogies with.

The other deep source of knowledge was the large thinking done by the “real AI” pioneers and their grad students — McCarthy, Minsky, Newell, Simon, Shaw, Bobrow, Winston, etc. (also with deep backgrounds). These systems were too slow to be practical programming languages, but they all had much richer ideas and more interesting epistemological bases. If you look at how “knowledge and inference” were represented in these systems you will find larger and similar ideas to the smaller ones put in practical programming languages.

A bottom line here is that I don’t know of any really successful extensions of simple instantiation from “Masters” that Ivan used in Sketchpad. Smalltalk at Parc was successful because we were very careful at the scale in which we worked (however, the various kinds of inheritance that the whole community experimented with — including us — remained distressingly ad hoc).

## [What is the Chronology of Early OOP see self answer for details](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-Chronology-of-Early-OOP-see-self-answer-for-details/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=409233249&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-14*

The problem in using this in programming is the way nature debugs new combinations (there is a little of this in ML).

With most software, we try to get almost there via *design* and do very little debugging — this is much more efficient than nature (which doesn’t know what it is supposed to wind up with). However, “generate and test” is a fall-back position that sometimes can work when we don’t know how to design.

On the other hand, we might not be able to understand all that a seemingly successful generated program might actually do — this is - in general - dangerous.

The actual biological processes allow for “more than the parents provide” (otherwise we wouldn’t wind up with new species, etc.).

## [What does Alan Kay think about Ray Kurzweil s concepts of Singularity and The Law of Accelerating Returns](https://www.quora.com/What-does-Alan-Kay-think-about-Ray-Kurzweil-s-concepts-of-Singularity-and-The-Law-of-Accelerating-Returns/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=409211995&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-14*

“Wisdom” is a word — like “virtue” — that means different things to different people.

One way to approach your quest is finding people you feel are “wise” (and try to see how they go about it), combined with reading, etc. to see if you can come up with your own characterization of “wisdom” — and then to see how you might become more do along these lines.

In my own opinion — and with my own definitions of wisdom — I think that all of the people — whether personally or via reading — I’ve considered *wise* go into “slow careful thinking” when confronted with something important. They may be helped by inborn temperament, but most of them have practiced “not reacting” and “slowing down”.

For example, when confronted with a very complex situation in which there are different sides contending, each with valid grievances, and where there is killing on both sides, a wise person will probably not try to untangle the legalities and who did what first (the “first” could have been centuries ago).

Instead, a wise person might try to seek a moral ground that can get past the complexities. For example, *“no one and no group should have killing children as a means to their end”*. This means to all involved: “stop right now”.

I mentioned Solon above, who was considered the wisest person in Athens when he lived (about 600BC — so “early Greece”). What follows is a bit of a legend because not a lot of concrete evidence survives.

The Ahtenians suffered under the previous tyrant Draco (he is where the term “draconian” came from), and asked Solon to “give us some laws”. Solon (actually being wise) was wise enough not to do this right away. Instead, he suggested that they write — on billboards and the walls of the buildings — all customs and rules that were deemed to be true. After this, a walk around the city revealed that most of these were in the form of proverbs, and most cancelled each other (e.g. “Out of sight, out of mind” but “Absence makes the heart grow fonder”). These contradictions (also found in most religious documents) happen because human beings seek “confirmation and resonance” much more than “actuality” — and the former can be answered with a proverb or belief.

Solon wasn’t the first to use writing to (a) slow things down, and (b) to allow much easier comparing and contrasting of ideas, but he was early. And this was one of the factors that got the 6th century and later Greeks to get interested in *reasoning* and *evidence* and *the larger schemes of things*.

An example of the latter, is that Solon during all this was reputed to have pointed out that *“One can judge a civilization by how well it treats its least well off members”.* This was not only a big — and wise — idea back then, but this idea is struggled with today more than 2600 years later. What does it mean to be in a society? What is different from being a hermit in a cave? Etc.

---

## [What do You think of Smallscript https www codeproject com Articles 5373567 Smallscript A Smalltalk Inspired Scripting Languag](https://www.quora.com/What-do-You-think-of-Smallscript-https-www-codeproject-com-Articles-5373567-Smallscript-A-Smalltalk-Inspired-Scripting-Languag/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=409202293&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-14*

For those who are interested in this general subject vis a vis Unix, I suggest looking at early versions of the system and especially the work of Doug McIlroy, and of Dave Farber — and in turn to look at “pipes”, “SNOBOL”, “awk”, etc.

And, let us ignore the per process memory overhead that Unix imposed, and think about Unix processes abstractly. Now we have a simple and powerful architecture of a tiny kernel with mostly processes communicating by sending and receiving strings. It is up to each process to parse the strings and decide what they are supposed to mean, and to perform other actions *at the receiving process’ discretion*. This architecture means that you can organize computations as communications structures of processes — using “pipes” — and larger systerms start to look more comprehensible and easier to make. The interprocess language is determined by the schemes adopted by the interior process parsers and reactors.

Sound like OOP? It sounds a lot like Smalltalk 72 to me, because I used something similar in order to win “the hallway bet” I had with Dan Ingalls and Ted Kaehler. In this case I used LISP as a model rather than just strings.

I wasn’t actually thinking directly about McIlroy’s work, but I’m sure I had read about “syntax macros” etc. Unix itself and C were contemporaneous with the early Smalltalks, and neither were developed enough to influence the other early on. But they both were the result of systems thinkers, etc.

A big difference was the idea in Smalltalk to have a simple universal way to define all meanings, and this forced it to be both simpler and more compact (and slower) than Unix+C+strings+memory overheads for processes, etc. (all of which conspired against eventual simplicity).

Smalltalk also benefitted tremendously from having the dynamically microcoded Alto, whereas Bell Labs decided to try to do Unix on PDP-11s (a tough architecture for making efficient late bound languages — however, as above, do take a look at the SNOBOLs and at the AWKs).

## [How can anyone who is intelligent believe in god](https://www.quora.com/How-can-anyone-who-is-intelligent-believe-in-god/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=407232496&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-03*

Yes, “just logic” can “reason”, but *science* is needed to use it when we are trying to get a better understanding of the worlds outside our thoughts and beliefs. I’ve always liked Einstein’s observation about reasoning to fellow physicists ca 1920:

*“As far as the laws of mathematics refer to reality, they are not certain;

 and as far as they are certain, they do not refer to reality.”*

You say:

*“We agree that any kind of waking up, in the context of human biology, is another dream.”*

But then you say:

*“How do you know you’re experiencing anything at all at this moment? Whatever the answer is, that’s what I’m talking about.”*

But here is where we look at things differently. I think that we can only “think and know and feel, etc.” in the context of human biology. So we — and thoughts such as those in the previous paragraph — are still in “our dream”.

This conclusion — per Einstein — is “not certain” — but it does have the methods behind it that have worked better in so many areas than pure “reason” and “feelings” disconnected from biology.

This is tough to discuss because most of our languages and internal mapping systems don’t have good ways to deal with *processes*, and tend to reify them into “more noun-like things”.

This has certainly been the case for “processes of thinking” in our brains. I often use the term “brain/mind” to provide a compound term for the “stuff in process” and the “manifestations of the stuff in process”.

My research community taught its members — usually when they were grad students — that one should argue/discuss/etc. to make progress, but never to try to win.

---

## [How can anyone who is intelligent believe in god](https://www.quora.com/How-can-anyone-who-is-intelligent-believe-in-god/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=407056731&comment_type=2)

*2024-05-02*

Hi Jacob

Please let me encourage you to put your ideas in a separate answer to this question. They are interesting enough to “get pondering going” and to motivate comments (which, to me, is the main value of Quora).

“Waking up” with respect to human biology — to me — is very (most?) likely to mean “moving from one kind of dream to another kind of dream”.

The way “dream” and “waking” are phrased could imply that we can escape from “dreaming”, but I personally doubt it.

Science is an interesting history of attempts to get our waking dreams to have more in common with — better maps of and generators of — the phenomena we can sense (and now with the help of instruments).

I find it very interesting that we still have to ponder the results of scientific technique with mental processes that intrinsically involve belief and constructions from our beliefs.

In the picture, you will see some hardware associated with most IBM computers, and especially the 1401 (the 1403 printer, the tape drives, etc.) These all would have been for I/O.

## [How do we program a universal bootstrapper that can run any meta circular evaluator](https://www.quora.com/How-do-we-program-a-universal-bootstrapper-that-can-run-any-meta-circular-evaluator/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=406747652&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-30*

Let me first confess that — along with many present and former biologists — slime molds were one of my favorite organisms when I was a student. (My main interest back then in the early 60s was “morphogenesis”, but the MolBio field was just getting started, and its major interests were simpler organisms (such as bacteria and the viruses that attact bacteria, simple single nucleated cell organisms, etc.).

However, it was already known — up to a point — that multicelled embryos organized much — if not most — of their development via many kinds of chemical gradients that would help individual cells have some sense of where they were in the whole organism, and some sense of which genes to turn on and off to fulfill what was called back then their “fate”.

So the behavior of slime molds — which were sometimes as indiviual cells, and even spores, which then could organize as large colonies, and even differentiate to some extent (resembling an embryo in many ways) — was (and is) fantastically interesting.

However, I’m a big fan of the “Paul MacCready ‘Sweet Spot’ “ — which is to find the easiest place to do research that is just barely qualitatively in the new domain. I often use this diagram in talks:

![](https://qph.cf2.quoracdn.net/main-qimg-cabc4c4e25d51f9474b6088a7d8b2d79)

This claims there are *thresholds* for many endeavors (including the all important *“What is actually needed” (WIAN)*), that are the gateways to qualitative advances.

The “Paul MacCready ‘Sweet Spot’ “ is just across the qualitative threshold from the “pink world” to the brand new “blue world”.

Bottom line — for me — is that I would spend a lot more time to find this sweet spot for this problem — and it would be a lot simpler than your slime mold investigation or Lilly’s dolphins. For example, a really good program along the lines of Lenat’s “AM” that could bootstrap from a prepared but completely unknown bitstring, would be an immense learning event.

This reminds me of the many decades work of my friend Mike Genesereth and [General Game Playing](http://ggp.stanford.edu/). The basic idea has been that — each year — Mike sets up a simulation environment that can be drived by a certain kind of logic programming. The game starts when he publishes a description of the game — in logic — to be *read* by the *program* written by a player. The *program* has to then synthesize a *computer player* for the game that will then try to win it. The range of games is very large.

I think you can see the relationship of this interesting area to what we are discussing above. I was lucky enough — long ago — to have had a student who was a winner of this competition (and his thesis was his approach) — since I was one of the hoped for signers, I had to learn this new problem domain (and I was very impressed).

It’s very worthwhile to learn about Mike’s work in all of the many areas he has explored.

---

## [How do we program a universal bootstrapper that can run any meta circular evaluator](https://www.quora.com/How-do-we-program-a-universal-bootstrapper-that-can-run-any-meta-circular-evaluator/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=406710278&comment_type=2)

This is essentially what John Lilly — who was the first to really try to communicate with dolphins — pointed out.

However IIRC — back then, when this was a hot topic — most could readily see that there could be lots of cases where there could not be enough in common for two species to communicate meaningfully (even on our own planet).

The “binary” vs “?” is a bit of a red-herring, because “marks” can be represented by many kinds of signals.

One of the notions back then was “if we send radiation” and “they” can receive it, then we can make a few assumptions, and possibly create a message whose pattern can be discerned.

## [What is the most shocked you have been when waking up](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-most-shocked-you-have-been-when-waking-up/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=405612451&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-24*

I’m basically a “systems guy”, and a long time ago got degrees in math and molecular biology before being seduced by ARPA computing research in the 60s and contributing a few ideas for new programming languages and networking development.

The main questions from this perspective have to do with scalability, shapable non-linearities, controllability, interoperability, latencies, stabilities, etc. A quote I furnished back then was *“You can fix a clock, but you have to negotiate with a system”*. Given this — if you get to design a system — a lot needs to be thought about long before it is actually needed (and it is very difficult to bolt on after the fact what was left out of the design).

This was actually done for the ARPA networks (the ARPAnet, and especially the Internet).

We can note that

(a) the US was greatly aided by not starting with much of a system already in place (but still had many “parts problems” at various scales).

(b) much of the impetus for the EU was/is a good idea, but had many legacy barriers, plus a poor design to incorporate learning curves into the process (the US also has the latter problem).

(c) from a systems POV, for the last 100 years or so, we have been way beyond needing working systems only the size of the (imperfect) US or EU. For the creatures we humans are, this is already tough sledding, and going to become much more acute.

---

## [What is the most shocked you have been when waking up](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-most-shocked-you-have-been-when-waking-up/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=405584880&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-24*

Fun comment, but one of the main official issues was “No taxation without representation”, which wasn’t the case for Brexit (though the “Take Back Control” slogans made it seem so).

By the way, the original organization of the US after the Revolutionary War was a “Continental Confederacy” of the original states that wound up not workling well at all (and with some similar problems to the EU, which is a kind of confederacy).

This eventually resulted in the Constitutional Convention of 1787 where a lot of thinking and planning had preceded it, and was a central part of the process and the public debate that followed. This was ratified in 1789 — it still had flaws, but its much better overall systems design was comprehenive enough to work.

## [Has Alan Kay started any activist movements to realize his vision of personal computing What were the hurdles The negative impact of capitalism and corporations etc What one can do as a political act](https://www.quora.com/Has-Alan-Kay-started-any-activist-movements-to-realize-his-vision-of-personal-computing-What-were-the-hurdles-The-negative-impact-of-capitalism-and-corporations-etc-What-one-can-do-as-a-political-act/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=405512944&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-23*

One of the most interesting properties of Snow’s Rede Lecture was how tiny a subgroup it was addressed to: basically a slightly extended Oxbridge establishment that had either gone for “Letters” or for “Science” — he left out most of the population, with or without documented education. (I’ll leave out a further dive into this for some other time.)

But it does bring up something that happened in the 50s and 60s which really puzzled those of us on the US side of the pond. In the 50s, and into the 60s, (IMO) there was more real “computer science” in the UK than in the US — this in the form of computer architectures and various important software inventions.

And there was also the amazing “LEO” (The Lyons Electronic Office), where a large tea parlor etc. company not only got interested in what was arguably the first real working programmable computer — Maurice Wilkes’ EDSAC at Cambridge — but — astoundingly — build and programmed one on their own to provide an astonishing “Electronic Office” system for most aspects of their business.

There are numerous books about LEO — but I should point out that the range of analysis, design, and construction done by the Lyons people was incredible.

By the early part of the 60s, the British lead in both HW and SW — and the business of both — was pretty much gone. This resulted in a blessing for the US: many young British computerists left the country and came to the US to pursue computing (including William Newman (son of Max) who were both friends of Turing).

I have never seen an analysis, but have talked to a number of the real old timers (I’m a member of the LEO support society). To oversimplify, one of the things that happened is that the factions of Technologists, Business, and Money apparently could not cooperate. Where there was a lot of Cold War research funding in the US, and then VCs, neither existed in a fruitful way in either the UK or in France (which had a similar version of this story).

Apparently (back to Snow), there were both class differences and subclass differences (boffins vs ?) that made fruitful cooperation impossible. (Why the LEO was successful early on only bolsters this perspective.)

IBM US got to the game late (and with many dubious marketing ploys), but still wound up owning the largest part of the computing business in both the UK and France.

In general education in the UK, there is still the horrendous practice of “General Certificate of Secondary Education” which is an exam around age 16 that is a vast — much too early — determiner of most children’s future in education, and in life. This is now more varied than it used to be (it originally closely followed what Snow decries: a false wall between “Letters” and “Science”). I am quite sure that this is extremely unhealthy for “wider futures for the UK”.

I don’t have as much substantive to say about this as I’d like to — it’s complex and I spent a lot more time trying to deal with the US’s own — very poor — educational system.

I’m pretty sure that most deep dives in both countries would find that conservative approaches to politics, business, and money in general are at the root of a lot of things that could have been done, and needed to be done (and are still needed to be done).

---

## [Has Alan Kay started any activist movements to realize his vision of personal computing What were the hurdles The negative impact of capitalism and corporations etc What one can do as a political act](https://www.quora.com/Has-Alan-Kay-started-any-activist-movements-to-realize-his-vision-of-personal-computing-What-were-the-hurdles-The-negative-impact-of-capitalism-and-corporations-etc-What-one-can-do-as-a-political-act/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=405498923&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-23*

From having lived in both countries — the US and the UK are more similar in many ways along these lines— for both better and worse — than one might think.

I think one interesting difference lies in the scaling — which can mean that the same percentage of “possibility of invention” in both countries, means there will numerically be about a factor of 5 more inventors in the US (this can sometimes be significant). And similarly with “possibility of good-type research funding” — a factor of 5 difference can really be significant as to whether something actually gets done.

To get a longer view, it’s worth going back to the 30s to see what “the boffins” were doing in the UK — and how one of their interests wound up being the significant technological factor in winning WWII (radar, and especially the invention of the cavity magnetron — and how this was invented).

Largely because of Henry Tizard, there was a “perfect sharing of talents and ideas” between the two countries that made the difference — this was done in 1940 before Pearl Harbor and the US entry to the war.

[Henry Tizard - Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Tizard)

HIs counterpart in the US was Alfred Loomis — a millionaire and physical scientist — who was a main welcomer to Tizard, and wound up — amongst many other things — providing the initial funding for Building 20 at MIT out of his own pocket.

[Alfred Lee Loomis - Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfred_Lee_Loomis)

## [How does Alan Kay view the connection between the relational model and real OOP How would you avoid impadence mismatch problem with RDBMS How was with Smalltalk Systems](https://www.quora.com/How-does-Alan-Kay-view-the-connection-between-the-relational-model-and-real-OOP-How-would-you-avoid-impadence-mismatch-problem-with-RDBMS-How-was-with-Smalltalk-Systems/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=405411906&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-23*

As I’ve related in other screeds, I had to be kicked in the head by seeing Sketchpad and Simula (S & S) in the same week of 1966.

Before that, most of the code I wrote was indeed “unit record processing” code for the Air Force and then for weather data tapes for the National Center for Atmospheric Research. I did see the “B220 File System” in the AF, which did make use of a kind of procedural embedding (but in the manner of abstract data types, not system modeling). I thought this was neat, but there were no flashes. Duh.

Once seeing S & S, it was an instant rotation of perspective and “point of view is worth 80 IQ points”.

I have always found it interesting that for a few years one part of my brain knew that “for representing arbitrary ideas” you have have processes, and another part just sat there like a lump and “processed static *data*” using data structures with outside arbitrary procedures.

I attribute part of the change in POV to moving from programming as a job to pay my way through college, to winding up in what I thought would be a one year stay in an actual computer department. Then “S & S”, and then seeing that both my Biology and Math majors had been at modeling much longer than computing, and I should have paid more attention to them, but this didn’t happen until I was in the new and different context.

## [What is the most shocked you have been when waking up](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-most-shocked-you-have-been-when-waking-up/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=405056259&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-21*

Hi George

I think it would help if you broke your long and multi-faceted comment up into a number of separate questions.

My old friend — Marvin Minsky — used to point out that our brain/mind likes “things in twos” and tends to put them in opposition (i.e. “true” and “false”, “random” vs. “directed”, etc.). His suggestion was “When you have a *two* try to find at least a *three*”.

So an interesting large idea to consider is that a system composed of parts which don’t do anything very interesting by themselves, can nonetheless do very interesting things when assembled into a whole. New behaviors can come about via organization of stuff which doesn’t behave that way (the new information content is not in the parts but the organization of the parts). So — in a random world with available energy — you can possibly get organizations that act with purpose and non-randomly.

One of the fascinations of Biology comes from “atoms being both attractive and standoffish” (as I think Feynman once noted). “Science” properly includes “study of systems”, but compared to Physics, Chemistry, and Biology, very little has been done to date (Abiogenesis, Computing and Socio-Psychology have been three of the larger sources of systems thinking so far).

## [What is the most shocked you have been when waking up](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-most-shocked-you-have-been-when-waking-up/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=404940411&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-20*

First — as a former biologist — I need to remind that neither Darwin’s original theories, nor the form of today’s evolutionary theories are of the form “Base Entity -> Higher Entity”. The center of the process is “fittedness” not “betterness” — nor “will to survive”. Etc.

Second — using the US as an example — we need to remember that in each of the last 4 presidential elections, the Democrat candidate won the poplular vote. The deciding factor for Trump — who lost the popular vote by 2 million votes — was the electorial college scheme — it is not a “one person, one vote” scheme.

So the actual question in the case of US elections is “Should the electoral system be fixed, perhaps by elimination?”.

Another question — vastly more complex — is whether there are better criteria than age and citizenship for being able to vote?. Whether the latter is retained or not, I think the US education system itself has to be reformed, not just for making representative government work well enough, but to raise the possibility of better lives for more citizens living in greater harmony.

I think that a large part of any new education has to include teaching and learning about our own species and why we behave the way we do.

## [What does Alan Kay think about Ray Kurzweil s concepts of Singularity and The Law of Accelerating Returns](https://www.quora.com/What-does-Alan-Kay-think-about-Ray-Kurzweil-s-concepts-of-Singularity-and-The-Law-of-Accelerating-Returns/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=404769893&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-19*

Neil was one of the nicest humans ever — his instinct was to be a public gadfly (and he was a great one) — his temperament was to be genial and humerous, and this allowed him to pass judgement in ways that those who believed differently would still listen (and maybe ponder).

One of the many interesting things about him was that he prefered writing so much that — despite his excellent ability to speak extemporaneously — he liked to write an essay for all his talks and then read it for the talk. This is usually “death” for a talk, but his writing and delivery was so good, most of the audience was not even aware that he was doing this. (It’s worth chasing down a few of his YouTube captures to see him do this.)

Another interesting tidbit was his belief that you should be able to explain anything in less than 50 pages (he didn’t really like to write at book length). His publishers convinced/forced him to do this, so he would take one of his 20–50 pagers and expand it to book size. He did get them to publish some of his original essays — the book “Conscientious Objections” — so you can judge for yourself.

A tragedy is that we could not get him to quit smoking (it is not at all easy for a heavy smoker to quit (from my own personal experience doing it)). This eventually got to him and he died a much earlier death than he should have. We lost a great human being when this happened.

---

## [What is the most shocked you have been when waking up](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-most-shocked-you-have-been-when-waking-up/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=404535755&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-18*

I’ll try to address the last part of your comment.

“Biology means variation” — this is built into the bottom-most level of how atoms and molecules behave when heated.

For most current (and former, like me) biologists, what are extremely interesting are the relative stabilities that can also form: so “variation within speciation, and without”.

For we humans, we can expect any population to have a distribution of traits within the generalizations we make about the group.

We can also expect (and do see) the cultures which every human group has (it is part of what it means to be human) mirror our genetics in various ways.

For those interested in Cultural Anthropology (and Cultural Psychology within this), the variation of behaviours moderated by the local culture are extremely interesting. For example, the ancient Grecian genius — Solon — is reputed to have said “A society can be judged by how it treats its least well off members”. This is a really interesting idea, and especially because many societies do not treat their least well off members well at all.

A society that buys off on Solon’s suggestion is going to be a very different one to live in compared to the most who reject the idea for “competition” and “survival of the fittest”, etc.

From the standpoint of “science”, all of a sudden, we have left the realm of “predicting fairly accurately from general observations” to join a larger non-linear world where inventions vastly change the situations for many.

One of the original purposes of “schools” was to teach ideas and methods to children and others that are not generally found in their surrounding culture.

One of the original purposes of public education in American was not preparation for jobs, but preparation for being able to act as a citizen in the tricky world of representative democracy.

Jefferson wrote in a letter in the 1800s:

*“I know of no safe repository for the ultimate powers of society but the people themselves; and if we think them not enlightened enough to exercise their control with a wholesome discretion, the remedy is not to take it from them, but to increase their discretion by education.”*

This is the crux of the matter: there are thresholds that need to be reached — in knowledge, how to argue to make progress, sense of the larger situations, and the future, etc. — in order for *enough discretion* to be gained by the body politic to allow our system to work.

It wasn’t that someone like Trump won an election somewhere that was shocking, it’s that he won in a country that had originally explicitly been set up to be more thoughtful about how it chose its representatives — and where the educational system was supposed to have been teaching some of the new skills of being thoughtful that have been invented.

I didn’t have a high opinion of the educational system in my country when I went to sleep that night — but I had enough faith in it, to be shocked — upon waking — when it failed so spectacularly.

---

However, the German situation in the 30s was still a surprise to many (and precisely because it was thought that the Education would have provided more perspective). After the 30s, many thought that this History placed within Education, would provide more perspective and wisdom in the future.

The surprise I felt was not that humans were acting this way, but, as I said above, was: “the idea that millions who had gone to school in the US had not learned enough of the real issues — or how to think about them — to see the monumental mistake they were making”.

This was despite that I’d spent a lot of time working in schools and also with more global organizations trying to improve what I thought was very poor schooling. Still, the extent to which I was off on the “level of *poor*” was shocking to me.

## [What is the most shocked you have been when waking up](https://www.quora.com/What-is-the-most-shocked-you-have-been-when-waking-up/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=404335834&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-17*

Good comment! Nasir’s 5–10 year estimate — he thought it through in more detail — is probably better than my 5 year one.

After the fact, and because I was living in London, I was asked about “things that could be done” to revitalize the situation.

My first request was to see the dynamic computer model of the country showing all in inflows and outflows of dependencies and usages of energy, fuel, food, etc. They didn’t have one — and this would make it very difficult to create a real plan. I figured they would need to create a good enough one.

Besides the planning, it really takes time to create infrastructure, and “the tools to make the tools” if the lead up has been a joint effort with the EU.

I was also worried that very little thought had been given to the previously set up solution to the Irish border (which partially involved how membership in the EU was used).

So, my reaction was “at least 5 years of prep”.

In actuality, two main things happened: Covid, and more of the “Br” than the “exit” (meaning that much of the original split hasn’t been really carried out — much of this to keep all those flows going).

I still consider myself “basically ignorant” about the UK systems situation — though I love living over here and love London (this is a pretty limited view). I’m also in my 80s now and not as interested in non-linear systems details as I used to be.

However, messing around with the meta-levels of a complex non-linear system without really good models can be quite dangerous. I’m hoping that the current situation will re-morph with Europe and the rest of the world using a more modern perspective.

---

## [What does Alan Kay think about Ray Kurzweil s concepts of Singularity and The Law of Accelerating Returns](https://www.quora.com/What-does-Alan-Kay-think-about-Ray-Kurzweil-s-concepts-of-Singularity-and-The-Law-of-Accelerating-Returns/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=404235286&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-16*

I think this is an excellent comment. It’s worth putting beside it a few thoughts from science, Neil Postman, Daniel Kahneman, etc.

A unifying idea is that most of our brain is “fast” and “reactive” and “remembering” and “believing”. These are part of the “expository fiction” that Kahneman calls “System 1”, and we can see why they were favored by evolutionary processes. I think we can also see that these properties/processes make actual (*real*) thinking very difficult (it is slow, avoids reaction, most memory, and beliefs).

One of the ways of thinking about Zen meditation is that it tries to deal with System 1 by avoiding it. Other methods also work — for example, there are ways of dealing with thoughts that keep them at bay long enough to have deeper insights (this is part of what one learns when one becomes a scientist: ways to get around System 1, etc.).

I’m sure Neil would have renamed his most famous book “Distracting Ourselves To Death” (or more likely written a new book with that name).

I think there is now more generally known about processes for gaining wisdom, but — as you point out — it is difficult for these processes to be found and pursued because of the great amount of additional noise in the world.

The legends about Solon are tantalizing — deemed the wisest man of his age — I’m trying to find the best sources for his story. It would make a good addition to this thread.

## [What is Alan Kays advice for smart people who dont yet know what theyre interested in working on How deep should people explore topics like maths and science before deciding it isnt for them](https://www.quora.com/What-is-Alan-Kays-advice-for-smart-people-who-dont-yet-know-what-theyre-interested-in-working-on-How-deep-should-people-explore-topics-like-maths-and-science-before-deciding-it-isnt-for-them/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=403219612&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-11*

When asked a similar question, Bertrand Russell replied “Humans are 10% Nature and 90% Nurture”. Even though today he might be a bit off on the percentages, he was likely quite right that “Nurture” from the cultures we grown up into (and put ourselves into) is the key.

Another key idea is that our species is “not that smart”. If we take about 200,000 years on the planet as a baseline for “Homo Sapiens”, then — in rough estimates — agriculture was invented about 6% ago, writing about 2.5% ago, “Greek thought” about 1.5% ago, “real science” about 400 years ago (about 1/5th of 1% ago), “real medicine” much more recently than that. And so forth.

Perhaps even more interesting is that most of our greatest inventions have been “inventions of methods” — meaning **crafted ways to do and think about things that our genetic brains can’t**. In other words, much of what we call “thinking” and “smart” are in fact mostly skills which can be and are learned on top of what we are born with — and many of these are essentially extensions of what we do with language.

A prodigy is a child who can do things that adults do as well as adults do. I would call this “inborn talent”. However, I am a musician. and used to be a pro, and perhaps just one of the many musicians I knew and played with started off with tons of talent. *Every musician I know — pro or amateur, great and not so great — has to practice a lot every day to “keep the craft and art going”.*

As Einstein pointed out above, loving what you do can make an enormous difference, especially when many fluencies require enough practicing to develop automatic skills. A real question for educators *should* be: “How can we help children find interesting things to play with for long enough to develop fluencies (and to know that they can do this)?”

I think it’s fair to say that most education systems today show no understanding of what children need to develop their potentials. This is a moral crime against children and ultimately our societies.

By contrast Maria Montessori was wonderful at understanding children and their development. And more has been learned since her heyday in the early 20th century.

Bottom lines here are (a) people are more able than they think they are (b) can learn more than they think they can (c) most people need to be within better prepared and set up environments for healthy development.

---

## [If Alan Kay could travel in time back to the inception of computing but could only take a notebook as a knowledge base from today which insights would he pick ignore how would he approach recreating computing and](https://www.quora.com/If-Alan-Kay-could-travel-in-time-back-to-the-inception-of-computing-but-could-only-take-a-notebook-as-a-knowledge-base-from-today-which-insights-would-he-pick-ignore-how-would-he-approach-recreating-computing-and/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=402779958&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-08*

Actually, this was a bit disappointing in the book and movie. The tablets were specifically only for reading (output) and mainly for news.

In the 60s there were already working flat screen displays using both plasma and liquid crystal technologies, so this wasn’t even a look forward of much reach. There were no personal computers shown and HAL was a mainframe. I was a friend of Marvin Minsky — who was a technical advisor on the film (and a friend of Clarke) — and he was somewhat disappointed in what Kubrick was willing to try.

A key turning point that wasn’t used in the movie was that Moore’s Law was well formulated in 1965 — so much more could have been thought through regarding what computers would be like in 2001.

However, I did like what Kubrick did with the story compared to Clarke’s version of the story.

## [If Alan Kay could travel in time back to the inception of computing but could only take a notebook as a knowledge base from today which insights would he pick ignore how would he approach recreating computing and](https://www.quora.com/If-Alan-Kay-could-travel-in-time-back-to-the-inception-of-computing-but-could-only-take-a-notebook-as-a-knowledge-base-from-today-which-insights-would-he-pick-ignore-how-would-he-approach-recreating-computing-and/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=402461865&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-07*

This is a really interesting comment. With all due respect — and I mean this — I don’t think that this is the case for success going forward — and it certainly was not the case for the big inventions in computing in the past.

There were plenty of things done poorly or wrong in the past, but the parallel paths that led to the great ideas, inventions, and whole industries — for the most part — did not use them.

For example, I think that certain kinds of public ownership of businesses is a good idea — especially the kind of capitalism that Peter Drucker advocated. However, most of “American Capitalism”, and especially today’s “Friedman Capitalism” is not at all a good idea for civilization — its main goals are tiny, and anti-creative.

By contrast, the great inventions were originally sponsored by ARPA, ONR, etc. — in the public domain, but using money allocated for defense in the Cold War. This is not technically “socialism”, but would be called that today — in that the funding came from the federal government from redistributed public taxes (as do all of our defense establishments).

The great inventions did not use much of what was popular in computing to be invented. Instead new computer and software architectures were devised, tested, built and used (e.g. Parc built almost 2000 of the Alto computers rather than trying to adapt HW that would be more trouble than it would be worth from vendors).

So in my pondered opinion, your allusions to LLMs, graphics chips, and capitalism don’t work as a basis for anything good.

I don’t think that hacker’s “non-architectural hacks” that do a few things at the expense of safety and understandability should be scaled into the technical and social mess we have today — it is turning human thought and discourse into a kind of intellectual slum. (Yuval Harari noted that “many computerists are now hacking the means of human thought and communication in human culture” and, even if they knew what they were doing (and the evidence is overwhelming that they don’t), messing with the underlying meta-nature and means of civilization is not a good idea.)

---

I shied away from this idea many years ago because I had the strong feeling that “money demands to be heeded and taken care of”. My feelings were strengthened by seeing research collegues start a company based on their research results, get rich, and then never go back to what they were happiest doing before the riches.

One of the most interesting things to note is the surprisingly dismal record of most new billionaires to support anything other than processes which will increase their current wealth.

I have a feeling that someone with their notebook and this plan will not fare well back in time after they get rich.

## [If Alan Kay were starting a computing research group today how would he think about where to locate it](https://www.quora.com/If-Alan-Kay-were-starting-a-computing-research-group-today-how-would-he-think-about-where-to-locate-it/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=402270172&comment_type=2)

*2024-04-06*

This is a really good observation and comment. Here are a few corrections and additions.

Xerox Parc came about because of the vast change of direction and perspective when ARPA became DARPA (via Congress and Viet Nam war). Xerox only wanted a long range research center, but because Taylor was hired to set it up, he decided to stock it with researchers who wanted to complete the first phase of the “ARPA-dream”.

He had funded most of the young “ARPA-PhDs” and “almost PhDs” so he recruited from the ARPA community. Parc thought of itself as an ARPA project funded by Xerox, etc.

In its heyday, ARPA funded Principal Investigators who thought they had good ways to make the “ARPA dream” happen. “Seeding” doesn not work as a word in your comment. “Full funding” is the way it was done. (ONR, a really good funder, seeded.)

The *tactics* at ARPA were indeed grander in *scope* but the *visions and aims* at ARPA and Parc were the same. The concentration of talent and ability at Parc resulted in a *wider range of results* than any other single ARPA project.

## [Was there ever a serious attempt to a multiple inheritance Smalltalk cc Alan Kay Dan Ingalls If there was why didnt it work out](https://www.quora.com/Was-there-ever-a-serious-attempt-to-a-multiple-inheritance-Smalltalk-cc-Alan-Kay-Dan-Ingalls-If-there-was-why-didnt-it-work-out/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=399829402&comment_type=2)

*2024-03-24*

To some extent “all ideas come from past ideas, but really good ideas go beyond being just derivative” — so we should think of researchers like Ivan Sutherland, Doug Engelbart — and Bret Victor — as “going beyond the information given” (as Jerome Bruner liked to say), to produce conceptions qualitatively new enough to create new categories of thought.

Unlike too many of today’s computerists, Bret both put in the effort to understand the best the of past, *and* was able to be lifted rather than trapped by it, to create perspectives that provide many new illuminations.

I think of him as being in the class of the great people I worked with and was inspired by back in the 60s and 70s.

## [What were the biggest mistakes made by Xerox](https://www.quora.com/What-were-the-biggest-mistakes-made-by-Xerox/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=396820265&comment_type=2)

*2024-03-09*

I try to address some of these issues in a white paper I wrote for Ellen MacArthur Foundation in the UK - titled “How” https://internetat50.com/references/Kay_How.pdf

One (overly simplified) way to look at it is to examine our basic built-in genetic drives. One of these is hunter-gathering, and if our daily surrounds are srutinized, we can see it working very strongly, but with more modern trappings. This is a very short-term (and strong) view of survival.

A huge invention about 12,000 years ago was *agriculture*, which required a longer view, much more cooperation, etc., the idea of “don’t eat your seed corn”, and so forth.

Businesses resemble hunting/gathering much more than they should — for example, the quarterly cycles turn “the harvest” into constant gathering and anything like “research” is forced to be expensed (in the US at least). Moreover, having an almost unregulated “market” for “shares” moves the actual content of businesses and their customers far away from the minds of speculators. Multinational corporations create a further remove. Etc.

War and threat of war is frightening enough to get both citizens and governments to turn to “the boffins” (which are normally shunned and put outside of most society) and to let them choose their “boffin ways”. This has caused the unfortunate correlations between large progress in science and technologies and wars.

---

## [What were the biggest mistakes made by Xerox](https://www.quora.com/What-were-the-biggest-mistakes-made-by-Xerox/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=396691778&comment_type=2)

*2024-03-08*

You would probably be very interested in learning about various theories of “business”, and especially about how “Big Busineses” developed alongside the actually good idea of “public ownership” of companies (“capitalism”), and how this became “American capitalism” in various stages, and (now) *not* a good way to set up a system.

Bush was a scientist and was motivated by what scientists both needed and would like to have — he widened this to what “knowledge workers” needed and would like to have and use.

Licklider was a scientist — experimental psychologist — who got fascinated by the interactive PDP-1 at BBN — and wrote in 1960 the influential essay “Man-Computer Symbiosis” (this was one of the main reasons that the *scientists who ran ARPA* gave Lick millions to start ARPA’s “Information Processing Techniques Office” (IPTO) in 1962.

Engelbart was an engineer, who wanted to improve humanity, and this got him to write (in 1962) “Augmenting Human Intellect”.

Ivan Sutherland was a brilliant engineering student who was internally driven to “follow his own nose”, and who was liked by the ARPA SAGE people, and given the TX-2 SAGE experimental computer all to himself for 3 hours each middle of the night, to “find interactive computer graphics and Sketchpad”.

John McCarthy was a mathematician who looked at the SAGE interactive terminals in the late 50s, and said “Every home will have one of these”, just like we have public utilities for water, electricity, and gas; there will be one for information and reckoning.

These were partially *artistic/aesthetic* reactions that drove the visions — and they are generally not found at all — or in insignificantly tiny amounts — in commercial companies (and there are no significant reward systems involving aesthetics — outside or inside — in companies as they stood then — and now).

Note that even what could be good about public ownership can make “doing good” difficult for many stockholders to deal with.

And — scaling this up to the idea that citizens of a country are a kind of stockholder — we have the problems of getting large scale support set up and backed for important processes like schooling, health, etc. When individuals ask “what’s in it for me?”, they are usually missing why systems of people both need to cooperate and why cooperation is the main builder and improver of civilization. This is a mess right now in both the US and the UK.

---

## [What were the biggest mistakes made by Xerox](https://www.quora.com/What-were-the-biggest-mistakes-made-by-Xerox/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=396307372&comment_type=2)

*2024-03-06*

I think it is a true statement that Jobs and Gates didn’t grok the enire vision (or even much of a smaller version of it). That was really too bad for computing in general.

The Manhattan Project was able to be done the way it was — see General Groves’ book “Now It Can Be Told” to get his perspective on this massive project he ran.

The critical part of it was that they *knew* (the physics was already good enough) that they could make a uranium bomb if they could gather enough fissionable material (the first, Hiroshima bomb, was never tested — they *knew* that it would work).

This meant that most of the Manhattan Project was large scale industrial engineering (whole towns were created from scratch) to get enough of the isotope they needed.

They did pre-test the plutonium bomb — mainly to see if their theories of “shaped charges” could and would compress a much smaller amount of plutonium to a critical mass.

The ARPA Dream was mostly a research project in which some high class “experimental engineering” had to be done (because computers are “artifacts” and the running of things hard and soft are the standins for nature). Much of what was worked on, was worked on for the first time, and — for many areas (like UI, SE, etc.) — required many experiments and a large learning curve to be traversed.

Marvin Minsky had many great lines. One of them was: *“If you don’t know what to do, do lots of things”.* That was ARPA’s and then Parc’s approach. (And Parc had a clearer idea — being later to the game — about *what was actually needed*.)

---

## [Do you believe it is ever a good idea to use a GOTO in a C C program why](https://www.quora.com/Do-you-believe-it-is-ever-a-good-idea-to-use-a-GOTO-in-a-C-C-program-why/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=393996600&comment_type=2)

*2024-02-24*

IIRC, this was one of the examples that led to “Zahn’s Device” — Chuck Zahn was a student in Knuth’s class. (See top of my answer here.)

Don grew up as a machine code programmer, and was famous for rarely having bugs (there are several great stories about this). So using “gotos” was second nature to him. Higher level languages and structured programming were partially motivated because few programmers had Don’s abilities (and few wanted to gain those skills). The motivations all around were to create new structures — sometimes in HW (such as index registers) — and mostly in SW — to allow programmers to express what they wanted in ways that were less intricate and harder to accidentally misuse.

Then a question arose: what if your structured language created inefficiencies for a scheme you just needed to run as quickly as possible. One side said “keep the goto (and pay the consequences of bad use”). The other side said, “Let’s invent “extensible languages” so we can add new required ideas as we find needs for these”. This introduces a kind of “meta”, which can be really dangerous itself.

Don did not like this kind of meta (he had had experience with one of the early ones that actually worked: Ned Irons’ IMP).

When we did the first Smalltalk, one of the first extensions that was tried (for fun) was to see if the extension ideas could handle “Zahn’s Device” (they could). There is an example of this in the “Smalltalk-72 Instruction Manual”.

---

## [What profound insights did Seymour Cray have that allowed him to design faster computers than others could at the time Do they consist of proper cooling and signal sync which frankly seem to be methods that computer](https://www.quora.com/What-profound-insights-did-Seymour-Cray-have-that-allowed-him-to-design-faster-computers-than-others-could-at-the-time-Do-they-consist-of-proper-cooling-and-signal-sync-which-frankly-seem-to-be-methods-that-computer/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=393209989&comment_type=2)

*2024-02-20*

The Alto microcode kernel processor was “hardware crafting hardware” (meaning that it was set up for creating the fastest cheapest “personal supercomputers” that we could devise). This worked very well: Chuck Thacker et al were able to do the first Alto from scratch in just a little over 3 months, and almost 2000 were eventually made for research use.

The level of complexity of the microcode was like the level of complexity of logic organizations, and was programmed very carefully — and very cooperatively — by what became a bit of a small club). It was not the most mindbending microcode design I’ve ever written on, but it was “challenging enough”.

As I recall the Alto had two register files (R & S) 16 bits wide and 32 words long. There was no cache (this was the bottom of the machine), and no interthread protection (agreements and negotiations were were made for “guaranteed registers” in the R file).

Another interesting complexity was how the zero overhead task switching was accomplished (another story for another time).

Somewhat surprisingly, this worked extremely well (for very similar reasons that HW usually is much more bug free than SW). The microcode programmers took on a HW mindset and were rewarded with high reliability under normally very dangerous conditions.

I think the early times helped as well. Most everyone in their early years had done a lot of machine code programming in unprotected conditions, and thus brought a lot of experience (and a calm mindset) to what needed to be done.

---
