## [Which is the best book for teaching programming to kids 11 to 16 years](https://www.quora.com/Which-is-the-best-book-for-teaching-programming-to-kids-11-to-16-years/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=391472231&comment_type=2)

*2024-02-12*

My fundamental heuristic has been to (a) learn as much about “everything” as possible, and then (b) try to forget it “except for the perfume”.

The idea is make space for new ideas, but without re-inventing the already good ones. The “perfume” reminds how “good things smell” and help move towards other good things (hard to say in words).

The main issues (to me) are not programming per se, but helping our species build in their minds and practices much better ways to deal with our situations — and especially our most important situations.

We learn most deeply when the ideas are integral with our surrounding culture (this is how our genetic heritage works), so the questions to ask are about “better, more designed cultures” and could our communications and media be better designed to be a part of this?

I think the way science works at its best is a big part of this, and that one way to approach new designs for culture is to think about what it would really mean to be embedded in “scientific thinking” (meaning something importantly distinct from mere “scientific knowledge”).

Similarly, what would it mean to be embedded in “artistic thinking” as opposed to mere “examples of art”?

And, what would it mean to really take children and childhood seriously and more comprehensively?

At some point, a few actually good ideas might be sensed over the hills we have to climb.

## [Do you believe it is ever a good idea to use a GOTO in a C C program why](https://www.quora.com/Do-you-believe-it-is-ever-a-good-idea-to-use-a-GOTO-in-a-C-C-program-why/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=391138740&comment_type=2)

*2024-02-10*

If there ever were a “double-edged sword” comment, this would be a good candidate.

One of the most interesting subtopics in the 60s was “extensible languages”. Most of the few attempts were not very good (as with most ideas), but there were several that worked as extensible higher level languages.

One of them was the first Smalltalk at Parc, and we were able to do many kinds of things with it. Two of the emerging negative features were:

Each extension eventually wound up making the code difficult to read and understand by other programmers, and resulted in essentially “too many languages”

More ominously, we found that even great programmers quite often introduced bad/poor extensions (driven in part by the heat and pace of debugging). Extension needed slow pondering and calm minds …

## [In software engineering when might pragmatism be harmful in the short term](https://www.quora.com/In-software-engineering-when-might-pragmatism-be-harmful-in-the-short-term/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=389487989&comment_type=2)

*2024-02-02*

Back in the early 60s when computers were logically tiny and slow, lots of optimization had to be done for almost everything (and led to very bad habits).

Edsger Dijkstra (Turing Award winner) said “Programming is logical brinksmanship”! He was one of the first to try to separate meaning from optimizations, and to prevent propagation of errors via modular programming.

A really interesting early use of one of Dijkstra’s systems and ideas was by Randall and Russell in a “dual Algol” system they did in the early 60s. Dijkstra had done an Algol system as an interpreter, and R & R created a version of this for fast programming and debugging, alongside an Algol processor that compiled very efficient code. This served the needs of the users extremely well — especially since they used the interpretive version as their *definition* of “the meaning of Algol” and used it to check the optimized version.

---

## [In software engineering when might pragmatism be harmful in the short term](https://www.quora.com/In-software-engineering-when-might-pragmatism-be-harmful-in-the-short-term/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=389484202&comment_type=2)

*2024-02-02*

I think it works against most fruitful processes in programming. “Pragmatics” in the old days was the concerns with “efficiencies of time and space”, and the temptations were to try to mix “ways” into the more pure “meanings” (or “semantics”) of the program.

In a simple imperative language, one is programming mostly in terms of “ways”, so it is easy to get carried away into trying to optimize with more clever “ways”. Building the discipline to separate out getting the meanings working before tackling the efficiencies can be difficult psychologically.

---

## [](https://www.quora.comhttps://aibuilders.quora.com/Isnt-artificial-intelligence-just-a-fancy-name-for-sophisticated-programming-running-in-fast-enough-computers-4?comment_id=387325879&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-22*

Just out of curiosity, I looked to see what the web->Oxford dictionary had to say about “artificial” and “artifact”.

I was very disappointed to find that their connotation was “made by a human” (whereas the actual meaning is “something made” — for example, a beaver lodge is an “artifact” and is “artificial”). The Greek root with the same meaning is “Techne”.

Here is an idea for readers to contemplate. “Science” is an organization of people, methods, and processes which, as a whole system, generally thinks better than most people, including individual scientists.

Using reasonable definitions of “artificial” and “intelligence”, *Science* is not just an actual “artificial intelligence”, but by far the best one that humans have come up with.

I’m familiar with both Rust and Swift.

You say: “*If the programmers are responsible for programming, it should be their duty to describe the computation accurately.”*

That is just what the great computing pioneers of the 40s thought. One of my heroes — Maurice Wilkes (the second Turing Award Laureate, who designed and made EDSAC, the first really programmable computer (pace Manchester) — was starting to walk upstairs in Cambridge, suddenly had a thought: ““**The realization came over me with full force that a good part of the remainder of my life was going to be spent in finding errors in my own programs.**”

They were all mathematicians, and thought programming would be like math, but it wasn’t much like math at all.

One of the main motivations behind the invention of “higher level languages” was to *automatically* ensure as much as possible by taking as much as possible out of having to “describe the (whole) computation accurately”. For example, dealing with indexing, storage allocation, race conditions, confusions of representation and meanings of subparts, modules, coordination between modules, etc.

All of the above were used in machine code programming but compounded errors, and were very hard to debug.

One of my favorite early systems — Ivan Sutherland’s Sketchpad — only dealt with “human requirements” and did all the rest of the programming/processing using internal problem solvers. The humans could make mistakes with the requirements, but most areas of human error were not accessible. (This is hard to do generally …)

I think one of the most persistent bad practices of the past that is still with us is the strong tendency to program both semantics (“meanings”) and pragmatics (“optimizations”) in the same body of code (this is done in pretty much every style of language). One could imagine a language in which every program has two columns: on the left side would be a debuggable “meaning”, and on the right side would be various optimizations of the meaning keyed by “when needed” conditions. The system should work with all the optimizations turned off (maybe these are debugged on a supercomputer) and then the optimizations can be written and checked against the meanings, etc.

## [What were the biggest mistakes made by Xerox](https://www.quora.com/What-were-the-biggest-mistakes-made-by-Xerox/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=387070399&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-21*

I know a few very prominent (i.e. Turing Award winners who have managed to keep their PhD theses “quiet”). The days back then had different perspectives on what a PhD should be (basically something that actually advanced the state of the art), and, thus, a thesis was often a “wonderful work in progress”, “something that three people were willing to sign” (as Ivan Sutherland quipped).

Ivan’s thesis was a remarkable exception in its high degree of completeness.

All these eventual Turing Award winners kept on working on their problem after getting their degrees and wound up with stellar results, including Klaus Wirth (with Euler, etc.). And they were all much more interested in trying to explain the eventually stellar results than the in haste theses in progress …

## [When was the first computer invented Who invented the Internet](https://www.quora.com/When-was-the-first-computer-invented-Who-invented-the-Internet/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=386912849&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-20*

Over the years, I’ve said that a type system should have “more meaning” to pay its way for the additional hassles required. For example, a useful type could be “this is the sine of a number” (rather than ‘this is just a floating point number”).

(Note that current type systems don’t do much.) ADTs don’t protect against race conditions, or have “time zones” — so using something like OOP to just do ADTs is probably a bad idea, and is not much more than trying to regain a bad style of programming (munging data structures — with or without types) from when one learned old style programming.

The notion of objects in Smalltalk was not to get “dynamic types” but to “model an idea” as a dynamic protected entity that can participate in a dynamic system. The fact of protection and the modeling provide the possibility of an “active semantics”, etc. The system design approach here is different than “data structures and procedures” — it was a “simulation approach” and we tried to avoid simulating weak and fragile ideas.

## [What were the biggest mistakes made by Xerox](https://www.quora.com/What-were-the-biggest-mistakes-made-by-Xerox/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=386846272&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-20*

I think of the “ARPA Dream” as being formulated by JCR Licklider. He had the “dream” and then when ARPA gave him funds and told him to make it happen, it became the “ARPA Dream”. Barton was not part of that, though this happened while he was at his most productive and inventive in the early 60s.

However, one of the early “ARPA Projects” was set up at Utah by Dave Evans in 1965, who admired Bob Barton, and was able to convince him to “take a sabbatical” from Burroughs and be a professor at the U of Utah. Barton thus became part of the ARPA community, and most certainly influenced and greatly enlarged its already freethinking nature (albeit mostly at the Utah project — but Utah had a large travel budget for grad students, so Barton’s ideas and attitudes started to spread around).

## [](https://www.quora.comhttps://aibuilders.quora.com/Isnt-artificial-intelligence-just-a-fancy-name-for-sophisticated-programming-running-in-fast-enough-computers-4?comment_id=386225320&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-17*

As a certified old fogey, I have been dismayed at the strong tendency in computing for new generations to use old labels, stripped of their initial meanings and visions, and paste them on inadequate subsets and distortions of the original ideas.

This happened early on for “Object-oriented programming”, and now for “Artificial Intelligence” (and even for “Machine Learning”). The old terms are not “gilding a lily” but attempts to “gild BS”.

Some of the reuse of terms is part of the human language process, and this even has happened in science (including the term “science” itself). Still, it is very painful to see most of the field working on limited subsets and claiming otherwise.

This is particularly critical to think about because — as Minsky pointed out years ago — our “minds” are really “societies of mindlets” which achieve enough synergies to be occasionally interesting and more powerful than the parts. This “society” and how it could work, is the proper business of what real research in “Artificial Intelligence” should be today.

---

## [](https://www.quora.comhttps://aibuilders.quora.com/Isnt-artificial-intelligence-just-a-fancy-name-for-sophisticated-programming-running-in-fast-enough-computers-4?comment_id=385791514&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-15*

As an old fogey (type my name into Google), I have retained the early sixties characterizations of “AI” (which were qualitatively different in tone and aim).

From your question here, I’m guessing that you don’t think my official answer above is sufficient to answer your question to my comment.

Perhaps another way to think about this is to contemplate what is added to a system when we organize it carefully. For example, a bit of organization of bricks will create a wall or a pyramid, but it was very hard for the ancients to come up with arches, and to make them (the actual history here is really interesting but off topic). In any case, when we — or *something* — *knows* — we need to include organizational knowledge, not just “brick-level” knowledge. And part of this is “knowing about knowing”, etc.

We now do know enough to not be impressed when an automobile wins a race with a human, but we have trouble seeing parallels to this (and not making category errors) in e.g. chess (we should now be past this), or “generative ‘AI’ “.

What has been interesting in various attempts to make “intelligent” processes over the years is the degree (or not) that inventors can take qualitative perspectives on the idea. From this POV there have been more interesting systems organizations for doing what “intelligence” in a human requires than MLs and/or LLMs.

There’s no question that parts of our brains do act a bit like the simplistic MLs of today — what’s missing are some of the more sophisticated organizational ideas smarter people in the past have come up with: e.g. take a look at Minsky’s book “Society of Mind” for the kinds of things that need to be thought about.

*2024-01-11*

The large context of the answer is that each of us has our own reality via how things process between our ears. This is not just between “smart” and “not-smart”, but exists in all humans, including “the smartest”. This means that, even at the highest levels of thinking, the thinkers and doers can have quite different goals and/or conceptions of the stated goals.

Many years ago NSF and the Annenburg School decided to illuminate these properties and the problems they can cause — in a film called “Private Universe”. It is very worth watching (and they then did followup films for those who were interested). “[A Private Universe](https://youtu.be/3un_VbXVefk”)

## [Which personal computer was the first to use an operating system not based on Unix](https://www.quora.com/Which-personal-computer-was-the-first-to-use-an-operating-system-not-based-on-Unix/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=384959772&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-11*

Two of the things we got interested in with Smalltalk was to be able to (a) get going quickly on any new machine/environment, and (b) run “bit-identically”. This led to including an official running-model of the kernel written in itself. This provides a readable understandable description of the semantics in which things can be tested, new kernel ideas tested, etc. But how to run something that is “metacircular”?

We already had been dealing with “bit-identical” for some years with number formats and various numeric operations. For example, many machines didn’t have floating point in those days, and if they did, the formats were often not standard. This means that critical math functions might not compute the same from machine to machine (and we wanted them to do so — think about Smalltalk objects on different machines sending messages to each other over the Ethernet or Internet …)

The solution was to have a method written in Smalltalk that could be automatically fallen back to if a primitive failed (this idea was used heavily in the original SAGE computers (Q7s)). So Smalltalk would run its own floating point if it couldn’t find a good enough version on the host machine.

Then there was the “interpreter simulator” whose job it was to run the model when you wanted “pure semantics”. This was adapted to help generate a new Smalltalk for a new machine. (It is worth reading about how this was done in the Squeak announcement paper - [https://tinlizzie.org/VPRIPapers/tr1997001_backto.pdf](https://tinlizzie.org/VPRIPapers/tr1997001_backto.pdf)).

Also note that in the various Smalltalks the two hierarchies — Classes and Objects — are circular at the top (e.g. there is a “Class” called “Class” …). We used to draw this as a circular link with a lightning bolt through the link (the lightning bold was the *microcode* in the kernel.

A lot of the early goodness here was due to Dan Ingalls, Ted Kaehler, and Dave Robson.

## [What profound insights did Seymour Cray have that allowed him to design faster computers than others could at the time Do they consist of proper cooling and signal sync which frankly seem to be methods that computer](https://www.quora.com/What-profound-insights-did-Seymour-Cray-have-that-allowed-him-to-design-faster-computers-than-others-could-at-the-time-Do-they-consist-of-proper-cooling-and-signal-sync-which-frankly-seem-to-be-methods-that-computer/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=384606583&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-09*

Back then, one of the great characters in computing — Dan Slotnik — wanted to do “many processor” architectures — the first was called “Solomon”. ARPA backed his project. This eventually became “Illiac IV” and wound up as a very difficult project to get all the way done.

Part of the problem was just resources — even just enough core memory to use various ways to avoid bottlenecks. Another was an interesting tradeoff (that slower CPUs were not that much cheaper than ultrafast ones, and pretty much all concerned went for the faster ones (and paid for this in many ways, not all of them good)).

However, looking ahead to MOS integrated circuits — as Gordon Moore did — could have helped. These were “too slow” in the 60s, but Moore liked them for many reasons: they were simple, their speed depended on their size, etc.

An interesting calculation that “must” have been done by “someone” (though I have never heard of it) is that one that asks: if my processors are slow — but rather cheap — how many of these plus local memory do I need to do one cycle of the Northern Hemisphere weather, using a finer grid than now, in a decently short enough time?

An interesting set of experiments at Parc by John Shoch (who was in my group at Parc) were the “worms” — this was possible because the several hundreds of Parc Altos could be organized using the Ethernet into a superdupercomputer (this was done at night, because most people no longer worked at night after the Alto). The results were very convincing.

## [What profound insights did Seymour Cray have that allowed him to design faster computers than others could at the time Do they consist of proper cooling and signal sync which frankly seem to be methods that computer](https://www.quora.com/What-profound-insights-did-Seymour-Cray-have-that-allowed-him-to-design-faster-computers-than-others-could-at-the-time-Do-they-consist-of-proper-cooling-and-signal-sync-which-frankly-seem-to-be-methods-that-computer/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=384424760&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-08*

We actually took quite a few pains in the 70s to try to explain the whole Alto microcode architecture to numerous chip companies, including (especially) Intel and Motorola.

The biggest problem is that none of those we talked to understood anything about SW, or why anyone might want to program in VHLLs, or even to make “personal computers” or do computer graphics, and especially bitmapped computer graphics (the Alto was a first for all these). Their goals were mainly “process goals” (getting the device geometries smaller and the transistors to run faster).

This actually set back personal computing even to this day — because the poorly designed microprocessors led to very low level and bad SW practices, which became defacto standards despite how poor they were (no one could tell the difference) — and this kind of “know nothingism” has happened a few more times.

A shame wasn’t it?

## [What was the best computer system you worked on or with and why For best and computer system pick your own definitions](https://www.quora.com/What-was-the-best-computer-system-you-worked-on-or-with-and-why-For-best-and-computer-system-pick-your-own-definitions/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=384157142&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-07*

The Wikipedia article could be a lot better — for example, it doesn’t mention the extensive use of “Medieval Mystery Plays” ideas in how and why the staging is so sparse and requires the imagination of the audience to work (of course, this is what theatre is all about).

[The Fantasticks - Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Fantasticks)

This show was originally a shorter one act, and was honed to brilliance. The first act is IMO one of the best, most “theatrical”, etc. The requirement for bringing it to Off Broadway was for Jones and Schmidt to write a two act show. The second act is by far the most difficult part of the show — it is after an intermission, “middles of plays and books” have “sagging problems”, you have to get the audience “back”, etc. I think they succeeded wonderfully well.

A key observation about “deep art” is by George Jean Nathan — a playwright and critic — who said: “People go to poor theatre trying to forget” — he meant “as with most TV” — “but they go to great theatre ‘tingling to remember’ “.

Deep art of all kinds is not about trying to distract, but instead to **get past our consciousness* *that is usually overwhelmed by our mundane needs in the world, and to trigger “big things we know and have forgotten to think about as much as we could and should”.

For this reason, Nathan called theatre “The Magic Mirror” (because you are trying to beam the intelligence of the audience back out at them).

How to do that without “preaching” is “the art of Art”.

In many cases, the artist is trying to communicate something they feel strongly about — and much of the time they are driven by the feelings and are not worrying about “art” at all …

This was partially inspired — as with so many of my “ideas” — by Ivan Sutherland’s Sketchpad system from the early 60s (it took an “engineering/scientific” view, and introduced “tolerances” and — most importantly — ways to continuously measure “fits” and “misfits”). The central key and principle behind any stochastic system is precisely the measure (and knowledge) and assessment and prognosis of the misfits.

## [Which personal computer was the first to use an operating system not based on Unix](https://www.quora.com/Which-personal-computer-was-the-first-to-use-an-operating-system-not-based-on-Unix/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=383951043&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-06*

I haven’t been following the adventures of Linus (I just recall that they started with him writing a microkernel).

Here’s one way to think about this. At ARPA and Parc (that was essentially another ARPA project, but funded by Xerox), there were three main inseparable ideas/goals/visions (a) interactive personal computing (b) pervasively networked together over the entire world. This was fueled by our reasons for thinking that (c) “Moore’s Law” was really going to happen in the way that it did.

This operated before we could do it, shaped how we eventually did it, and is the way we still think about it.

(c) also means that you can buy your way into the future i.e. what will be affordable in the future you can have right now by paying 20 or more times as much (you can make a time machine!). This is what we did, and this allowed thoughts and deeds generally unthinkable when we started.

Progress and experimentation need some strategies and tactics that allow both integrity and experimentation. One way to get both is to have protected, relatively small, objects and ways to configure and run them. This argues for a tiny kernel (as above) that is all about HW resources that can’t be changed willy nilly. Most of the rest *do* want to be changeable willy nilly *and* you also need stable configurations for vanilla day to day work. These are not incompatible.

Since the actual early intentions of “OOP” have been changed, let me just suggest thinking about the overall architecture as “requestable servers” (local and global) and that one’s own machine is much more of an extension to the pervasive networking idea than the other way round.

Locking in end-users to any monolithic OS is anti the spirit of personal computing — and software itself — (and certainly anti the spirit of the original inventors).

---

## [Which personal computer was the first to use an operating system not based on Unix](https://www.quora.com/Which-personal-computer-was-the-first-to-use-an-operating-system-not-based-on-Unix/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=383928429&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-06*

One of the ironies in your comment is that one of the perspectives of the original Unix group at Bell Labs was *really good* — namely that the kernel should be as small as possible and “only” concerned with allocation of “time” and “space” — everything else would be done by standard processes — and all processes (a) would be protected from each other (b) intercommunicate the same way (through neutral messages), this meant (c) you could structure communications using “pipes” (Doug McIllroy), (d) kernel processes would have a few extra privileges.

Sound a little like OOP? The main differences had to do with that processes carried some state as overhead, and so couldn’t model all computations, and that messages were in terms of strings (which made it a more involved process to communicate “symbols”, etc.).

Some years later Linus was disgusted by the “barnacles” of Unix and went back to the original tiny kernel idea (and wrote a really nice one that was only 1000 lines of C).

But what happened yet again? And why?

Quora doesn’t have the space for detailed analysis here. But you are free to speculate why some computerists tend to muck up initially good ideas …

The Parc approach that Smalltalk used happened before Unix and C, and was pure OOP. The main point for here is that “protected virtual machines” allow most things that used to be put in OSs to simply be objects (because they can protect themselves), and that what needs to be special is code to protect and allocate the parts of the HW that can’t protect themselves. This allowed two different perspectives (a) Smalltalk was its own OS, and (b) OOP doesn’t need the old style concept of an OS.

---

## [What are some good biographies that every aspiring programmer should read](https://www.quora.com/What-are-some-good-biographies-that-every-aspiring-programmer-should-read/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=383910688&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-06*

I haven’t started one, and am still actually living a life of *doing things I love* … so I have a feeling I will never write one.

When Quora asked for a one-word “tombstone” description, I replied “Curious”.

When conferences ask for a “short bio”, I usually reply: “No one owes more to his research community than I do”. This sums it up for me.

However, the ACM (via Jean Sammet) did suspend my regular activities by “strongly encouraging” me to write “The Early History Of Smalltalk” for their HOPL II conference and history series (circa early to mid-90s).

One of the requirements for the Kyoto Prize (2004) was that I had to write and give a “Commemorative Lecture” that had to include some autobiographical elements. [https://tinlizzie.org/VPRIPapers/m2004002_center.pdf](https://tinlizzie.org/VPRIPapers/m2004002_center.pdf)

I wrote a tribute to the ARPA/Parc community in 2004 when Butler Lampson, Chuck Thacker, Bob Taylor and I received the NAE Draper Prize for “Networked Personal Computing”. [https://tinlizzie.org/VPRIPapers/m2004001_power.pdf](https://tinlizzie.org/VPRIPapers/m2004001_power.pdf)

Recently — for a 50th year celebration of this and that — the University of Utah asked me to give a talk about what I had done in my 2.5 year long doctoral program — this is more of a history than a bio: “[08 We Were So Lucky Alan Kay](https://youtu.be/ktPCH_p80e4”)

*Biographies* are tricky — told by others they are generally quite inaccurate and often miss the point. Unfortunately, much the same obtains for *autobiographies*! Still the latter are the best if the author has found ways to take multiple perspectives on themselves.

---

## [What profound insights did Seymour Cray have that allowed him to design faster computers than others could at the time Do they consist of proper cooling and signal sync which frankly seem to be methods that computer](https://www.quora.com/What-profound-insights-did-Seymour-Cray-have-that-allowed-him-to-design-faster-computers-than-others-could-at-the-time-Do-they-consist-of-proper-cooling-and-signal-sync-which-frankly-seem-to-be-methods-that-computer/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=383893565&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-06*

One of the earliest machines to use this was the IBM 7030 Stretch. The “stunt box” on the 6600 reorganized the order of excecution of about 30 instructions at a time (many of the instructions on the RISC-like architecture were 15 bits long, packed 4 to a 60 bit word). Etc.

“Zero overhead” for the TX-2 and the Alto meant multiple program counters and “to the side HW” that could decide — while an instruction was being executed — which PC to use for the next instruction according to “need” bits that were set by various HW conditions. This “perfect interleaving” worked wonderfully well on both machines (and is also a much better way to run well without an interrupt system).

---

*2024-01-04*

The spring memories almost certainly stored by speed of sound through the spring — as did later spring memories used as tapped delay lines for audio reverb units in guitar amplifiers.

MOS transistors are basically “capacitive” (and thus quite easy to understand). The Parc Alto could run 16 “zero overhead” microcode tasks, and much of what used to be HW was simulated by this (including refreshing the MOS 1103 memory chips, the display, the disk, the keyboard, etc.) We got a lot of bank for the buck from this idea (which apparently first appeared on Wes Clark’s legendary TX-2 supercomputer used for SAGE — and also was the computer that Sketchpad was done on).

## [Where do you think programs begin to be a big ball of mud That is where do you think programs get large enough that a person cannot track all the interactions between the parts even if the parts are coherent](https://www.quora.com/Where-do-you-think-programs-begin-to-be-a-big-ball-of-mud-That-is-where-do-you-think-programs-get-large-enough-that-a-person-cannot-track-all-the-interactions-between-the-parts-even-if-the-parts-are-coherent/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=383042981&comment_type=2)

*2024-01-02*

Thank you!

Here’s one way to look at this. Biology is truly amazing in how it works and can find combinations that “work” via random changes, some of which fit in some ways more closely to the surrounding environments.

One product of all this is “us” with 10 trillion of our own cells and 90 trillion micro-organisms as partial parasites and partial helpers.

A key observation of “us” is that — while we may “think” better than most other biological organisms — we are actually terrible thinkers, and our best thinking is done via following (grudgingly invented) “methods” that our genetics doesn’t know of directly.

In other words, some of “us” were just smart enough to “invent actual thinking”, and history shows this is (a) very powerful, and (b) really difficult to learn and teach, and to get “us” to learn.

In other words, I think that “real thinking” — cognitive thinking — is incredibly important compared to more simple “blunder and test” tactics — *and* that terrible “black swan” events are really dangerous when the industrial revolution can magnify “random”, “poor”, and “bad” thinking by billions and trillions.

I rate the current “thinking” in software as “generally poor down to bad”, that large language models make things generally much worse — and that computing should drastically try to upgrade the general quality of its thinking to a level enough above threshold to make it safe and useful.

---

## [What does Alan Kay think about programming and teaching programming with copilots and LLMs of today](https://www.quora.com/What-does-Alan-Kay-think-about-programming-and-teaching-programming-with-copilots-and-LLMs-of-today/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=381554982&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-26*

One thing to note here is that the original and current visions that stemmed from the inspiration of Seymour were not in binary absolute, assembly, etc., nor in FORTRAN, Algol, or LISP, but instead in some kind of “higher level” that was already “doing a few more things for kids”. One question was always: “Is this too much help?” (is the instrument already doing too much of the playing for the learner?) This a well known problem in music with regard to the piano vs. singing and instruments that are more like singing (violin, flute, etc.).

I thought LOGO was an excellent design for a “child’s artifact” in most respects. It did require and facilitate “mathematical and process kinds of reasoning” and could also exist in the real world enough to have some of the mathematical processes be real scientific models.

Also, behind my zeal for Seymour’s (and Jerome Bruner’s) ideas, was the anthropological context that the children were partly in public school to gradually learn the skills and habits of “good citizens” in a representative democracy that they would be joining as voters in a few years.

At the base of a rather complex set of thoughts here, was Bacon’s urging that humans needed to invent much better methods (and use them) for getting around the terrible thinking that we tend to naturally. He called for a “new science” (which today we just call “science”) that was made up from these methods, and would be used not just to uncover nature, but to also mitgate and expand the possibilities for improved “human natures”.

I liked Montessori’s ideas of “special toys with epistemological whisperings”, and liked her minimalism and vision. This helped me understand what Seymour was trying to do.

As a musician I understood the delicate pitfalls vs benefits from exclusively learning keyboards, and could see that the same kind of lures and possible upsides existed for interactive computing as well.

After all these years, much has played out just with Ken’s number 1. With most of it, “the piano has taken over the music”, and I feel that 2. and 3. are destined to be much much worse. Montissori’s “whisperings” have been shouted down by the lure of “merely doing something” (as opposed to really “learning important ideas via helpful and minimally assisted doings”.

---

## [Where do you think programs begin to be a big ball of mud That is where do you think programs get large enough that a person cannot track all the interactions between the parts even if the parts are coherent](https://www.quora.com/Where-do-you-think-programs-begin-to-be-a-big-ball-of-mud-That-is-where-do-you-think-programs-get-large-enough-that-a-person-cannot-track-all-the-interactions-between-the-parts-even-if-the-parts-are-coherent/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=381536135&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-26*

No, I was referring to the first running Smalltalk-72 on the Alto (which used 64KB for the screen and 64KB for working memory. ST-72 ran in that, and had windows and graphics, etc. (The IEEE Computer issue that Adele and I wrote in March ’77 was entirely done with ST-72.)

Most of the reasons that ST-72 was more workable than TinyTalk had to do with the poor instruction set and bandwidth of the 8-bit micros, versus the 16-bit memory and vast speed difference of the Alto microcode — which allowed emulation of VMs to be efficient.

The DigiTalk people were working in MSDOS with a larger memory footprint and they decided not to late bind the “engine” and instead used a semi-code-compiler to get more effective speed from the kernel. Also, there was no bitmap to be dealt with. They made a lot of good engineering tradeoffs to get a workable system, even if it was less flexible than the original scheme.

---

## [What does Alan Kay think about programming and teaching programming with copilots and LLMs of today](https://www.quora.com/What-does-Alan-Kay-think-about-programming-and-teaching-programming-with-copilots-and-LLMs-of-today/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=381368147&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-25*

Hi Ken

I wish you had accepted (though Atari crashed as a company soon after).

My notions were based on my strong belief that “theater” is not only good for audiences, but “making theatre” is good for the makers. It’s a great experience for children.

Here’s something I’ve mentioned a number of times that came from the children we worked with at Parc (they were programming, using computer graphics, animation, and musical tools). When asked gently to think about “best uses”, they said that “You shouldn’t automate the center of your interests, but it could really help learning when some of the environment could be automated”.

For example, if you were learning to play music, having the computer play the piece is generally going to be a barrier — but if you are learning to arrange music, having the computer “be the orchestra” can really help.

Similarly, we did some of the first real-time virtual organ emulation, and the kids thought that was great, but having the computer be a player-piano for the pieces — instead of having a human learn to play — was contrary to good behavior and art.

This echoed much of the “small is beautiful” and “appropriate technology” ideas and ethos of the 60s, especially transmitted through the Whole Earth Catalog, etc. I think it also reflected much of the way Seymour thought as well: he was much more concerned with how the inner child developed than with automating human “centers”.

---

## [Where do you think programs begin to be a big ball of mud That is where do you think programs get large enough that a person cannot track all the interactions between the parts even if the parts are coherent](https://www.quora.com/Where-do-you-think-programs-begin-to-be-a-big-ball-of-mud-That-is-where-do-you-think-programs-get-large-enough-that-a-person-cannot-track-all-the-interactions-between-the-parts-even-if-the-parts-are-coherent/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=381361908&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-25*

Yes, this bothered us also in the 70s when Smalltalk was being crafted on a 64K *byte* computer. We thought that this should eventually be a new kind of “algebra” that could enforce the meanings of the interface intentions (in the “Early History of Smalltalk” I also mentioned that I regretted not using a semantic model more like the one Pat Winston used in his thesis, which in Smalltalk terms, also had classes for the messages and methods themselves — this also allowed for “slot inheritance” etc).

We did experiment with “slot inheritance” in Smalltalk-72, and quite liked the possibilities for “more guarded meanings”. Not being able to guard intentions was continually annoying. This is one of the reasons I didn’t like Simula-style inheritance: it was much too ad hoc.

However, this period of “tiny and slow” was successfully alleviated with “better manners”. This applied to the Alto also which used one shared register file for up to 16 interleaved microcode processes. “Good manners” were required for safety!

---

## [Where do you think programs begin to be a big ball of mud That is where do you think programs get large enough that a person cannot track all the interactions between the parts even if the parts are coherent](https://www.quora.com/Where-do-you-think-programs-begin-to-be-a-big-ball-of-mud-That-is-where-do-you-think-programs-get-large-enough-that-a-person-cannot-track-all-the-interactions-between-the-parts-even-if-the-parts-are-coherent/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=381347220&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-25*

Hi Ken

I too associate this with Perlis …

There are more slants to this interesting question (which I should add to the incomplete answer above), but will start with here).

One is a variant of the cognitive load problem: for me, I found that when I was doing a lot more designing and larger thinking, my ability to keep lots of code straight in my head diminished.

Another is that it would be really nice to have a “narrative view” of the definition of an OOP system, and the active system itself.

For example, I found that Larry Tesler’s famous “code browser” for Smalltalk rendered it almost un-perusable for me — it really catered to people who worked with it every day and “knew more or less where things were” — and this obtained even after quite good search and “explorer” facilities were added.

## [What does Alan Kay think about programming and teaching programming with copilots and LLMs of today](https://www.quora.com/What-does-Alan-Kay-think-about-programming-and-teaching-programming-with-copilots-and-LLMs-of-today/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=381184520&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-24*

Hi Ken

Analogies can be useful until they get overdrawn — but here goes. The distinction I would like to make is pretty similar to that between those who play and compose music, and those who “conduct music” (especially those who “air conduct”).

My image of “thinking” is (a) as a learned skill and art form that can be sweetened by initial talent and grown by lots of hours of experience and will power, and (b) rather more like composing and playing, than conducting.

A big deal about conducting in music is that most conductors are also very good players, and many have been composers. However, there is also “air conducting” (and one could imagine a motion sensor game that would translate some form of arm motions to how synthesized music would be rendered in real-time — this would be another form of “Guitar Hero”).

I think that a “conductor” of an LLM will learn *something* via the process of guiding the LLM to fashion desired “good enough” results. But — in my main definitions of thinking — I don’t think there is enough of the best kinds of experiences and “exercises” to actually help grow the best kinds of thinking.

And in any case, the “air conducting” of an LLM is likely to be both debilitating and dangerous. The equivalents of what conductors in music have to go through first also have to happen in this arena.

## [How might Alan Kay elaborate on You could think of it Val Shorres Meta II as an active interface language on objects](https://www.quora.com/How-might-Alan-Kay-elaborate-on-You-could-think-of-it-Val-Shorres-Meta-II-as-an-active-interface-language-on-objects/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=380949225&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-23*

This is an excellent comment (and with many parts).

To echo my reply to Claude Wilbur: What I meant was that messages should not be *commands* that have to be obeyed — messages should only be *requests* (that can be refused by the receivers), so *every entity is a “protected server”*

George understands this by indicating that he wants to make things “less command based”, I’m simply advocating *no commands*, which still allows *requests* that look like *imperatives*.

He doesn’t realize that we agree when he says about the Internet (one of my favorite examples): “which is still an organized effort, a distributed effort but we replace its parts according to predetermined rules, called the protocols and services, which we should know about and how they work in advance.”

I am simply advocating both much larger and also more flexible and extensible designs for “protocols and services”.

We don’t agree on “the meaning of meaning”. I don’t think that consciousness is required to have *meaning*ful exchanges/communications between entities. What is needed is much more along the lines of processes with *negotiations and agreements*.

Long ago JCR Licklider set up the ARPA computer research community, not just to invent personal computing, but to hook everything in the world together with an “intergalactic network”. He pointed out that if we did these — he funded these, and we did them — our main problem would then be “communicating with aliens”. He meant — at that scale — not just human to human, but human to machines, and machines to machines. He also implied that ideas for communicating with “real aliens from outer space” could possibly be used (and actually funded some work that John McCarthy did at the Stanford AI Lab which looked into this: especially the LYCOS schemes of Freudenthal).

It’s worth thinking *really in the large here* (the actual situation today is now more than large enough to require this), about how to deal with “meaningful communications and usages” when initial agreement doesn’t exist, but where possibilities for creating negotiations do. (For example, if TCP/IP were extended just a little could this be not just possible, but could be made practical?)

---

## [How might Alan Kay elaborate on You could think of it Val Shorres Meta II as an active interface language on objects](https://www.quora.com/How-might-Alan-Kay-elaborate-on-You-could-think-of-it-Val-Shorres-Meta-II-as-an-active-interface-language-on-objects/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=380947021&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-23*

“George” commented here and I got the email from Quora, but his excellent comment hasn’t shown up, so I’ll reproduce it here and reply below:

**George commented on your answer to: "How might Alan Kay elaborate on "You could think of it [Val Shorre's Meta II] as an active interface language on objects"?"**

It's an interesting argument, but all the declarative code that I'm aware of is just some sort of encapsulation of imperative constructs with higher-level constructs that, in some cases, get in the way of unwanted details.

Still, the imperative code is in action under the hood, even in programming languages that have a sort of declarative syntax. But it's still just a nice touch to make programming less verbose and command-based (I make heavy use of such things).

In my experience, the world of programming cannot be in terms of meaning. Meaning applies to things with some kind of consciousness and is also relative. Perhaps with better semantics and less verbosity, with multiple levels of automatic code generation and optimization, but still, computers are state machines with some kind of state-manipulation commands (SW / HW).

Organizing things into namespaces and domains with live messages (methods or whatever) is a very powerful idea, but in many cases, this reduces the predictability of the system, and I don't see (till now) any effective way to scale systems without coordinated restructuring, not even the internet, which is still an organized effort, a distributed effort but we replace its parts according to predetermined rules, called the protocols and services, which we should know about and how they work in advance.

I have always found that hybrid systems are the most effective, e.g., combining imperative and declarative code, and dynamic dispatch (Messaging) out of a certain namespace and static dispatch internally for certain subdomains, also for GUI, at least the current ones, where certain things should be more graphical and clear upfront, combined with some sort of textual manipulation for more complex behaviors (all credit to you for making me aware of this early!).

The lib-first approach and macros of some modern languages combined with some dynamic features (where needed) are not that bad—well, nothing radical, but I found life to be a lot easier now. I will appreciate more concrete examples of what you suggest.

## [As a beginner what are the best ways to approach Computer Science](https://www.quora.com/As-a-beginner-what-are-the-best-ways-to-approach-Computer-Science/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=377525368&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-06*

I asked a number of questions in the answer I gave above — and I think it is a very good thing that you are (mostly) asking questions in your reply above). In the original questions in the 60 about “what could a science of computing be?”, most who were concerned assumed that “systems” would be a main part of any worthwhile formulation.

I think this is less emphasized today because of the way most colleges and most students are catering to what they consider the “givens” of making a living doing computing in the commercial world (which includes horrendous legacies and already existing poor systems which are felt to be to complex to fix or replace).

Because of my age and the research climate that existed when I started in the 60s, I only had to deal with other people’s “givens” in my first few years. The research community in those days was large and sophisticated enough to not have to deal with commercial computing at all, and was free to invent new kinds of computing, networks, systems, etc.

This makes it very difficult for me to come up with meaningful comments about today’s computing environments. Instead, I try to get practitioners today to learn how to criticize what they have taken for granted, and as “normal”.

## [What is Alan Kays alternative solution for the web which he mentioned in OOPSLA 97](https://www.quora.com/What-is-Alan-Kays-alternative-solution-for-the-web-which-he-mentioned-in-OOPSLA-97/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=376798224&comment_type=2)

*2023-12-03*

AppleScript started out as a really good idea. The bottleneck turned out to be the reluctance/laziness of software companies to produce reasonable APIs — and especially in a the polymorphic form that you help end-users find properties and traits (this was a key idea in the Mac GUI, and the aim was to carry it through to what you saw when you popped the hood.

One of the main lessons from Hypercard was that it really was possible to make an end-user programming system in which real things could be done: via a combination of careful designs of traits and user interface. The Applescript project was not able to put out the same level of effort, and this resulted in a more complicated environment in which an end-user had to devise scripts.

## [What is Alan Kays alternative solution for the web which he mentioned in OOPSLA 97](https://www.quora.com/What-is-Alan-Kays-alternative-solution-for-the-web-which-he-mentioned-in-OOPSLA-97/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=376661170&comment_type=2)

In the mid-oughts I was asked to do a number of talks by universities, and would ask the audience of students several questions. One was “What did Doug Engelbart do?”. The best answers I got resembled “Didn’t he have something to do with the mouse?”.

My question back to the audience was “How many of you have typed ‘E n g e l b a r t’ into Google?” Most hadn’t.

I said, “Look, a bunch of us back in the 60s and 70s put a lot of effort into inventing personal computing and local/global networking so you could find out things by just typing a question (among many other things)” You have these devices with you. Why haven’t you had any curiosity about the giants who were the founders of our field?”.

I just typed ‘E n g e l b a r t’ into Google. The first hit was the Wikipedia article on him. The 4th hit were the videos of the great 1968 demos. The 5th hit was the “Engelbart Institute” where there is much golden information about Doug, including the 75 or so papers he wrote — try reading his original proposal to the Air Force (ca 1962) — look for the link to the original document (it has more impact).

You have indicated a bit of curiosity (that’s good), now please do what I suggest above to learn more.

## [What was the historical significance of FORTRAN](https://www.quora.com/What-was-the-historical-significance-of-FORTRAN/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=374942859&comment_type=2)

*2023-11-24*

This is an interesting comment… both for implicitly asking when all these actually started, and when their meanings changed.

The ideas of automata do go back before there were computers to run programs, but there are examples such as Euclid’s GCD algorithm and Ada’s Bernouli Numbers program that existed before working computers. Turing and Von Neumann both made use of finite automata ideas (and the latter, recursion) before there were computers to program.

The notion of grammar also is ancient, going back to at least to Dionisius Thrax in B.C. Greece.

Even so, the history I’m familiar of parsing and translation methods in computing indicates that most of the actual early work in the late 40s and early 50s was pretty ad hoc. The parsers for “George” (the Laning-Zwerler system) and FORTRAN were pretty crude and predated awareness e.g. of what automate studies people were doing about the same time. IIRC Chomsky’s papers started appearing around the mid-50s and his book on Syntax soon after.

---

## [What was the historical significance of FORTRAN](https://www.quora.com/What-was-the-historical-significance-of-FORTRAN/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=374856661&comment_type=2)

*2023-11-23*

It’s interesting that “parsing and compiling” were at the center of “deep computer science” in the late 50s through the 60s.

IIRC, FORTRAN was originally done using a method by Rutishauser, which first involved taking an arithmetic expression and fully parenthesizing it, and then parsing that. The early Whirlwind “George” system dealt with nestings, but only up to 6 deep (it was not recursive, but more like a regular expression decoder).

Samelson and Bauer in Germany in the late 50s were early pioneers of using stacks and precedence comparisons to parse recursive formulas. These techniques were used by Barton in the Balgol compiler (a dialect of Algol 58) for the Burroughs 220.

---

## [What programming language does Alan Kay consider to be the most advanced](https://www.quora.com/What-programming-language-does-Alan-Kay-consider-to-be-the-most-advanced/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=373505019&comment_type=2)

*2023-11-17*

Thanks for your thoughtful detailed comment. As far as it goes, I tend to agree with your points.

However, the point I am trying to make is that the actual situation has gotten *qualitatively more difficult and even dangerous* since the 60s/70s — and, to me, this calls for a qualitative change-out in most of the current approaches and tools.

The impulse to move from binary to assembler, and then from assembler to “higher level languages” (like FORTRAN, ALGOL, etc) came from a number of pressures, including desiring qualitative improvements in ease of programming, and programs that could be put through different compilers for different hardwares. Similar arguments to yours for e.g. C/C++ were made for holding onto assembly languages, etc.

*What I’m trying to say here is that the combination of Moore’s Law, personal computing, and the Internet, have given rise to a situation where deep knowledge and tools for languages like C/C++, etc. actually hurt rather than help.*

An analogy is to structural construction — using wood, bricks, and stone — which also gave rise to deep skills and tools. The idea of using iron based metals as large scale building materials took a long time to be generated and accepted, but most of the industrial revolution required the change to making structures and complex objects using metal rather than wood, bricks and stone.

![](https://qph.cf2.quoracdn.net/main-qimg-153d1d80d71b5e3697531bc23a0e293f-lq)

**Akashi Straits Bridge — The pylons are the height of the Empire State Building — not buildable with wood, bricks, etc.**

I’m saying/claiming that programming needs another qualitative jump beyond most thoughts today.

## [What programming language does Alan Kay consider to be the most advanced](https://www.quora.com/What-programming-language-does-Alan-Kay-consider-to-be-the-most-advanced/answer/Alan-Kay-11?comment_id=373275109&comment_type=2)

*2023-11-16*

I know you realize (I hope) that with regard to “Whole Earth Needs”, Smalltalk is almost completely obsolete. Anything we can make in quantity (including computing processes) needs to be made inside a system that will not just simulate effects locally but with regard to the larger systems the computing processes are embedded in. LLMs not just don’t come close, but many of the ways they make errors are part of the larger problem.
